A well-quasi-order for continuous functions
Raphaël Carroy and Yann Pequignot

Abstract
We prove that continuous reducibility is a well-quasi-order on the class of continuous functions between separable metrizable spaces with analytic zero-dimensional domain. To achieve this, we define scattered functions, which generalize scattered spaces, and describe exhaustively scattered functions between zero-dimensional separable metrizable spaces up to continuous equivalence.



1
Introduction

Definition 1.1
.

Given topological spaces
X,X^{\prime},Y,Y^{\prime}
,
we say that a function
f:X\rightarrow Y

continuously reduces
to a function
g:X^{\prime}\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
if there are two continuous functions
\sigma:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}

and
\tau:\im(g\circ\sigma)\rightarrow\im(f)
such that
f=\tau\circ g\circ\sigma
. We also say that the pair
(\sigma,\tau)

continuously reduces
, or simply
reduces
,
f
to
g
.

\sigma
\tau
f
g
\leq

Continuous reducibility is a transitive and reflexive relation, which makes it a
quasi-order
.
As usual with quasi-orders, there is an induced equivalence relation: we write
f\equiv g
when both
f\leq g
and
g\leq f
hold,
and we say that
f
and
g
are
continuously equivalent
.
We also denote by
f<g
the relation
f\leq g
and
f\not\geq g
, and we say that
f
and
g
are
incomparable
when both
f\not\leq g
and
g\not\leq f
.

An
antichain
is a set of pairwise incomparable elements, and an
infinite strictly descending chain
is a sequence
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

satisfying
f_{n+1}<f_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. A quasi-order is called a
well-quasi-order
, or
wqo
, if it has no infinite antichains and no infinite descending chains.

A topological space is
Polish
if it is separable and completely metrizable, and
zero-dimensional
if it has a basis consisting of
clopen sets
,
that is sets that are both open and closed. A topological space is
analytic
if it is a continuous image of a Polish space.

Our main result is the following theorem.

Main Theorem 1
.

Continuous reducibility is a well-quasi-order on the class of continuous functions from an analytic zero-dimensional space to a separable metrizable space.

The analyticity hypothesis in
Main Theorem

1
is only used to show that all such continuous functions with uncountable image are equivalent to the identity function on the Cantor space. In fact, we prove

Main Theorem 2
.

Continuous reducibility is a well-quasi-order on the class of continuous functions from a separable metrizable zero-dimensional space to a countable metrizable space.

The above theorem is proved following a dichotomy which is reminiscent of the case of spaces.
Recall that a space is
scattered
if any of its non-empty subsets contains an isolated point. We suggest to make the following definition:
a function
f
between topological spaces is
scattered
if any non-empty subset of its domain contains a non-empty open set on which
f
is constant.
Every continuous function with a scattered image is scattered, but scattered continuous functions may have a non scattered image (for example, any bijection
:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{Q}
) or domain (for example, a constant function on
\mathbb{Q}
).

While the space
\mathbb{Q}
of rationals is universal for countable metrizable spaces, any non scattered metrizable space contains a copy of
\mathbb{Q}
.
We show analogous results for functions with
\id_{\mathbb{Q}}
, the identity on
\mathbb{Q}
, in the role of
\mathbb{Q}
, thereby establishing that, up to continuous equivalence,

\id_{\mathbb{Q}}
is the only non scattered continuous function from a metric space to a countable metric space.

With this in mind, the main challenge in proving our
Main Theorem

2
concerns the scattered functions.
The result at the heart of our main theorem can then be stated as follows.

Main Theorem 3
.

Continuous reducibility is a well-quasi-order on the class of scattered continuous functions from a zero-dimensional separable metrizable space to a metrizable space.

We thus in particular answer positively
[
Car13b
, Question 5.5]
, as then conjectured.

1.1
The context

Our
Main Theorem

1
ties together two concepts, continuous reduction and well-quasi-orders.
The notion of reduction originally comes from computability theory; given sets
X
and
Y
we say that a relation
R\subseteq X^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}

reduces
to

S\subseteq Y^{n}
if there is a map
\sigma:X\to Y
satisfying
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in R
if and only if
(\sigma(x_{1}),\ldots\sigma(x_{n}))\in S
.
Continuous and Borel reducibilities between definable (equivalence) relations on Polish and analytic spaces have been explored in a rich series of works,
see
[
Kec99
,
MRar
,
For18
]
for surveys on that matter, and the references contained therein.

Well-quasi-orders (
wqo
s) are a natural generalization of well-orders in the context of partial quasi-orders. They too have a rich history and body of works,
see
[
Kru72
]
for an early account of
wqo
theory, and
[
SSW20
]
for a recent book on
wqo
theory.
Proving that a rich class of structures is a
wqo
often requires an extensive analysis, as illustrated for instance by Robertson and Seymour’s twenty papers on
the graph minor, culminating in their
wqo
theorem in
[
RS04
]
.
Our article presents similarities with another celebrated
wqo
result: Laver’s theorem
[
Lav71
]
proving Fraïssé’s conjecture that embeddability is
wqo
on countable linear orders.
Much like Laver’s work on linear orders, our result on continuous functions mostly consists of unfolding the properties of scattered objects.
Our result thus comes back to the origins of the concept of
‘scatteredness’
, first studied by Cantor for spaces.
See
[
PS59
,
Sem59
,
KR74
]
for more on scattered spaces.

Wqo
results for continuous reductions are fairly rare, the first one is the Wadge-Martin-Monk result that continuous reducibility is
wqo
on Borel subsets
of the Baire space
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
equipped with the product of the discrete topology (see
[
Wad72
,
KLS11
]
for the early works, and
[
CMM22
,
CMRS23
]
for recent accounts).
Continuous reduction on subsets, also known as Wadge theory, can be seen as continuous reduction between characteristic functions.
In this sense our
Main Theorem

1
is in the lineage of all these results.

Wadge theory has also sparked a lot of research, among which
[
vMS87
]
in the late 1980’s tackles for the first time more than characteristic functions.
A consequence of that work – not phrased this way at the time – is that continuous reducibility is
wqo
on Borel functions with finite image.
In
[
vMS87
]
is also considered the stronger notion of
topological embeddability
,
which is the quasi-order on functions obtained by requiring the reducing pair
(\sigma,\tau)
in
Definition

1.1

to be
topological embeddings
, that is injective continuous functions whose inverse is also continuous.
They prove that
\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{2}
-measurable functions with finite image are
wqo
under topological embeddability, and recently Hertling gave a description of this class of functions
[
Her20
]
.

A number of finite basis results can be proven for topological embeddability between functions, see
[
Sol98
,
PS12
,
Deb14
,
CM20
]
.
However, in
[
CPV19
]
with Zoltán Vidnyánszky we manage to prove that topological embeddability between continuous functions defined on the Baire space,
as a quasi-order, reduces all analytic quasi-orders, and therefore is as far away from a
wqo
as it can possibly be.

Starting in the 1990’s and the work of Weihrauch, the computable version of continuous reducibility between functions has become subject of interest in computable analysis, see
[
HW94
]
.
In computable analysis, continuous reducibility between functions is known as
topological strong Weihrauch reducibility
. We still choose the name continuous reducibility, not only
to underline the filiation with descriptive set-theoretical studies of definable reducibilities, but also because the objects studied in computable analysis are not functions but multi-functions, which changes radically the nature of the question.
Interestingly, Dzhafarov recently proved in
[
Dzh19
]
that (computable) strong Weihrauch reducibility on multi-functions has a lattice structure that contains a copy of all
countable lattices, making it also very far from being a
wqo
. See
[
BGP21
]
for a recent survey on Weihrauch redubility in computable analysis.

1.2
The strategy

The proof of
Main Theorems

1
,
2
and
3
takes most of the paper. We now briefly summarize our strategy, explaining the organization of the paper along the way.
First off, we adopt a strategy similar to Laver’s in
[
Lav71
]
and prove that continuous reducibility actually enjoys the stronger property introduced by Nash-Williams
[
NW65
]
: it is a better-quasi-order.
Here we briefly give a definition and use some results that are to be recalled later, but we will rely entirely on the existing literature on better-quasi-orders,
see for instance
[
Sim85
,
Mar94
,
Peq17
,
CP20
]
.

We denote by
[\mathbb{N}]^{\mathbb{N}}
the
Ellentuck space
of all infinite subsets of natural numbers.
We identify each element of the Ellentuck space with its increasing enumeration, making
[\mathbb{N}]^{\mathbb{N}}
(homeomorphic to) a closed subset of
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
,
thus endowing it with a Polish topology. Given
Z\in[\mathbb{N}]^{\mathbb{N}}
we denote by
{}_{*}Z
the
shift
of
Z
, that is
Z\setminus\{\min{Z}\}
.
Given a quasi-order
(Q,\leq_{Q})
, a
Q
-multisequence
is a locally constant map
\varphi:[\mathbb{N}]^{\mathbb{N}}\to Q
, we say that it is
bad
if
for all
Z\in[\mathbb{N}]^{\mathbb{N}}
we have
\varphi(Z)\not\leq_{Q}\varphi({}_{*}Z)
. We say that
Q
is a
better-quasi-order
, or
bqo
, if there are no bad
Q
-multisequences.
As the name suggests, every
bqo
is in particular a
wqo
. We aim for the following theorem, which is thus a strengthening of
Main Theorems

1
and
2
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Continuous reducibility is a better-quasi-order on the class of continuous functions
f:X\to Y
from a zero-dimensional separable metrizable space
X
to a metrizable space
Y
such that either
X
is analytic or
Y
is countable.

We start by characterizing scattered functions in a way that parallels the characterization of scattered spaces using isolated points (see
[
MS20
,
Fré27
,
Sem59
]
).

The set of points at which a function
f
is locally constant is an open set of its domain, so the restriction of
f
to the set of points on which
f
is not locally constant defines a derivative
that we call the
Cantor–Bendixson derivative
of
f
(see
Subsection

2.2
and
[
Kec95
, Section 34.D]
for more on derivations).
The fixed point for this derivative is called the
perfect kernel
of
f
, and the minimal ordinal index of the fixed point is the
Cantor–Bendixson rank
.

The restriction of
f
to its perfect kernel is nowhere locally constant, this is why we also call the perfect kernel the
nowhere locally constant part
of
f
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Suppose that
X
is metrizable,
Y
Hausdorff, and
f:X\to Y
continuous.
Then
f
is scattered if and only if it has empty perfect kernel if and only if
\id_{\mathbb{Q}}
does not embed topologically in
f
.

We will see in
Proposition

2.11
that, much like for Fréchet’s result on scattered spaces
[
Fré27
]
,
the extra hypotheses on
X
,
Y
and
f
in
Theorem

1.3
are only needed to prove that the third condition implies the first two.

We proceed with proving that there are, up to continuous equivalence, only two non-scattered continuous functions at stake in
Theorem

1.2
:

\id_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
\id_{\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}}
(
Theorem

2.13
).
This allows us to focus next on scattered functions. More precisely,
Theorem

1.2
follows from the following strengthening of
Main Theorem

3
(see
Theorem

2.13
).

Theorem 1.4
.

Continuous reducibility is a
bqo
on the class of scattered continuous functions from a zero-dimensional separable metrizable space to a metrizable space.

We will see that the class of functions from
Theorem

1.4
can be represented, up to homeomorphism, as the class
\mathsf{Scat}
of scattered continuous functions
f:A\to B
where
A,B
are subsets of the Baire space
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
.
For
\alpha\in\omega_{1}
, we denote by
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha}
the functions in
\mathsf{Scat}
that have Cantor–Bendixson rank exactly
\alpha
.
The proof of
Theorem

1.4
splits in two parts:
the first part consists of proving that if
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha}
is
bqo
for all
\alpha<\omega_{1}
, then
Theorem

1.4
holds.
This is done in
Section

3
through a generalization to
\mathsf{Scat}
of the main theorem from
[
Car13b
]
: the General Structure
Theorem

3.17
.

The second part is the central novelty of this paper, it consists of proving by induction that continuous reducibility is
bqo
on
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha<\omega_{1}
;
it will be the goal of
Sections

4
,
5
and
6
.

In
[
Car13b
]
the main results were proven for the class of continuous functions between Polish zero-dimensional spaces with countable image.
We dedicate
Subsections

2.4
and
3
to generalize and strengthen these results to more general classes of spaces and functions
1
1

1

In an effort to provide a self-contained account of the result, all proofs are included even when they are essentially the same as in
[
Car13b
]
. Similarities are duly signaled.
.
For now let us just give a brief outline.

Our strategy for proving that continuous reducibility is
bqo
on
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha}
is to define operations that allow to describe
every continuous equivalence class of functions in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha}
from functions with smaller rank.
To do so, we consider an operation on continuous function that we call
gluing
. It is the natural operation of sum on functions;
it roughly consists of the disjoint union on both domains and co-domains of a sequence of functions.
We use the term gluing to distinguish this operation from the disjoint union performed on domains only, that will also be used in this paper.

We say that a set
\mathcal{F}
of functions is
finitely generated
if there exists a finite set
\mathsf{G}
of functions such that each element of
\mathcal{F}

is continuously equivalent to a finite gluing of elements of
\mathsf{G}
.
It is easy to see that continuous reducibility is always
bqo
on a finitely generated set (cf.
Proposition

2.33
).
The main result of the present article can therefore be formulated as follows.

Theorem 1.5
.

For all
\alpha\in\omega_{1}
, the set
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha}
is finitely generated.

To prove
Theorem

1.5
we will explicitly produce a finite generating set of functions for all
\alpha<\omega_{1}
(
Subsection

5.3
);
this set is made of required functions obtained inductively from generators of smaller ranks using three operations:
the aforementioned gluing (
Subsection

2.4
), the Pointed Gluing (
Section

3
),
and the Wedge operation (
Subsection

5.1
).

To prove
Theorem

1.5
, we proceed in two main steps.
For the first one, we prove new upper and lower bound results for the Pointed Gluing operation (
Propositions

3.7
and
3.13
);
these allow us to identify minimum functions in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\leq\alpha}
and maximum functions in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\leq\alpha}
(thus generalizing yet another result of
[
Car13b
]
) in
Propositions

3.12
and
3.15
.
We use this machinery to prove the (aforementioned) General Structure
Theorem

3.17
. That result takes care of
\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda}
for all limit
\lambda
simultaneously.

The second step is the main new feature, we prove finite generation of
\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda+n+1}
for all limit
\lambda
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, uniformly on
\lambda
, by induction on
n
.
A first new ingredient is the identification of specific functions that we call
centered
(
Section

4
), we use the induction hypothesis to prove that:
in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda+n+1}
all functions are locally centered (
Theorem

4.8
) and there are only finitely many centered functions (
Theorem

4.10
).
Using this first new ingredient and the Wedge operation (which is the second new ingredient) we treat separately the base case
\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda+1}

(
Theorems

4.13
and
5.11
) and the general one (
Theorem

6.16
).

We conclude the article by discussing the optimality of our strategy, proving that the generators that we identify cannot be omitted (
Theorem

7.5
), as well as
future directions for research following two main angles: continuous reducibility on more generally definable functions on (general) Polish spaces (
Subsection

7.2.1
),
and functions with uncountable image on more general spaces (
Subsection

7.2.2
). We give all along
Section

7
a healthy list of questions and conjectures.

2
Some known and less known preliminaries

Given a function
f:X\to Y
, we use the following classical notations. We denote by
\dom f=X
the domain of
f
and refer to
Y
as the codomain of
f
.
We write
\im f=\{f(x)\mid x\in X\}
for the image, or the range, of
f
.
For
A\subseteq X
,
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A}:A\to Y
stands for the restriction of
f
to
A
, and
\id_{X}:X\to X
denotes the identity function on
X
.
For
B\subseteq Y
,
f^{-1}(B)=\{x\in X\mid f(x)\in B\}
denotes the preimage of
B
by
f
, and we call
co-restriction
of
f
to
B
the function
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{f^{-1}(B)}
, denoted by
f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{B}
.
To improve readability, we often use the multiplicative notation
fg
for the composition
f\circ g
of
g
with
f
.

It follows from the definition of continuous reducibility that any function
f:X\to Y
is continuously equivalent to the surjective function
f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{\im f}:X\to\im f
,
where
\im f
is endowed with the subspace topology
2
2

2

However, restricting our attention to surjective functions would not simplify proofs nor notations.
Indeed some functions like the centered functions from
Section

4
are equivalent to their corestriction to arbitrary small subset of their image.
.

About the existence of a reduction
(\sigma,\tau)
between functions
f
and
g
, it is useful to notice that to any given (potential)
\sigma
can only correspond to a single
\tau
.
More precisely,
f\leq g
if and only if there exists a continuous
\sigma:X\to X^{\prime}
such that the relation

T=\{g\sigma(x),f(x))\mid x\in X\}

is the graph of a continuous function
\tau:\im(g\sigma)\to\im(f)
.

While
\sigma
fully determines
\tau
, a given candidate
\tau
can potentially correspond to various
\sigma
.
Indeed,
f\leq g
if and only if there exists a subset
B
of
Y^{\prime}
and a continuous surjection
\tau:B\to\im(f)
such that the relation

\{(x,y)\in X\times X^{\prime}\mid g(y)\in B\text{ and }\tau g(y)=f(x)\}

admits a continuous uniformization (for more on uniformizations, see
[
Kec95
, Section 18]
).

We next show that continuous reducibility between functions respects measurability and generalizes both (coarse) Wadge reducibility and embeddability.

Recall that for two topological spaces
X
and
Y
,
A\subseteq X

Wadge reduces to

B\subseteq Y
if there is a continuous function
\sigma:X\to Y

such that
\sigma^{-1}(B)=A
, denoted by
A\leq^{X,Y}_{W}B
. Given
A\subseteq X
the notation
{\mathbf{1}}_{A,X}:X\to\{0,1\}
stands for the characteristic function of
A
in
X
.

A continuous function
\sigma:X\to Y
is a topological embedding (or simply an embedding) if and only if there is a continuous function
\tau:\im(\sigma)\to X

such that
\tau\sigma=\id_{X}
, we also say that
(\sigma,\tau)

witnesses
the embedding from
X
to
Y
.

We sometimes write
‘reduces’
and
‘embeds’
instead of
‘continuously reduces’
and
‘topologically embeds’
.

Finally, a
pointclass
is a functional class
\mathbf{\Gamma}
mapping any topological space
X
to a subset of the power set of
X
with the property
that given any continuous function
\sigma:X\to Y
if
A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Y)
then
\sigma^{-1}(A)\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(X)
.
Observe that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(X)
is closed under preimages by continuous functions from
X
to
X
, for all spaces
X
.
A function
f:X\to Y
is said
\mathbf{\Gamma}
-measurable
if the preimage of an open set of
Y
by
f
is in
\mathbf{\Gamma}(X)
.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
X,X^{\prime},Y
and
Y^{\prime}
be topological spaces.

(i)

X
embeds in
Y
if and only if
\id_{X}\leq\id_{Y}
.

(ii)

If
A\subseteq X
and
B\subseteq Y
then
{\mathbf{1}}_{A,X}\leq{\mathbf{1}}_{B,Y}
if and only if
A\leq^{X,Y}_{W}B
or
A\leq^{X,Y}_{W}Y\setminus B
.

(iii)

Given a pointclass
\mathbf{\Gamma}
, if
f:X\to Y
reduces to
g:X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}
and
g
is
\mathbf{\Gamma}
-measurable, then so is
f
.

Proof.

A space
X
embeds in
Y
if and only if a pair of maps
(\sigma,\tau)
witnesses the embedding from
X
to
Y
if and only if
(\sigma,\tau)
reduces
\id_{X}
to
\id_{Y}
.

There are only two (continuous) functions
\{0,1\}\to\{0,1\}
, the identity and the function swapping
0
and
1
.
So, given
A\subseteq X
and
B\subseteq Y
,
(\sigma,\tau)
reduces
{\mathbf{1}}_{A,X}
to
{\mathbf{1}}_{B,Y}
if and only if
A\leq^{X,Y}_{W}B
(in case
\tau
is the identity)
or
A\leq^{X,Y}_{W}Y\setminus B
(in case
\tau
swaps
0
and
1
).

Fix a continuous reduction
(\sigma,\tau)
from
f
to
g
, and assume that
U\subseteq Y
is open. Note that we have
f^{-1}(U)=(g\sigma)^{-1}(\tau^{-1}(U))=\sigma^{-1}(g^{-1}(\tau^{-1}(U)))
. By continuity of
\tau
,
\tau^{-1}(U)
is open in
\im(g\sigma)
so there exists an open set
V
in
Y^{\prime}
with
\tau^{-1}(U)=V\cap\im(g\sigma)
. Since
g
is
\mathbf{\Gamma}
-measurable and
\sigma
is continuous, it follows that
g\sigma:X\to Y^{\prime}
is
\mathbf{\Gamma}
-measurable. Therefore, we get that
f^{-1}(U)=(g\sigma)^{-1}(\tau^{-1}(U))=(g\sigma)^{-1}(V)\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(X)
, as desired.
∎

We use the following basic facts all along the paper without reference.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
f
be a function between topological spaces.

(i)

If
X\subseteq\dom f
then
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{X}\leq f
,

(ii)

if
f
is continuous and
\dom f
embeds in
X
then
f\leq\id_{X}
, and

(iii)

if
f
is continuous and
\im f
embeds in
Y
then
f\leq\id_{Y}
.

Proof.

The desired continuous reductions are:

(\id_{X},\id_{f(X)})
;
(\sigma,f\tau)
with
(\sigma,\tau)
witnessing the embedding from
\dom f
into
X
;
and finally
(\sigma f,\tau)
with
(\sigma,\tau)
witnessing the embedding from
\im f
into
Y
.
∎

Using the universal properties of
\mathbb{Q}
,
2^{\mathbb{N}}
and
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}

[
Kec95
, (7.12),(7.8)]
, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 2.3
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be continuous.

(i)

If
\im f
is countable and metrizable, then
f\leq\id_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

(ii)

If
X
is zero-dimensional separable metrizable, then
f\leq\id_{\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}}
and
f\leq\id_{2^{\mathbb{N}}}
.

In particular,
\id_{2^{\mathbb{N}}}\equiv\id_{\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}}
.

Finally, recall from the introduction that when a reduction
(\sigma,\tau)
from
f
to
g
consists of topological embeddings, we say that
f

embeds topologically
3
3

3

Observe that this actually coincides with the following definition used in the articles cited in the introduction:
f
embeds in
g
if there is a pair
(\sigma,\tau)
of embeddings such that
\tau f=g\sigma
. See
[
CPV19
, Section 3]
for more details.

in
g
, we denote it by
f\sqsubseteq g
.
Topological embeddability and continuous reducibility coincide on topological embeddings.

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
f
and
g
be functions between topological spaces.
Assume that
(\sigma,\tau)
reduces
f
to
g
.

If
f
is injective, then
\sigma
,
g\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\im\sigma}
and
\tau
are injective too.

If moreover
f
is an embedding, then so is
g\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\im\sigma}
, and actually
(\sigma,\tau)
embeds
f
in
g
.

Proof.

To see the first point, note that if any of those maps is not injective, nor is
f
since
f=\tau g\sigma
holds.

If now
f
is an embedding, by definition there is an embedding
h
such that
hf=\id_{\dom f}
, but since
hf=h\tau g\sigma=\id_{\dom f}
, we know that
\sigma
is an embedding too.
Similarly
fh=\tau g\sigma h=\id_{\im f}
and
\tau
is an embedding. Finally we get
g\sigma h\tau=\id_{\im\sigma}
, which concludes.
∎

We say that a function
f:X\to Y
is
homeomorphic
to a function
f^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}
if
f\leq f^{\prime}
is witnessed by
(\sigma,\tau)
where
\sigma:X\to X^{\prime}
and
\tau:\im f^{\prime}\to\im f
are both homeomorphisms.
Note that in this special case, not only does
f
embed in
f^{\prime}
and reduces to
f^{\prime}
, but also the pair
(\sigma^{-1},\tau^{-1})
is a homeomorphism from
f^{\prime}
to
f
. Let us denote by
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
the class of functions
f:A\to B
where
A,B
are subsets of the Baire space
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
.

Proposition 2.5
.

Any function between
0
-dimensional separable metrizable spaces is homeomorphic to a function in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
.
So is any function from a
0
-dimensional separable metrizable space to a countable metrizable space.

Proof.

Because any
0
-dimensional separable metrizable space is homeomorphic to a subset of
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
and every countable metrizable space is homeomorphic to a subset of
\mathbb{Q}
, hence
0
-dimensional.
∎

2.1
Non-scattered functions

Recall that a topological space
X
is
scattered
if any of its non-empty subset has an isolated point.
For a metrizable space,
X
is scattered if and only if
\mathbb{Q}
does not embed into
X
(see for instance
[
Sem59
, Théorème 2]
, or
[
Kec95
, Notes on (7.12)]
).
We show an analogous characterization for continuous functions.

Definition 2.6
.

We say that a function
f
is
scattered
if for every non-empty subset
A\subseteq\dom f
the restriction
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A}
is constant on some non-empty open subset of
A
.

Note that
f
is not scattered if and only if it admits a non-empty restriction
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A}
which is
nowhere locally constant
,
that is such that
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}
is not constant for every non-empty open subset
U
of
A
.
Remark that the identity on the space of rationals
\id_{\mathbb{Q}}
is not scattered. For continuous functions between metrizable spaces, this function is actually the only obstruction to being scattered.

Theorem 2.7
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a continuous function from a metrizable space to a Hausdorff space.
If
f
is not scattered, then
\id_{\mathbb{Q}}\sqsubseteq f
.

Proof.

If
f
is not scattered, then it admits a non-empty restriction
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A}
that is nowhere locally constant and
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A}\sqsubseteq f
.
Hence, it suffices to show that if
f
is nowhere locally constant, then
\id_{\mathbb{Q}}\sqsubseteq f
.

Let
d
be a compatible metric for
X
.
We make the following observation: if
x\in X
,
\epsilon>0
and
f(B(x,\epsilon))\subseteq U
for some open set
U
of
Y
,
then there exists
x^{\prime}\in B(x,\epsilon)
,
\epsilon^{\prime}<\epsilon
and open sets
U_{0},U_{1}\subseteq U
such that
B(x,\epsilon^{\prime}),B(x^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime})\subseteq B(x,\epsilon)
,

U_{0}\cap U_{1}=\emptyset
,
f(B(x,\epsilon^{\prime}))\subseteq U_{0}
and
f(B(x^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime}))\subseteq U_{1}
.
To see this, note that since
f
is nowhere locally constant there exists
x^{\prime}\in B(x,\epsilon)
such that
f(x)\neq f(x^{\prime})
.
Since
Y
is Hausdorff, there exists disjoint neighborhoods
U_{0},U_{1}\subseteq U
separating
f(x)
from
f(x^{\prime})
.
By continuity of
f
, we can find a small enough
\epsilon^{\prime}<\epsilon
such that
f(B(x,\epsilon^{\prime}))\subseteq U_{0}
and
f(B(x^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime}))\subseteq U_{1}
and
B(x,\epsilon^{\prime}),B(x^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime})\subseteq B(x,\epsilon)
.

We use this to define Cantor schemes
4
4

4

See
[
Kec95
, Definition 6.1]
for a definition of
Cantor scheme
.

(C_{s})_{s\in 2^{<\mathbb{N}}}
consisting of open balls
B(x_{s},\rho_{s})
of
X
and
(U_{s})_{s\in 2^{<\mathbb{N}}}
consisting of open sets of
Y
such that for all
s\in 2^{<\mathbb{N}}
:

(i)

f(C_{s\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(i)})\subseteq U_{s\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(i)}
for
i=0,1
,

(ii)

\rho_{s}\leq 2^{-|s|}
.

Choose
x_{\emptyset}\in X
, let
\rho_{\emptyset}=1
, set
C_{\emptyset}=B(x,1)
and
U_{\emptyset}=Y
. Assume we have defined
C_{s}=B(x_{s},\rho_{s})
.
The preliminary observation allows us to find
x^{\prime}
and
\rho\leq 2^{-(|s|+1)}
such that
B(x_{s},\rho)
and
B(x^{\prime},\rho)
are included in
C_{s}
and whose images by
f
are separated by open sets
U_{0},U_{1}\subseteq U_{s}
.
We set
x_{s\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)}=x_{s}
,
x_{s\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)}=x^{\prime}
,
\rho_{s\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)}=\rho_{s\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)}=\rho
and
U_{s\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(i)}=U_{i}
for
i=0,1
.

Note that by construction, we have
\{x_{s}\}=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}C_{s\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{n}}
for every
s\in 2^{<\mathbb{N}}
.
Identifying
\mathbb{Q}
with the subset of
2^{\mathbb{N}}
of sequences that are eventually constant equal to
0
, our Cantor scheme induces a function
\sigma:\mathbb{Q}\to X
which is an embedding by
[
Kec95
, (7.6)]
.
Moreover,
f\sigma
is an embedding too.
To see this, note that
f\sigma(\mathbb{Q}\cap N_{s})=f\sigma(\mathbb{Q})\cap U_{s}
for all
s\in 2^{<\mathbb{N}}
.
Indeed if
x\in\mathbb{Q}\cap N_{s}
then by construction
\sigma(x)\in C_{s}
and so
f\sigma(x)\in U_{s}
.
Conversely, if
x\notin\mathbb{Q}\cap N_{s}
then
x\in N_{t}
for some
t\in 2^{<\mathbb{N}}
incompatible with
s
and so
f\sigma(x)\in U_{t}
but then
f\sigma(x)\notin U_{s}
since
U_{s}\cap U_{t}=\emptyset
.

Since
f\sigma
is an embedding, its inverse
\tau:\im(f\sigma)\to\mathbb{Q}
,
f\sigma(x)\mapsto x
is an embedding too and clearly
\id_{\mathbb{Q}}=\tau f\sigma
. Therefore
\id_{\mathbb{Q}}\sqsubseteq f
, as desired.
∎

Continuity
5
5

5

Metrizability as well, see
[
Sem59
, Footnote of Théorème 2]
.
is needed in
Theorem

2.7

Example 2.8

(A non-scattered function that does not reduce
\id_{\mathbb{Q}}
)
.

Let
f
be the characteristic function of a dense and co-dense subset of
\mathbb{Q}
.
Then
f
is not scattered because it is nowhere locally constant, as its image on every basic open set is exactly
2
.
Being discontinuous and having finite image, it cannot continuously reduce
\id_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

For a finite basis result for all functions from
\mathbb{Q}
to an analytic metric space with respect to closed topological embeddability, see
[
CM20
, Theorem 5.5]
.

Our next goal is to understand the continuous functions with uncountable range up to continuous reducibility.
For a continuous function
f
with a Polish domain and separable metric image, the proof of
Theorem

2.7
can be adapted to show that if
f
is not scattered,
then the constructed Cantor schemes are actually witnessing
\id_{2^{\mathbb{N}}}\sqsubseteq f
.

We next show that we can extend this phenomenon to analytic domains and Borel functions,
for which we first establish equivalent conditions for the embeddability of
\id_{2^{\mathbb{N}}}
into a function.
Recall that a function is
Baire-measurable
if preimages of open sets have the Baire property,
and that
\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{2}
sets are countable intersections of open sets.

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
f
be a function between topological spaces. The following are equivalent:

(i)

There is an embedding
\sigma:2^{\mathbb{N}}\to\dom(f)
such that
f\sigma
is an embedding.

(ii)

There is a Baire-measurable
\sigma:2^{\mathbb{N}}\to\dom(f)
such that
f\sigma
is an embedding.

(iii)

There is
K\subseteq\dom(f)
homeomorphic to
2^{\mathbb{N}}
such that
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{K}
is both injective and Baire-measurable.

Proof.

Any embedding – being continuous – is Baire-measurable, so the first item implies the second one.

If
f\sigma
is injective, then so is
\sigma
. Since
\sigma
is moreover Baire-measurable on
2^{\mathbb{N}}

then by
[
Kec95
, (8.38)]
it must be continuous on a dense
\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{2}
subset
G
of
2^{\mathbb{N}}
.
In particular,
G
is a perfect Polish space and so there exists an embedding
\sigma^{\prime}:2^{\mathbb{N}}\to G

[
Kec95
, (6.2)]
.
Now
\sigma\sigma^{\prime}
is both injective and continuous, so
K=\im\sigma\sigma^{\prime}
is homeomorphic to
2^{\mathbb{N}}
by compactness. Moreover,
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{K}=(f\sigma)\sigma^{-1}
is an embedding as a composition of embeddings. Hence
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{K}
is injective and Baire measurable, which shows that the second item implies the third one.

Finally, suppose there is
K\subseteq\dom(f)
homeomorphic to
2^{\mathbb{N}}
such that
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{K}
is both injective and Baire-measurable. By

[
Kec95
, (8.38)]
again,
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{K}
is continuous on a dense
\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{2}
subset of
K
. So there exists an embedding
\sigma:2^{\mathbb{N}}\to K

such that
f\sigma
is both injective and continuous, hence an embedding by compactness, and so the first item holds.
∎

We say that a class of functions has the
Perfect Function Property
if every function in it either has countable image or
\id_{2^{\mathbb{N}}}
embeds in
f
.
Observe that a class of spaces has the perfect set property if and only if the class of their identity functions has the perfect function property.

The following proposition has various proofs. It was first proven in
[
Car13b
, Proposition 2.1]
using Silver’s Theorem and
a game-theoretic proof was recently found by Lutz and Siskind
[
LS23
]
.
These proofs both use the third item from
Proposition

2.9
; here we propose another proof, suggested to us by Zoltán Vidnyánszky.

A subset
A
of a topological space
X
is
Borel
if it is in the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the open sets,
and a function is
Borel
if it is Borel-measurable. Note that zero-dimensionality is not used in the following result.

Proposition 2.10
.

The class of Borel functions from analytic to separable metrizable spaces has the Perfect Function Property.

Proof.

Let
f:X\to Y
be Borel with uncountable image. As
X
is analytic we can take a continuous surjection
\phi:\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\to X
.
Since
\im(f\phi)
is analytic and its completion is an uncountable Polish space, the Perfect Set Theorem for analytic sets
[
Kec95
, (29.1)]
ensures that there is an embedding
\tau:2^{\mathbb{N}}\to\im(f\phi)
.
The relation
xRx^{\prime}
if and only if
\tau(x)=f\phi(x^{\prime})
is analytic in
2^{\mathbb{N}}\times\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
, so by the Jankov–von Neumann uniformization Theorem (
[
Kec95
, (29.9)]
)
it has a Baire-measurable uniformizing function
\sigma:2^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
, so
f\phi\sigma=\tau
is an embedding, and this concludes by
Proposition

2.9
.
∎

2.2
Scattered functions

We generalize the Cantor–Bendixson analysis of spaces to functions, which was already initiated in
[
Car13b
]
.

Given a function
f
and
x\in\dom f
, we say that
x
is
f
-isolated
if
f^{-1}(\{f(x)\})
is a neighbourhood of
x
,
i.e.
f
is locally constant at
x
.
Note that a function
f
is scattered if and only if every non-empty subset
A
of
\dom f
contains an
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A}
-isolated point.
Given
A\subseteq\dom f
, we let
I(f,A)
stand for the set of all
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A}
-isolated points. It is an open subset of
A
.
We now define inductively the following closed sets of
\dom f
:

\displaystyle\CB_{0}(f)

\displaystyle=\dom f,

\displaystyle\CB_{\alpha+1}(f)

\displaystyle=\CB_{\alpha}(f)\backslash I(f,\CB_{\alpha}(f)),

\displaystyle\CB_{\lambda}(f)

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{\alpha<\lambda}\CB_{\alpha}(f)\mbox{ for }\lambda\mbox{ limit.}

We set
\CB(f)=\min\{\alpha\mid\CB_{\alpha}(f)=\CB_{\alpha+1}(f)\}
, and
we call it the
Cantor–Bendixson rank
of
f
, or
\CB
-rank
for short.
Recall from the introduction that
\ker_{\CB}{f}:=\CB_{\CB(f)}(f)
is called the perfect kernel of
f
, and
f
is nowhere locally constant on it.
In particular, functions with an empty perfect kernel play a central role in this paper as are exactly the scattered functions.
Note that this equivalence stated in introduction as part of
Theorem

1.3
actually holds for functions between topological spaces.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
f
and
g
be two functions between topological spaces. Then
f
is scattered if and only if
f
has empty perfect kernel.

Proof.

If
f
has non-empty perfect kernel
A
, then by definition of the Cantor–Bendixson derivative
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A}
is nowhere locally constant, so
f
is not scattered.
Conversely, if
f
has empty perfect kernel, then for some ordinal
\alpha
we have
\dom(f)=\bigcup_{\beta<\alpha}I(f,\CB_{\beta}(f))
,
so given any non-empty
A\subseteq\dom(f)
, setting
\gamma=\min\{\beta<\alpha\mid A\cap I(f,\CB_{\beta}(f))\neq\emptyset\}
,
then
A\subseteq\CB_{\gamma}(f)
and
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A}
is locally constant at any
x\in A\cap I(f,\CB_{\gamma}(f))
, which shows that
f
is scattered.
∎

The Cantor–Bendixson rank is defined using a closed derivative, so any function with a second countable domain has a countable
\CB
-rank
6
6

6

See
[
Kec95
, Theorem 6.9]
.
Since a locally constant function on a second countable space has countable range, it follows from
Proposition

2.11
that any scattered function with second countable domain has countable image.
In particular, every function from
Main Theorem

3
, namely the scattered continuous functions from a zero-dimensional separable metrizable space to a metrizable space, has countable range and therefore is homeomorphic to a function in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
by
Proposition

2.5
. With this in mind, we define the class of functions which is the main focus of this article as follows:

Definition 2.12
.

The class
\mathsf{Scat}
denotes the class of functions
f\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
which are continuous and scattered.

We are now in a position to show that
Theorem

1.2
reduces to
Theorem

1.4
.
Recall that both
wqo
and
bqo
are closed under finite unions, so if
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
are
wqo
(resp.
bqo
) then so is
Q\cup Q^{\prime}
.
Recall as well that quasi-orders whose induced equivalence relation has finitely many classes are
bqo
(see
[
CP20
, Example 3.8 and Proposition 3.11]
).
As announced we have:

Theorem 2.13
.

For every function
f
that satisifies the hypothesis of
Theorem

1.2
, one of the following holds:
f\equiv\id_{\mathbb{Q}}
,
f\equiv\id_{\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}}
or
f
is scattered.
In particular,
Theorem

1.4
implies
Theorem

1.2
.

Proof.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a continuous function with
X
zero-dimensional separable metrizable and
Y
metrizable. If
f
is scattered then being continuous with second countable domain it has countable range. So in light of
Proposition

2.5
, there exists
\hat{f}\in\mathsf{Scat}
such that
f
is homeomorphic to
\hat{f}
and in particular
f\equiv\hat{f}
.

So suppose that
f
is non scattered, so
\id_{\mathbb{Q}}\sqsubseteq f
by
Theorem

2.7
. If
\im f
is furthemore countable, then actually
f\equiv\id_{\mathbb{Q}}
by
Corollary

2.3
. Otherwise if
X
is furthermore analytic, then
f
has the Perfect Function Property by
Proposition

2.10
and so
\id_{\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}}\sqsubseteq f
. In this case, since
X
is zero-dimensional separable metrizable, then in fact
f\equiv\id_{\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}}

Corollary

2.3
.

Therefore, up to continuous equivalence, the class of functions described in
Theorem

1.2
can be written as the union of
\mathsf{Scat}
together with the two equivalence classes of
\id_{\mathbb{Q}}
or
\id_{\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}}
. Hence if
\mathsf{Scat}
is
bqo
then the whole class is
bqo
.
∎

Here are few basic facts about our closed derivative.

Fact 2.14
.

Let
f
be a function between topological spaces.

(i)

For every open set
U\subseteq\dom(f)
and every ordinal
\alpha
we have
\CB_{\alpha}(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U})=\CB_{\alpha}(f)\cap U
.

(ii)

If
f
scattered and
\CB(f)
is limit, then for all
x\in\dom(f)
there is an open
U\ni x
with
\CB(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U})<\CB(f)
.

(iii)

If
f
is scattered and has nonempty compact domain, then
\CB(f)
is a successor ordinal
\alpha+1
and
f(\CB_{\alpha}(f))
is finite.

Proof.

(i)

By induction on
\alpha
, observing that for
U
open and
x\in U
we have
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}
is locally constant at
x
if and only if
f
is locally constant at
x
.

(ii)

As
\ker_{\CB}{f}=\bigcap_{\alpha<\CB(f)}\CB_{\alpha}(f)=\emptyset
, for all
x\in\dom f
there exists
\alpha<\CB(f)

such that
x
belongs to the open set
U=\dom f\setminus\CB_{\alpha}(f)
. By the first item, it follows that
\CB(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U})\leq\alpha
.

(iii)

If
f
had a limit
\CB
-rank
\lambda
, then the decreasing sequence of closed sets
(\CB_{\alpha}(f))_{\alpha<\lambda}
would have empty intersection,
a contradiction with the compactness of
\dom f
.
So
\CB(f)=\alpha+1
and
\CB_{\alpha}(f)
is compact – as a closed set of
\dom f
. Since
\CB_{\alpha+1}(f)=\emptyset
,
the sets
f^{-1}(\{y\})\cap\CB_{\alpha}(f)
for
y\in\im(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\CB_{\alpha}(f)})
, form a partition of
\CB_{\alpha}(f)
in nonempty open sets,
so
f
must have must have finite range on
\CB_{\alpha}(f)
.∎

The following basic results on the interplay between continuous reducibility and the
\CB
-derivative on functions is proven in
[
Car13b
, Proposition 2.2 and Corollary 2.3]
for Polish spaces,
but the same proofs work for arbitrary functions between topological spaces.

Proposition 2.15
.

Let
f
and
g
be two functions between topological spaces.

(i)

If
f\leq g
and
g
is scattered, then
f
is scattered too.

(ii)

If
(\sigma,\tau)
continuously reduces
f
to
g
, then for all
\alpha
we have
\sigma(\CB_{\alpha}(f))\subseteq\CB_{\alpha}(g)
.

Proof.

Let
(\sigma,\tau)
continuously reduce
f
to
g
and
A\subseteq\dom f
. We show that for all
x\in A
, if
g\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\sigma(A)}
is locally constant at
\sigma(x)
, then
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A}
is locally constant at
x
. If
g\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\sigma(A)}
is locally constant at
\sigma(x)
, then there exists
U\ni\sigma(x)
open in
\dom g
such that
g(U\cap\sigma(A))
is a singleton. By continuity of
\sigma
there exists an open set
V\ni x
of
\dom f
such that
\sigma(V)\subseteq U
.
Hence
g\sigma(V\cap A)
is a singleton, and therefore
f=\tau g\sigma
is constant on
V\cap A
. This directly implies the first item and by induction it yields the second item.
∎

We now have all the elements to prove
Theorem

1.3
from the introduction:
a continuous function between metrizable spaces is scattered if and only if it has non-empty perfect kernel if and only if
\id_{\mathbb{Q}}
does not embed in it.

Proof of
Theorem

1.3
.

After
Proposition

2.11
, note that
\id_{\mathbb{Q}}
is not scattered and use
Theorem

2.7
and
Proposition

2.15

(i)
.
∎

Here is useful relation on the
\CB
-rank of a scattered function and the ranks of its restrictions to open sets.

Corollary 2.16
.

Let
f
be a scattered function and
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
be an open covering of
\dom(f)
for some set
I
.
Then
\CB(f)=\sup_{i\in I}\CB(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{i}})
.

Proof.

Set
\alpha=\sup_{i\in I}\CB(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{i}})
. Using
Fact

2.14

(i)
, for all
i\in I
, we have
\CB_{\alpha}(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{i}})=\emptyset
and

\CB(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{i}})=\CB(f)\cap A_{i}
, and so:

\CB_{\alpha}(f)\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in I}\CB_{\alpha}(f)\cap A_{i}=\bigcup_{i\in I}\CB_{\alpha}(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{i}})=\emptyset,

and so
\CB(f)\leq\alpha
. Now if
\beta<\alpha
, then
\beta<\CB(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{i}})
for some
{i\in I}
and

\CB_{\beta}(f)\supseteq\CB_{\beta}(f)\cap A_{i}=\CB_{\beta}(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{i}})\neq\emptyset.

Since
\CB_{\CB(f)}(f)=\emptyset
, it follows that
\beta<\CB(f)
, hence
\alpha=\CB(f)
.
∎

Given a scattered function
f
, we define as follows the
\CB
-degree
of
f
that we denote by
N_{f}
. When
\CB(f)
is limit or null, set
N_{f}=0
.
When
\CB(f)=\alpha+1
for some
\alpha<\omega_{1}
, we let
N_{f}
be the cardinality of
f(\CB_{\alpha}(f))
.
We also call
\CB
-type
of
f
the pair
\tp(f)=(\CB(f),N_{f})
.

We denote by
\leq_{lex}
the lexicographical order on sequences of ordinals. Here is an immediate application of
Proposition

2.15
(see
[
Car13b
, Fact, p. 642]
).

Corollary 2.17
.

For any scattered functions
f
and
g
,
f\leq g
implies
\tp(f)\leq_{lex}\tp(g)
.

Proof.

Assume that
f\leq g
as witnessed by
(\sigma,\tau)
. By
Proposition

2.15

(ii)
, we have
\sigma(\CB_{\CB(g)(f)})\subseteq\ker_{\CB}g=\emptyset
so
\CB(f)\leq\CB(g)
. If
\CB(f)<\CB(g)
, then
\tp(f)\leq_{lex}\tp(g)
. So assume that
\CB(f)=\CB(g)=\alpha
. If
\alpha
is limit, then by definition
N_{f}=N_{g}=0
. Otherwise,
\alpha=\beta+1
and
\sigma(\CB_{\beta}(f))\subseteq\CB_{\beta}(g)
by
Proposition

2.15

(ii)
.
Hence for every
x\in\CB_{\beta}(f)
we have
f(x)=\tau(g\sigma(x))
with
g\sigma(x)\in g(\CB_{\beta}(g))
,
which shows that
\tau
maps surjectively a subset of
g(\CB_{\beta}(g))
onto
f(\CB_{\beta}(f))
, so
N_{g}\geq N_{f}
as desired.
∎

2.3
Disjoint union

Definition 2.18
.

Given an index set
I
and a sequence
(f_{i})_{i\in I}
of functions between topological spaces,
we say that a function
f
is the
disjoint union
of the maps
f_{i}
and we write
f=\bigsqcup_{i\in I}f_{i}

if
f=\bigcup_{i\in I}f_{i}
and
(\dom f_{i})_{i\in I}
is a clopen partition of
\dom(f)
.

Note that the disjoint union over the empty set is the empty function.
The previous definition makes sense for any set
I
, but in this paper
I
will always be countable, so in general we assume that
I\subseteq\mathbb{N}
.
When the context is clear, we write
(f_{i})_{i}
instead of
(f_{i})_{i\in I}
.

The main reason for the disjoint union operation is its the following strong link with local properties for functions with
0
-dimensional domains.
Given a class
\mathcal{F}
of functions, we say that a function
f
is
locally in
\mathcal{F}
, or
locally
\mathcal{F}
for short,
if for all
x\in\dom(f)
there is an open
U\ni x
such that
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}\in\mathcal{F}
.

Given sequences
s\in\mathbb{N}^{<\mathbb{N}}
and
x\in\mathbb{N}^{\leq\mathbb{N}}
we write
s\sqsubseteq x
when
s
is a
prefix
, or an
initial segment
, of
x
.
Recall that the sets
N_{s}=\{x\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid s\sqsubseteq x\}
for all
s\in\mathbb{N}^{<\mathbb{N}}
form a basis of clopen sets for
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
.
A
tree

T
(on
\mathbb{N}
) is a subset of
\mathbb{N}^{<\mathbb{N}}
closed under prefixes, it is
pruned
when all
s\in T
have a strict extension
t\sqsupset s
in
T
.
In this case we denote by
[T]
the set of infinite branches of
T
.
Recall that sets of the form
[T]
for pruned trees
T
on
\mathbb{N}
are in bijection with closed sets
C\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
(see
[
Kec95
, (2.4)]
).

Proposition 2.19
.

Let
f
be a function with separable metrizable 0-dimensional domain and
\mathcal{F}
a class of functions.
Then
f
is locally
\mathcal{F}
if and only if
f=\bigsqcup_{i\in I}f_{i}
for some sequence of functions
(f_{i})_{i\in I}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

Being separable metrizable and 0-dimensional, the domain of
f
is homeomorphic to a subset of
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
,
so without loss of generality we can suppose that
X=\dom f
is actually a subset of
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
.
Call
T
the pruned subtree of
\mathbb{N}^{<\mathbb{N}}
satisfying
[T]=\overline{X}
the closure of
X
in
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
.
Let
S=\{s\in T\mid f\upharpoonright{(N_{s}\cap X)}\in\mathcal{F}\}
and define
B
as the set of
\sqsubseteq
-minimal elements of
S
.
The elements of
B
are pairwise incomparable for the prefix relation
\sqsubseteq
, so the clopen sets
N_{s}
for
s\in B
are pairwise disjoint.
Since
f
is locally
\mathcal{F}
, for all
x\in X
there exists
s\in B
with
s\sqsubset x
, so the sets
N_{s}\cap X
for
s\in B
form an open cover of
X
.
We have thus obtained a clopen partition
\{N_{s}\cap X\mid s\in B\}
of
\dom f
such that
f\upharpoonright{(N_{s}\cap X)}\in\mathcal{F}
for all
s\in B
, proving our point.
∎

Scattered functions
f
with successor rank
\alpha+1
which are constant on
\CB_{\alpha}(f)
are ubiquitous and play an important role in the sequel.

Definition 2.20
.

A function
f
is said to be
simple
if it is scattered with
\CB
-degree
1
. A simple function has successor
\CB
-rank
\alpha+1
for some ordinal
\alpha
and we call the unique point in the image of
f
on
\CB_{\alpha}(f)
the
distinguished point of
f
.

A first immediate but crucial application of
Proposition

2.19
is the following essential lemma that generalizes
[
Car13b
, Lemma 2.4]
with the same proof.

Lemma 2.21

(Decomposition Lemma)
.

Any scattered function from a
0
-dimensional separable metrizable space is locally simple.

If moreover it has successor rank
\beta+1
, then it is locally of type
(\beta+1,1)
.

Proof.

Let
f:X\to Y
be scattered with
X
a
0
-dimensional separable metrizable.
By induction on
\CB(f)
. Vacuously true if
\CB(f)=0
. If
\CB(f)
is limit, then every
x\in\dom f
admits a clopen neighbourhood
C
such that
\CB(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{C})<\CB(f)

and the result follows by induction hypothesis and
Proposition

2.19
.
Finally, assume that
\CB(f)=\beta+1
and let
I=f(\CB_{\beta}(f))
. Since
f
is locally constant on the closed set
\CB_{\beta}(f)
,
we can choose for each
y\in I
an open set
U_{y}
of
\dom(f)
such that
U_{y}\cap\CB_{\beta}(f)=f^{-1}(\{y\})\cap\CB_{\beta}(f)
.
Applying the generalized reduction property of open sets
[
Kec95
, 22.16]
to the open cover of
\dom(f)
given by
V_{y}=U_{y}\cup(\dom(f)\setminus\CB_{\beta}(f))
for
y\in I

yields a clopen partition
(C_{y})_{y\in I}
of
\dom(f)
with
C_{y}\subseteq V_{y}
for all
y\in I
.
Note that for all
y\in I
we have
C_{y}\cap\CB_{\beta}(f)=f^{-1}(\{y\})\cap\CB_{\beta}(f)
,
which readily implies that each
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{C_{y}}
is simple of
\CB
-rank equal to
\beta+1
using
Fact

2.14

(i)
, as desired.
∎

We say that an operation on a class
\mathcal{C}
of objects – such as sets, spaces, functions –
preserves
a property
\mathcal{F}
if
the operation gives a result in
\mathcal{F}
whenever the operands are in
\mathcal{F}
.
For instance, the disjoint union operation preserves continuity by the following basic but useful criterion for continuity, similar to
[
Eng89
, 2.2.4 and 2.2.6]
.

Given a metrizable space
X
and an index set
I
, we say that a family
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
of subsets of
X
is a
relative clopen partition
in
X

when
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
forms a clopen partition of the subspace
\bigcup_{i\in I}A_{i}
, or equivalently
if the sets
A_{i}
are pairwise disjoint and relatively open in
\bigcup_{i}A_{i}
.
Note that a pairwise disjoint family
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
is a relative clopen partition exactly when the labelling function
c:\bigcup_{i}A_{i}\to I
,

c(a)=i
if and only if
a\in A_{i}
, is continuous when
I
is endowed with the discrete topology.

Lemma 2.22
.

Let
X
and
Y
be topological spaces with
X
metrizable and
(f_{i}:A_{i}\to B_{i})_{i\in I}
be countable sequence of continuous functions with
A_{i}\subseteq X
and
B_{i}\subseteq Y
for all
i\in I
.
If
(A_{i})_{i}
is a relative clopen partition, then
f=\bigsqcup_{i\in I}f_{i}
is continuous.

Proof.

Since
X
is metrizable, it is enough to prove that for any sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converging in
\bigcup_{i\in I}A_{i}
to
x
, the sequence of images converges to
f(x)
.
If
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges in
\bigcup_{i}A_{i}
, say to
x\in A_{i}
for some
i
,
then as
A_{i}
is open in
\bigcup_{i}A_{i}
, then for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
we have
x_{n}\in A_{i}
for all
n\geq N
. Since
(f_{i}(x_{n}))_{n\geq N}
converges to
f_{i}(x)
by continuity of
f_{i}
, it follows that
(f(x_{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
f_{i}(x)=f(x)
.
∎

We remark that we can remove the metrizability assumption on
X
in the above result by requiring that the sets
A_{i}
are
separated by open sets
, namely that
there is a family
(B_{i})_{i\in I}
of pairwise disjoint open sets in
X
such that
A_{i}\subseteq B_{i}
for all
i\in I
.
In fact, in metrizables spaces, relative clopen partitions coincide with families separated by open sets. This follows from the fact that metric spaces are paracompact
[
Rud69
]
and therefore hereditarily collectionwise normal
[
Eng89
, Theorem 5.1.18 and Problem 5.5.1]
.
For
0
-dimensional separable metrizable spaces, a direct proof of this fact can be given using similar ideas as in
Proposition

2.19
.

2.4
Gluing

We close this preliminary section with the study of the Gluing operation which provides a natural
‘addition’
for functions. Combined with the Pointed Gluing operation, also defined in
[
Car13b
]
and studied in
Section

3
, this operation will allow us to define maximum (respectively minimum) functions among functions in
\mathsf{Scat}
with
\CB
-rank at most
\alpha
(respectively at least
\alpha
) for all countable ordinal
\alpha
.

Given sequences
s\in\mathbb{N}^{<\mathbb{N}}
and
x\in\mathbb{N}^{\leq\mathbb{N}}
we denote by
s\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}x
the concatenation of
s
with
x
,
and for any
A\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\leq\mathbb{N}}
the notation
s\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}A
stands for
\{s\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}x\mid x\in A\}
.
The following operation is called
sum of the mappings
in
[
Eng89
, 2.2.E]
.

Definition 2.23
.

We call
gluing
of a countable sequence
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
of subsets of
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
the set
\bigoplus_{i\in I}A_{i}=\bigcup_{i\in I}(i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}A_{i}
.
Given a countable sequence of functions
(f_{i}:A_{i}\rightarrow B_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
,
We call
gluing
of
(f_{i})_{i\in I}
, the map

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i\in I}f_{i}:\bigoplus_{i\in I}A_{i}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{i\in I}B_{i}

\displaystyle(i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}x

\displaystyle\longmapsto(i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}f_{i}(x).

The following facts are either classical exercises left to the reader, or a direct consequence of
Corollary

2.16
.

Fact 2.24
.

(i)

The gluing operation preserves countability
7
7

7

Because we only consider
countable
gluings here.
, openness, closure, and Polishness of sets; continuity, injectivity, surjectivity and scatteredness of functions.

(ii)

For
I\subseteq\mathbb{N}
and
(f_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
, we have
\CB(\bigoplus_{i}f_{i})=\CB(\bigsqcup_{i}f_{i})=\sup_{i}\CB(f_{i})
.

(iii)

Gluing
commutes with the identity natural transformation
, that is for any
(X_{i})_{i\in I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
we have
\id_{\bigoplus_{i\in I}X_{i}}=\bigoplus_{i\in I}(\id_{X_{i}})
.

2.4.1
Gluing as an upper bound

Our main task for each of our three operations is to prove sufficient – and, whenever possible, necessary – conditions on operands
(g_{i})_{i}

for the result of the operation to reduce (or be reduced by) an arbitrary function
f
.
The next result represents the first step, characterizing the situation where
\bigoplus_{i}g_{i}
is an upper bound for a given function
f
.
These results are all new, except for the next one that was somehow already present in
[
Car13b
, Lemma 3.3]
.

Proposition 2.25

(Gluing as upper bound)
.

Let
(g_{i}:X_{i}\to Y_{i})_{i\in I}
be countable sequence in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
. The following are equivalent for every function
f:A\to B
between topological spaces:

(i)

f\leq\bigoplus_{i\in I}g_{i}
,

(ii)

there is a clopen partition
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
of
A
such that
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{i}}\leq g_{i}
for all
i\in I
.

Proof.

Suppose that for all
i\in I
there is a continuous reduction
(\sigma_{i},\tau_{i})
from
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{i}}
to
g_{i}
. Set then
\sigma:x\mapsto(i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}\sigma_{i}(x)
if
x\in A_{i}
and
\tau:(i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}y\mapsto\tau_{i}(y)
. The map
\tau
is continuous by
Lemma

2.22
. Since the sets
A_{i}
are pairwise disjoint,
\sigma
is well-defined, and it is continuous because the sets
A_{i}
are clopen.
The fact that
(\sigma_{i},\tau_{i})
is a reduction for all
i\in I
implies that
(\sigma,\tau)
is a reduction from
f
to
\bigoplus_{i\in I}g_{i}
.

Suppose now that
(\sigma,\tau)
continuously reduces
f
to
\bigoplus_{i}g_{i}
, and set
A_{i}=\sigma^{-1}(N_{(i)})
.
Since
(N_{(i)})_{i}
is a clopen partition of
\dom(\bigoplus_{i\in I}g_{i})
and
\sigma
is continuous,
(A_{i})_{i}
is a clopen partition of
\dom(f)
.
Conclude by observing that
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{j}}\leq(\bigoplus_{i\in I}g_{i})\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{N_{(j)}}\equiv g_{j}
for all
j\in I
.
∎

The following simple corollary will be used extensively in the sequel.

Corollary 2.26
.

Let
f:A\to B
in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
and let
\mathsf{P}
be a clopen partition of
A
.
Then
f=\bigsqcup_{P\in\mathsf{P}}f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\leq\bigoplus_{P\in\mathsf{P}}f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}
.

2.4.2
Gluing as a lower bound

Dually, bounding a function
f
from below with a gluing involves corestrictions of
f
.

Proposition 2.27
.

Let
(g_{i}:X_{i}\to Y_{i})_{i\in I}
be a countable sequence in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
.
The following are equivalent for every function
f:A\to B
between metrizable spaces:

(i)

\bigoplus_{i\in I}g_{i}\leq f

(ii)

there exists a family
(U_{i})_{i\in I}
of subsets of
B
satisfying:

(a)

(U_{i})_{i\in I}
is a relative clopen partition, and

(b)

for all
i\in I
we have
g_{i}\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U_{i}}
.

Proof.

Suppose first that
(\sigma,\tau)
continuously reduces
\bigoplus_{i\in I}g_{i}
to
f
, then the sets
U_{i}=\tau^{-1}(N_{(i)})
,
i\in I
,
form a clopen partition of
\dom\tau=\im f\sigma
.
As
g_{i}\leq(\bigoplus_{j\in I}g_{j})\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{N_{(i)}}=(\tau f\sigma)\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{N_{(i)}}=(f\sigma)\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U_{i}}\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U_{i}}
, we have the direct implication.

For all
i\in I
, we fix a continuous reduction
(\sigma_{i},\tau_{i})
from
g_{i}
to
f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U_{i}}
,
and set
\sigma:(i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}x\mapsto\sigma_{i}(x)
for
x\in X_{i}
and
\tau:y\mapsto(i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}\tau_{i}(y)
for
y\in\dom\tau_{i}
.
Since the sets
U_{i}
are pairwise disjoint and
\dom\tau_{i}\subseteq U_{i}
,
(\sigma,\tau)
is a well-defined pair of maps. Note that
\sigma
is continuous by
Lemma

2.22
.
Since
(U_{i})_{i}
is a relative clopen partition and
\dom\tau_{i}\subseteq U_{i}
, so is the family
(\dom\tau_{i})_{i}
and
\tau
is continuous by
Lemma

2.22
too.
The fact that
(\sigma_{i},\tau_{i})
are reductions for all
i\in I
implies that
(\sigma,\tau)
is a reduction from
\bigoplus_{i\in I}g_{i}
to
f
.
Indeed, for
x\in X_{i}
we have
\tau f\sigma((i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}x)=(i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}\tau_{i}f\sigma_{i}(x)=(i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}g_{i}(x)
.
∎

We use the following simple corollary extensively in the sequel.

Corollary 2.28
.

Let
f:A\to B
be in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
. If
\mathcal{U}
is a family of pairwise disjoint open sets of
B
, then
\bigoplus_{U\in\mathcal{U}}f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}\leq f
.

In order to prove that a function
f
is continuously equivalent to a gluing of functions, one needs to consider both restrictions and corestrictions of
f
.
One needs to find both a clopen partition of the domain of
f
to use
Proposition

2.25
and a relatively clopen partition in the image of
f

to use
Proposition

2.27
.
While a clopen partition of the image of a continuous
f
induces a clopen partition of the domain as in the following corollary,
we will also consider families in the domain and the range of
f
that are not related in this way (see
Theorem

6.12
).

Corollary 2.29
.

Let
f:A\to B
be a continuous function between topological spaces
and
(g_{i}:X_{i}\to Y_{i})_{i\in I}
be countable sequence in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
.

If there exists a clopen partition
B=\bigsqcup_{i\in I}B_{i}
with
f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{B_{i}}\equiv g_{i}
for all
i\in I
, then
f\equiv\bigoplus_{i\in I}g_{i}
.

For any countable ordinal
\alpha
, we write
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha}
(resp.
\mathsf{Scat}_{\leq\alpha},\mathsf{Scat}_{<\alpha}
) for the set of all functions of
\CB
-rank
\alpha
(resp.
\leq\alpha,<\alpha
) in
\mathsf{Scat}
.

For instance,
\mathsf{Scat}_{0}
consists only of the empty function, and
\mathsf{Scat}_{1}
is the class of locally constant functions.

A function of finite
\CB
-degree has finite image on the last
\CB
-derivative, so the previous corollary immediately gives the generalization to
\mathsf{Scat}
of
[
Car13b
, Lemma 4.3]
:

Corollary 2.30
.

Let
\alpha<\omega_{1}
and
f\in\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha+1}
.
If the
\CB
-degree of
f
is
n+1
, then
f\equiv\bigoplus_{i\leq n}f_{i}
for some sequence
(f_{i})_{i\leq n}
of simple functions in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha+1}
.

Proof.

Take a finite clopen partition of the co-domain of
f
such that each piece contains exactly one point of the image of
f
on
\CB_{\alpha}(f)
and apply the previous corollary.
∎

Given functions
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
, we write
f_{0}\oplus f_{1}
instead of
\bigoplus_{i\in 2}f_{i}
for the binary gluing operation.
For
n\leq\omega
and
f\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
, we write
nf
for
\bigoplus_{i\in n}f
.
A direct application of
Proposition

2.25
gives:

Fact 2.31
.

Let
(f_{i})_{i\in I},(g_{k})_{k\in K}\subseteq\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
. If there exists
\iota:I\to K
with
f_{i}\leq g_{\iota(i)}
for all
i\in I
, then
\bigoplus_{i\in I}f_{i}\leq\bigoplus_{k\in K}g_{k}
.

In particular, if
f\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
and
m\leq n\leq\omega
, then
mf\leq nf
.

Definition 2.32
.

If
\mathcal{F}
is a set of functions, then we denote by
\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(\mathcal{F})
the set of all finite gluings
\bigoplus_{i<n}f_{i}
for some finite sequence
(f_{i})_{i<n}
of functions in
\mathcal{F}
.
We say that a set
\mathcal{C}
of functions is
generated
by a set
\mathcal{F}
if for all
g\in\mathcal{C}
there exists
f\in\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(\mathcal{F})
such that
g\equiv f
.

We say that
\mathcal{C}
is
finitely generated
if it generated by a finite set.

Remark that for
\mathcal{C}
to be generated by
\mathcal{F}
, we do not require
\mathcal{F}
to be a subset of
\mathcal{C}
.
Note the empty function always belongs to
\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(\mathcal{F})
as the gluing of the empty sequence and every element of
\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(\mathcal{F})
takes the form
\bigoplus_{i\leq k}n_{i}f_{i}
where
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
n_{0},\ldots,n_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
and
f_{0},\ldots,f_{k}\in\mathcal{F}
are pairwise distinct.

Given two quasi-orders
(P,\leq_{P})
and
(Q,\leq_{Q})
, we say that a map
\varphi:P\to Q
is a
co-homomorphism
when for all
p,p^{\prime}
in
P
we have

\varphi(p)\leq_{Q}\varphi(p^{\prime})
implies
p\leq_{P}p^{\prime}
. In such a case, if
Q
is a
wqo
(resp. a
bqo
) then so is
P
(
[
CP20
, Lemma 3.10]
).

Recall also that
wqo
s (resp.
bqo
s) are stable by finite products, and that all well-orders are
bqo
s (
[
CP20
, Example 3.9 and Proposition 3.14]
).

Proposition 2.33
.

Continuous reducibility is a
bqo
on any finitely generated set of functions.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be generated by a finite set of functions
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{0},\ldots,f_{k}\}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
For all
f\in\mathcal{C}
there is an
s_{f}\in\mathbb{N}^{k+1}
such that
f\equiv\bigoplus_{i\leq k}s_{f}(i)f_{i}
.
But then if
f,g
in
\mathcal{C}
satisfy
s_{f}(i)\leq s_{g}(i)
for all
i\leq k
, then
f\leq g
by
Fact

2.31
,
so
f\mapsto s_{f}
is a co-homomorphism from
\mathcal{C}

to the finite product of well-orders
\mathbb{N}^{k+1}
, which makes
\mathcal{C}
a
bqo
.
∎

The previous proposition motivates our strategy underlined in
Theorem

1.5
.
To prove that each level
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha}
is a
bqo
, we actually describe a finite set of functions that generates it in the above sense.
The level
\mathsf{Scat}_{0}
consists only of the empty function, so
the first interesting level is
\mathsf{Scat}_{1}
, that of locally constant functions, to which we now turn.

We use several time in this paper the following well-known exercise.

Fact 2.34
.

Any infinite metrizable space contains an infinite discrete subspace.

We close this section with the analysis of the locally constant functions up to continuous equivalence.

Proposition 2.35
.

The class of locally constant functions
f:X\to Y
from a second countable space
X
to a metrizable space
Y
is (finitely) generated by
8
8

8

For notational simplicity we adopt the convention that
1=\{(0)^{\infty}\}
.

\{\id_{1},\id_{\mathbb{N}}\}
. In fact,
f\equiv\id_{I}
where
I
is a discrete set with cardinality
|\im f|
.

Proof.

Observe first that any constant function on a non-empty topological space is continuously equivalent to
\id_{1}
.
Now if
f
is locally constant, the sets
A_{y}=f^{-1}(\{y\})
for
y\in\im(f)
form a clopen partition of
\dom(f)
such that
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{y}}\equiv\id_{1}
for all
y\in\im f
.
By
Proposition

2.25
, we get
f\leq\bigoplus_{y\in\im f}\id_{1}
and note that since
X
is second countable,
\im f
is countable.

If
\im f
is finite of cardinality
n
, then it is discrete and so using
Proposition

2.27
with
B_{y}=\{y\}
for
y\in\im f
,
we get
n\id_{1}\leq f
. Therefore
f\equiv n\id_{1}
in this case.

If
\im f
is infinite, being metrizable it contains a countably infinite discrete subspace by
Fact

2.34
,
so we similarly obtain
\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\id_{1}\leq f
using
Proposition

2.27
.
Therefore in this case
f\equiv\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\id_{1}\equiv\id_{\mathbb{N}}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

In the sequel we introduce the necessary concepts which allows us to generalize the previous simple proof to all successor levels of
\mathsf{Scat}
.
Crucially, we define
centered functions
which generalizes constant functions in
Section

4
.
Similarly to the unique image of a constant function, centered functions admit a special point in their image, their
cocenter
.
While there is, up to equivalence, only one constant function, we will show that there are, up to equivalence, only finitely many centered functions of a given
\CB
-rank

Theorem

4.10
.
We eventually prove in
Theorem

7.1
that every function
f
is locally centered.

In the next section, we study the general structure of
\mathsf{Scat}
which mainly establishes that the structure of limit levels is as simple as one could hope for and ensures that finite generation of all levels indeed entail that
\mathsf{Scat}
is
bqo
.

3
Pointed Gluing and the General Structure

After
Proposition

2.33
, we now need to argue that
Theorem

1.5
implies
Theorem

1.2
.
The key to do so is to develop the technology to increase the
\CB
-rank
9
9

9

As we have seen, gluing functions does not.
, which is the objective of this section.
This is the motivation for introducing the Pointed Gluing operation from
[
Car13b
]
, and study its binding conditions in the same way we did for the Gluing operation.
This will be central in
Sections

5
and
6
,
and gives the generalization to
\mathsf{Scat}
of
[
Car13b
, Theorem 5.2]
that we obtain at the end of the present section.

Note that the pointed gluing operation is implicitly used in the theory of Wadge degrees since Wadge’s thesis
[
Wad83
]
, sometimes with slight variations (see for instance
[
Sel04
, Section 5]
).
It has been used explicitly in
[
CMRS23
, Subsection 2.6]

Recall that for a sequence
s\in\mathbb{N}^{<\mathbb{N}}
we denote by
s^{n}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
the concatenation of
s
with itself
n
-many times.
We make the convention that
s^{0}
is the empty sequence and
s^{\infty}
is the infinite concatenation of
s
with itself.

Definition 3.1
.

Given a sequence
(F_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of subsets of
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
,
we call
pointed gluing
of
(F_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
the set
\pgl(F_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}=\{(0)^{\infty}\}\cup\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}(0)^{i}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}F_{i}
.

Given a sequence of functions
(f_{i}:A_{i}\rightarrow B_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
,
we call
pointed gluing
of
(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, the map

\displaystyle\pgl_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f_{i}:\pgl_{i\in\mathbb{N}}A_{i}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\pgl_{i\in\mathbb{N}}B_{i}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\longmapsto\begin{cases}(0)^{i}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}f_{i}(x^{\prime})&\mbox{if }x=(0)^{i}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}x^{\prime}\\
(0)^{\infty}&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Fact 3.2
.

The pointed gluing operation preserves countability, closure, compactness, Polishness of sets; continuity, injectivity, surjectivity, scatteredness of functions.

It commutes with the identity natural transformation, i.e.
\id_{\pgl_{i}X_{i}}=\pgl_{i}\id_{X_{i}}
.

The point
(0)^{\infty}
is a continuity point of
\pgl(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
for any sequence of functions
(f_{i})_{i}
in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
.

The following proposition generalizes
[
Car13b
, Proposition 3.1]
.

A sequence
(\alpha_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of ordinals is called
regular
when for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a natural
n>m
such that
\alpha_{m}\leq\alpha_{n}
, or
equivalently, when
\sup_{n}(\alpha_{n}+1)=\limsup_{n}(\alpha_{n}+1)
.

Recall that if
f
is simple with
\CB(f)=\alpha+1
the distinguished point of
f
is the unique
y\in\im(f)
satisfying
\{y\}=f(\CB_{\alpha}(f))
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
f=\pgl_{n\in\mathbb{N}}f_{n}
for some sequence
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of scattered functions in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
.
If
(\CB(f_{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is regular and
\alpha=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}(\CB(f_{n}))
, then
\CB_{\alpha}(f)=\{(0)^{\infty}\}
.
In particular,
f
is simple of distinguished point
(0)^{\infty}
, and
\CB(f)=\alpha+1
.

Proof.

Since
f_{n}\equiv f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{N_{(0)^{n}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)}}
, we have
\CB(f_{n})=\CB(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{N_{(0)^{n}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)}})
by
Corollary

2.17
.
If
\beta<\alpha
then by regularity
\CB_{\beta}(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{N_{(0)^{n}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)}})=\CB_{\beta}(f)\cap N_{(0)^{n}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)}
is non-empty for infinitely many
n
,
which implies that
(0)^{\infty}\in\CB_{\beta+1}(f)
. Therefore
(0)^{\infty}\in\CB_{\alpha}(f)
.
Since
\CB_{\alpha}(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{N_{(0)^{n}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)}})
is empty for all
n
, it follows that
\CB_{\alpha}(f)=\{(0)^{\infty}\}
.
∎

Fact 3.4
.

Given a sequence
(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
,
we have
\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f_{i}\leq\pgl_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f_{i}
.

Proof.

Use
Proposition

2.27
with
f=\pgl_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f_{i}
and
B_{i}=N_{(0)^{i}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)}
for
{i\in\mathbb{N}}
.
∎

We write
x_{n}\to x
to say that a sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of points in a topological space converges to a point
x
.
We recall the following useful sufficient criterion for continuity (
[
Car13b
, Claim 3.2]
).

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
A
and
B
be metrizable spaces and
f:A\rightarrow B
.
Suppose that
U
is an open subset of
A
satisfying:

(i)

f
is continuous both on
U
and on
A\setminus U
, and

(ii)

for all
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
U
, if
x_{n}\rightarrow x\in A\setminus U
then
f(x_{n})\rightarrow f(x)
.

Then
f
is continuous.

Proof.

Since
A
is metrizable, we prove sequential continuity at every point
x\in A
. Take a sequence
x_{n}\rightarrow x
, we need to prove that
f(x_{n})\rightarrow f(x)
.
Observe first that if
x\in U
then a tail of
(x_{n})_{n}
is actually included in the open set
U
, so using continuity of
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}
we have
f(x_{n})\rightarrow f(x)
.

So suppose that
x\notin U
, and partition
\mathbb{N}
in two pieces:
I=\{n\in\mathbb{N}\mid x_{n}\in U\}
and
J=\mathbb{N}\setminus I
.
If
I
is finite, then continuity of
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A\setminus U}
yields
f(x_{n})\rightarrow f(x)
.
Otherwise, let
V\ni f(x)
be an open set, the second hypothesis gives
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\in I
, if
n>N
then
f(x_{n})\in V
.
If
J
is finite, fix
m=\max\{N,\max J\}
to get
f(x_{n})\in V
for all natural numbers
n>m
.
If
J
is infinite, use continuity of
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A\setminus U}
to get
N^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
f(x_{n})\in V
for all
n\in J
with
n>N^{\prime}
.
Therefore
f(x_{n})\in V
for all natural numbers
n>\max\{N,N^{\prime}\}
, which proves that
f(x_{n})\rightarrow f(x)
in this case too.
∎

3.1
Pointed gluing as an upper bound

We say that a sequence of functions
(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

is reducible by (finite) pieces to
a sequence
(g_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of functions
if there is a family
(I_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of pairwise disjoint finite sets of
\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have
f_{n}\leq\bigoplus_{i\in I_{n}}g_{i}
.

For our analysis of the pointed gluing, we introduce the following way of partitioning a function at a given point of its codomain.

Definition 3.6
.

For
B\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
,
y\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the
n
-th ray of
B
at
y
is the clopen subset of
B
defined by

B^{(y,n)}:=\{x\in B\mid y\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{n}\sqsubseteq x\text{ and }y\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{n+1}\not\sqsubseteq x\}.

In such a case, if
f:A\to B
is a function, the
n
-th ray of
f
at
y
is the function
f^{(y,n)}:=f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{B^{(y,n)}}
.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
be continuous and
(g_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
.
If
y\in B
and
(f^{(y,j)})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is reducible by pieces to
(g_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
,
then
f\leq\pgl_{i\in\mathbb{N}}g_{i}
.

Proof.

Let
f:A\to B
in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
be continuous and note that if
y\notin\im f
then we can actually conclude by
Proposition

2.25
, so suppose that
y\in\im f
.
Fix a family
(I_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
witnessing the reduction by pieces, and for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
a reduction
(\sigma_{n},\tau_{n})
from
f^{(y,n)}
to
\bigoplus_{i\in I_{n}}g_{i}
.
Observe that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\sigma_{n}(x)=(i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}x^{\prime}
for some
i\in I_{n}
and
x^{\prime}\in\dom g_{i}
.
Since
f
is continuous and the sets
B^{(y,n)}
are clopen, the sets
A_{n}:=\dom(f^{(y,n)})=\dom(\sigma_{n})
form a clopen partition of
A\setminus f^{-1}(\{y\})
,
so
\tilde{\sigma}=\bigsqcup_{n}\sigma_{n}
is a well-defined function on
A\setminus f^{-1}(\{y\})
.
Define
\sigma
as follows:
\sigma(x)=(0)^{\infty}
if
f(x)=y
, and
\sigma(x)=(0)^{i}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}x^{\prime}
when
\tilde{\sigma}(x)=(i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}x^{\prime}
.

Let
I=\bigsqcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}I_{n}
and
\eta:I\to\mathbb{N}
be such that
\eta(i)
is the unique
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
i\in I_{n}
.
Define now
\tau
as follows:
\tau((0)^{\infty})=y
, and
\tau(x)=\tau_{\eta(i)}((i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}x^{\prime})
when
x=(0)^{i}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}x^{\prime}
,
(i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}x^{\prime}\in\im\tau_{\eta(i)}
and
i\in I
.

As the sets
I_{n}
are pairwise disjoint,
\eta
is injective and
\tau
is well-defined.
Note that, by construction,
\sigma
is defined on all of
A
and
f=\tau(\pgl_{i\in\mathbb{N}}g_{i})\sigma
.
So it suffices to show that
\sigma
and
\tau
are continuous.

We now use
Lemma

3.5
with the open set
U=\dom f\setminus f^{-1}(\{y\})=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}A_{n}
to obtain continuity of
\sigma
:
as
\tilde{\sigma}
is continuous by
Lemma

2.22
so is
\sigma
on
U
, hence it is enough to check the second condition.
Towards that goal, suppose that a sequence
(x_{n})_{n}
in
\dom f
with
f(x_{n})\neq y
converges to
x
with
f(x)=y
. To show that
\sigma(x_{n})\rightarrow(0)^{\infty}
, we prove that given any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\sigma(x_{n})\in N_{(0)^{m}}
for sufficiently large
n
.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
j_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
f(x_{n})\in B^{(y,j_{n})}
. By definition of
\sigma
we have
\sigma(x_{n})\in N_{(0)^{i}}
for some
i\in I_{j_{n}}
.
Now, as the sets
(I_{j_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are nonempty and pairwise disjoint, we have
\min I_{j_{n}}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
.
So there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq N
we have
\min I_{j_{n}}\geq m
and therefore
\sigma(x_{n})\in N_{(0)^{m}}
.

We proceed similarly for
\tau
using this time
U=\dom\tau\setminus\{(0)^{\infty}\}
. Note that
\tau
is continuous on
U
by
Lemma

2.22
.
Now take a sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
U
converging to
(0)^{\infty}
, we prove that
\tau(x_{n})\rightarrow y
. Given
m\in\mathbb{N}
, set
M=\max(\bigcup_{j\leq m}I_{j})
. Since
x_{n}\rightarrow(0)^{\infty}
there is
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
x_{n}\in N_{(0)^{M+1}}
for all
n>N
.
Therefore by definition of
\tau
, for all
n>N
we have
\tau(x_{n})\in\bigcup_{j>m}\im(\tau_{j})
, which means that
\tau(x_{n})\in N_{y\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{m}}
.
We thus get
\tau(x_{n})\rightarrow y
, so
\tau
is continuous by
Lemma

3.5
.
∎

Proposition

3.7
gives the following rough – yet very useful – way to upper bound a continuous function using the pointed gluing operation.

Corollary 3.8
.

Let
f:A\to B
be a continuous function in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
.
Then
f\leq\pgl_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f^{(y,i)}
for all
y\in B
.

The following corollary states another relationship of the pointed gluing with the finite gluing:

Corollary 3.9
.

Let
(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be continuous in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
.
For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\pgl_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f_{i}\equiv(\bigoplus_{i<n}f_{i})\oplus\pgl_{i\geq n}f_{i}
.

Proof.

Using
Proposition

3.7
with
y=(0)^{\infty}
, we get
(\bigoplus_{i<n}f_{i})\oplus\pgl_{i\geq n}f_{i}\leq\pgl_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f_{i}
.
Moreover, we see that
\pgl_{i}f_{i}\leq(\bigoplus_{i<n}f_{i})\oplus\pgl_{i\geq n}f_{i}
using
Proposition

2.27
with the clopen partition
\{N_{(0)^{i}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)}\mid i<n\}\cup\{N_{(0)^{n}}\}
.
∎

Recall that we saw in
Proposition

3.3
that the pointed gluing of a sequence with regular
\CB
-ranks is simple. Given a simple function
f
with distinguished point
y
, we have
f\leq\pgl_{n\in\mathbb{N}}f^{(y,n)}
by
Corollary

3.8
. While it is not true in general (see
Proposition

7.8
) that
f\equiv\pgl_{n\in\mathbb{N}}f^{(y,n)}
, we have the following:

Proposition 3.10
.

If
f\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
is scattered of
\CB
-rank
\alpha+1
is simple of distinguished point
y
, then the sequence
(\CB(f^{(y,n)}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is regular and its supremum is
\alpha
.

Proof.

Take a simple
f:A\to B
is in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha+1}
for some
\alpha<\omega_{1}
with distinguished point
y
, and set
\alpha_{i}=\CB(f^{(y,i)})
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
By simplicity,
\CB_{\alpha}(f)\subseteq f^{-1}(\{y\})
and so for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\CB_{\alpha}(f^{(y,i)})=\CB_{\alpha}(f)\cap f^{-1}(B^{(y,i)})=\emptyset
,
which implies
\alpha_{i}\leq\alpha
. Hence
\sup_{i}\alpha_{i}\leq\alpha
.
To get the other inequality as well as regularity,
we need to show that for all
\beta<\alpha
and for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
there is an
n>m
such that
\beta<\alpha_{n}
.

Suppose towards a contradiction that for some
\beta<\alpha
for all
n>m
we have
\alpha_{n}\leq\beta
. Setting
g:=f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\dom(f)\setminus(\bigcup_{i\leq m}\dom(f^{(y,i)}))}

we observe that
\CB_{\beta}(g)\subseteq f^{-1}(\{y\})
because
\CB_{\beta}(g^{(y,n)})
is always empty: for
n\leq m
it is by definition of
g
, and for

n>m
this follows from our assumption that
g^{(y,n)}=f^{(y,n)}
has
\CB
-rank
\alpha_{n}\leq\beta
using
Fact

2.14

(i)
.
Thus
g\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\CB_{\beta}(g)}
is either empty or constant, in any case
\CB(g)\leq\beta+1\leq\alpha
.
Observe finally that
f=g\sqcup(\bigsqcup_{i\leq m}f^{(y,i)})
is a disjoint union of functions of
\CB
-rank
\leq\alpha
, so by
Corollary

2.16

we get
\CB(f)\leq\alpha<\alpha+1
, a contradiction.
∎

3.2
Maximum functions

As an important consequence of the “upper bound” criterion stated in
Proposition

3.7
, we identify functions which are maximum among functions in
\mathsf{Scat}
with
\CB
-rank at most
\alpha
.

Recall that
\omega f
denotes the gluing of the constant sequence with value
f
. Similarly, we write
\pgl f
for the pointed gluing of the constant sequence with value
f
.

Definition 3.11
.

We define by induction on
\alpha
a function
\mathsf{l}_{\alpha}
in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha}
and a function
\mathsf{k}_{\alpha+1}
in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha+1}
.
Set
\mathsf{l}_{0}=\emptyset
and
\mathsf{k}_{1}=\pgl\emptyset
.
Suppose that for all
\beta<\alpha
