the functions
\mathsf{l}_{\beta}
and
\mathsf{k}_{\beta+1}
are defined.

If
\alpha=\beta+1
for some
\beta<\alpha
, then set
\mathsf{k}_{\alpha+1}=\pgl\mathsf{k}_{\alpha}
and
\mathsf{l}_{\alpha}=\omega\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\beta}
.

Otherwise, that is if
\alpha
is limit, then fix a sequence
(\alpha_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
cofinal in
\alpha
and set
\mathsf{k}_{\alpha+1}=\pgl_{n}\mathsf{k}_{\alpha_{n}+1}
.
Fix as well an enumeration
(\beta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\alpha
and set
\mathsf{l}_{\alpha}=\bigoplus_{n}\mathsf{l}_{\beta_{n}}
.

Note that
\mathsf{k}_{1}\equiv\id_{1}
and
\mathsf{l}_{1}\equiv\id_{\mathbb{N}}
. For
\alpha
limit, up to continuous equivalence, the definition of these functions does not depend on the choice of the sequences
(\alpha_{n})_{n}
and
(\beta_{n})_{n}
.
For
\mathsf{k}_{\alpha+1}
, it follows from a classical well-known result of Mazurkiewicz and Sierpinskì
[
MS20
, Théorème 1]

- we will get an alternative proof of that later with
Theorem

3.16
;
as for
\mathsf{l}_{\alpha}
, by
Propositions

2.25
and
2.27
,
different choices of enumerations
(\beta_{n})_{n}
produce continuously equivalent functions
\mathsf{l}_{\alpha}
.

Observe that since the Gluing and Pointed Gluing operations commute with the identity natural transformation and
\mathsf{k}_{1}=\id_{\{(0)^{\infty}\}}
, the functions from
Definition

3.11
are identity functions on their domains.
The spaces
\dom\mathsf{k}_{\alpha}
are somewhat classical objects, they were defined exactly this way in
[
Car13b
]
.
Their properties can prove useful in other – yet related – contexts, as illustrated by
[
CMRS23
, Proposition 3.12]
.
We will see here (in
Proposition

3.15
) that each function
\mathsf{k}_{\alpha+1}
is minimum in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\geq\alpha+1}
.

The functions
\mathsf{l}_{\alpha}
were defined in
[
Car13a
, Definition 5.34]

and a version of the following result was proved for functions with Polish
0
-dimensional domain (
[
Car13a
, Proposition 5.36 and Corollary 6.6]
).

Proposition 3.12
.

For all
\alpha<\omega_{1}
:

(i)

the function
\mathsf{l}_{\alpha}
is a maximum of
\mathsf{Scat}_{\leq\alpha}
,

(ii)

the function
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\alpha}
is a maximum for simple functions in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\leq\alpha+1}
,

(iii)

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
(n+1)\mathsf{k}_{\alpha+1}
is a maximum among functions of
\CB
-type
(\alpha+1,n+1)
with compact domains.

Proof.

First notice that if
\alpha\leq\beta
, then we have
\mathsf{l}_{\alpha}\leq\mathsf{l}_{\beta}
and
\mathsf{k}_{\alpha+1}\leq\mathsf{k}_{\beta+1}
.
For
\alpha=0
, we have
\mathsf{l}_{0}=\emptyset
and
\mathsf{k}_{1}=\pgl\mathsf{l}_{0}=\id_{\{(0)^{\infty}\}}\equiv\id_{\{0\}}
, so all items follows from
Proposition

2.35
.

We prove the first two items simultaneously by strong induction on
\alpha
: suppose they both hold for all
\beta<\alpha
.
To see that
\mathsf{l}_{\alpha}
is a maximum in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\leq\alpha}
, let
f\in\mathsf{Scat}
with
\CB(f)\leq\alpha
.
By the Decomposition
Lemma

2.21
,
f
is locally simple. If
\alpha
, is limit
f=\bigsqcup_{i}f_{i}
with
f_{i}
simple and
\CB(f_{i})=\beta_{i}+1<\alpha
and so by induction hypothesis the second item ensures that
f_{i}\leq\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\beta_{i}}\leq\mathsf{l}_{\beta_{i}+1}
. If
\alpha
is successor,
f=\bigsqcup_{i}f_{i}
with
f_{i}
simple and
\CB(f_{i})=\beta+1=\alpha
and again the induction hypothesis implies that
f_{i}\leq\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\beta}
. In both, cases we get
\bigoplus_{i}f_{i}\leq\mathsf{l}_{\alpha}
and so
f\leq\mathsf{l}_{\alpha}
by
Proposition

2.25
.

Now take
f
simple in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\leq\alpha+1}
and call
y
its distinguished point.
By
Corollary

3.8
we have
f\leq\pgl_{j\in\mathbb{N}}f^{(y,j)}
, but by simplicity of
f
we also have
\CB(f^{(y,j)})\leq\alpha
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
. As
\mathsf{l}_{\alpha}
is a maximum in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha}
, we get
f^{(y,j)}\leq\mathsf{l}_{\alpha}
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
and so
f\leq\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\alpha}
by
Proposition

3.7
.

We finally prove the second point by a double induction. Suppose that for all
\beta<\alpha
, if
f\in\mathsf{Scat}
has compact domain and
\CB
-type
(\beta+1,n+1)

for some natural
n
, then
f\leq(n+1)\mathsf{k}_{\beta+1}
.

Take now
f
with compact domain and
\CB(f)=\alpha+1
. Suppose first that
f
is simple, that is
n=0
.
Then for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
the
\CB
-rank of the
f^{(y,j)}
is at most
\alpha
by
Proposition

3.10
.
Moreover, each ray has compact domain, so it is either empty or
\tp(f^{(y,j)})=(\beta_{j}+1,n_{j}+1)
for some
\beta_{j}<\alpha
and
n_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
by
Fact

2.14
.
In any case, by induction hypothesis the sequence
(f^{(y,j)})_{j}
is reducible by pieces to the sequence

(\mathsf{k}_{\alpha_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, where
(\alpha_{n})_{n}
is either constant if
\alpha
is itself successor, or cofinal in
\alpha
if it is limit.
In any case, by
Proposition

3.7
we obtain
f\leq\mathsf{k}_{\alpha+1}
, as desired.

Suppose now that
tp(f)=(\alpha+1,n+1)
for some
n>0
, then by
Corollary

2.30
we have
f\equiv\bigoplus_{i\leq n}f_{i}
with
f_{i}
simple for all
i\leq n
.
Now using the simple case, we indeed get
f\leq(n+1)\mathsf{k}_{\alpha+1}
.
∎

3.3
Pointed gluing as a lower bound and minimum functions

Given a sequence
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of subsets of a space
A
, and a point
a\in A
, we say that
(A_{n})_{n}

converges
to
a
and we write
A_{i}\rightarrow a

when for any open neighborhood
U
of
a
, there is a natural number
m
such that for all
n\geq m
we have
A_{n}\subseteq U
. For instance, in any space
B
, the sequence of rays
(B^{(y,j)})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
at some point
y
converges to
y
.

Characterizing when a pointed gluing reduces continuously to a given function appears to be considerably harder. The following rough criterion is sufficient for the sequel.

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
f:A\to B
be a function between metrizable spaces and
(g_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
.

Assume that there is a point
x\in A
and a sequence
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of clopen sets of
A
satisfying:

(i)

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
f(x)\notin\overline{f(A_{n})}
,

(ii)

The sets
f(A_{n})
, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, form a relative clopen partition,

(iii)

A_{n}\rightarrow x
,

(iv)

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
g_{n}\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{n}}
.

Then
\pgl_{n\in\mathbb{N}}g_{n}\leq f
.

Proof.

Choose for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
a reduction
(\sigma_{n},\tau_{n})
from
g_{n}
to
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{n}}
.
We define
\sigma
and
\tau
as follows: set
\sigma((0)^{\infty})=x
,
\sigma((0)^{n}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}x^{\prime})=\sigma_{n}(x^{\prime})
if
x^{\prime}\in\dom\sigma_{n}
, and
\tau(f(x))=(0)^{\infty}
,

\tau(y)=(0)^{n}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}\tau_{n}(y)
if
y\in\dom(\tau_{n})
. Note that
\tau
is well-defined since the sets
\dom(\tau_{n})\subseteq f(A_{n})
are pairwise disjoint and do not contain
f(x)
.

The pair
(\sigma,\tau)
is the desired reduction, it remains to show that it is continuous.
To see that
\sigma
is continuous, we proceed using
Lemma

3.5
with
U=\dom\sigma\setminus\{(0)^{\infty}\}
. As
\sigma
is continuous on
U
by
Lemma

2.22
, we check the second condition of
Lemma

3.5
.
Any sequence
(x_{n})_{n}
in
\dom\sigma\setminus\{(0)^{\infty}\}
converging to
(0)^{\infty}
yields by definition
a sequence
(k_{n})_{n}
in
\mathbb{N}
such that
k_{n}\to\infty
and
x_{n}\in N_{(0)^{k_{n}}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Hence
\sigma(x_{n})\in A_{k_{n}}
for all
n
by definition of
\sigma
and since
A_{n}\rightarrow x
, it follows that
\sigma(x_{n})\rightarrow x
, which proves that
\sigma
is continuous.

To prove the continuity of
\tau
we use
Lemma

3.5
again, this time with
U=\dom\tau\setminus\{f(x)\}
. As the sets
f(A_{n})
form a relative clopen partition, so do the sets
\dom\tau_{n}
, so
\tau
is continuous on
U
by
Lemma

2.22
. To prove the second condition, take a sequence
(y_{n})_{n}
in
\im(f\sigma)\setminus\{f(x)\}
such that
y_{n}\rightarrow f(x)
.
For all
n
, let
k_{n}
be the unique natural number such that
y_{n}\in f(A_{k_{n}})
. We need to show that
\tau(y_{n})\rightarrow(0)^{\infty}
, and therefore, by definition of
\tau
, it is enough to see that
k_{n}\rightarrow\infty
.
But this is indeed the case, since otherwise
(y_{n})_{n}
would admit a subsequence included in a set
f(A_{i})
for some
i\in\mathbb{N}

and therefore we would have
f(x)\in\overline{f(A_{i})}
, a contradiction.
∎

The conditions of the previous statement are quite difficult to satisfy in practice. In fact, we exclusively make use of the following specific case which relies on conditions that are simpler to verify, yet apparently much stronger.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
f:A\to B
be a continuous function in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
and
(g_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
.

Assume that for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
and all open neighborhood
U\ni x

there is a continuous reduction
(\sigma,\tau)
from
g_{i}
to
f
such that
\im(\sigma)\subseteq U
and
f(x)\notin\overline{\im(f\sigma)}
.
Then
\pgl_{i\in\mathbb{N}}g_{i}\leq f
.

In fact,
\pgl_{i\in\mathbb{N}}g_{i}\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{V}
for all clopen neighborhood
V
of
x
.

Proof.

We define by induction a sequence
(A_{n})_{n}
of clopen subsets of
A
such that the sets
f(A_{n})
are separated by open sets,
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have
f(x)\notin\overline{f(A_{n})}
,
A_{n}\subseteq N_{x\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{n}}
and there exists a continuous reduction
(\sigma_{n},\tau_{n})
from
g_{n}
to
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{n}}
.
This sequence satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

3.13
, so the result follows.

Assume that the sets
(A_{i})_{i<n}
have been defined as above.
Let
k
be such that
N_{f(x)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{k}}
is disjoint from the closed set
\bigcup_{i<n}\overline{f(A_{i})}
.
Use continuity of
f
at
x
to choose a clopen neighborhood
U
of
x
included in
N_{x\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{n}}
such that
f(U)\subseteq N_{f(x)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{k}}
.
The hypothesis guarantees the existence of a continuous reduction
(\sigma_{n},\tau_{n})
from
g_{n}
to
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}
with
f(x)\notin\overline{\im(f\sigma_{n})}
.
This allows us to choose a clopen neighborhood
W\ni f(x)
disjoint from
\overline{\im(f\sigma_{n})}
and we set
A_{n}=U\setminus f^{-1}(W)
.
The set
A_{n}\subseteq U\subseteq N_{x\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{n}}
is clopen as a difference of two clopen sets,
f(A_{n})\cap W=\emptyset
so
f(x)\notin\overline{f(A_{n})}
,

f(A_{n})\subseteq N_{f(x)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{k}}\setminus W
so
N_{f(x)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{k}}\setminus W
is an open set witnessing separation of the sets
A_{n}
.
Finally, notice that
(\sigma_{n},\tau_{n})
is actually a reduction from
g_{n}
to
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{n}}
since
\im\sigma_{n}\subseteq A_{n}
.
∎

Seeing pointed gluing as an upper bound gave us maximum functions in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\leq\alpha}
(
Proposition

3.12
); similarly the lower bound criterion gives us minimum functions in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\geq\alpha+1}
.

Proposition 3.15
.

For all
\alpha<\omega_{1}
and
f\in\mathsf{Scat}
, if
\alpha+1\leq\CB(f)
, then
\mathsf{k}_{\alpha+1}\leq f
.

Proof.

We prove by strong induction for every
\alpha<\omega_{1}
, the following statement:
\mathsf{k}_{\alpha+1}\leq f
for every simple function
f\in\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha+1}
. To see that this is enough, we remark that if this statement holds for all
\beta<\alpha
, then actually for all
f\in\mathsf{Scat}_{<\alpha}
if
\beta+1\leq\CB(f)
then
\mathsf{k}_{\beta+1}\leq f
.

To prove this remark, suppose that
f\in\mathsf{Scat}
with
\CB(f)<\alpha
. Since
f
is locally simple by the Decomposition
Lemma

2.21
, we can write
f=\bigsqcup_{i\in I}f_{i}
with
f_{i}
simple, and
\sup_{i\in I}\CB(f_{i})=\CB(f)
by
Corollary

2.16
. So if
\beta+1\leq\CB(f)
,
\beta+1\leq\CB(f_{i})=\beta_{i}+1
for some
i\in I
and we have
\mathsf{k}_{\beta+1}\leq\mathsf{k}_{\beta_{i}+1}\leq f_{i}\leq f
, where
\mathsf{k}_{\beta_{i}+1}\leq f_{i}
is granted by the statement, which proves the remark.

For
\alpha=0
, note that
\mathsf{k}_{1}
continuously reduces to any nonempty function.
So suppose that
\alpha>0
and that the statement holds for every
\beta<\alpha
.

Take a simple function
f\in\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha+1}
and let
y\in\im f
be the distinguished point of
f
.
Seeing that
\mathsf{k}_{\alpha+1}
is a pointed gluing, we seek to apply
Proposition

3.14
for some point
x\in\CB_{\alpha}(f)
. Let
U\ni x
be any open set of
\dom f
.
Notice first that as
x\in\CB_{\alpha}(f)
and
x\in U
we get that
f_{U}=f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}
is simple and
\CB(f_{U})=\alpha+1
by
Fact

2.14

(i)
.
Note that by
Proposition

3.10
the sequence
(\CB(f_{U}^{(y,n)}))_{n}
is regular of supremum
\alpha
. If
\alpha=\beta+1
is successor, then there exists
N
such that
\alpha=\CB(f_{U}^{(y,N)})
. By the induction hypothesis combined with our first remark, we get
\mathsf{k}_{\alpha}\leq f_{U}^{(y,N)}
.
Notice that if
(\sigma,\tau)
witnesses this reduction then both
\im\sigma\subseteq U
and
\overline{\im f\sigma}\subseteq B^{(y,N)}
hold. This ensures that
\mathsf{k}_{\alpha+1}=\pgl\mathsf{k}_{\alpha}\leq f
by
Proposition

3.14
.

If
\alpha
is limit, for all
\beta<\alpha
there exists
N
such that
\beta+1\leq\CB(f_{U}^{(y,N)})<\alpha
. So by the induction hypothesis combined with our first remark, we get
\mathsf{k}_{\beta+1}\leq f_{U}^{(y,N)}
.
So similarly as in the successor case, we get
\mathsf{k}_{\alpha+1}=\pgl_{k}\mathsf{k}_{\beta_{k}+1}\leq f
, for
(\beta_{k})_{k}
cofinal in
\alpha
.
∎

3.4
General Structure

We can now complete the analysis of all functions in
\mathsf{Scat}
with compact domain, thus generalizing
[
Car13b
, Theorem 4.2]
.

Theorem 3.16
.

Assume that
f
and
g
are two functions in
\mathsf{Scat}
with a compact domain, then
f
continuously reduces to
g
if and only if
tp(f)\leq_{lex}tp(g)
.
More specifically
f\equiv(n+1)\mathsf{k}_{\alpha+1}
, where
tp(f)=(\alpha+1,n+1)
.

In particular, continuous reducibility is a pre-well-order
10
10

10

A
pre-well-order
is a linear
wqo
(for more on this see
[
Kec95
, Section 34.A]
).

of length
\omega_{1}
on functions in
\mathsf{Scat}
with compact domain.

Proof.

By definition for
\alpha<\beta
we have
\mathsf{k}_{\alpha+1}\leq\mathsf{k}_{\beta+1}
and since
\CB(\mathsf{k}_{\alpha+1})=\alpha+1<\beta+1=\CB(\mathsf{k}_{\beta+1})

we get
\mathsf{k}_{\beta+1}\not\leq\mathsf{k}_{\alpha+1}
, so the functions
\mathsf{k}_{\alpha+1}
for
\alpha<\omega_{1}
form a well-order of length
\omega_{1}
.

Therefore by
Fact

2.31
it is enough to prove that for
f\in\mathsf{Scat}
with compact domain then
f\equiv(n+1)\mathsf{k}_{\alpha+1}
, where
tp(f)=(\alpha+1,n+1)
.
Now by
Corollary

2.30
we only have to show it for simple functions, which follows from
Propositions

3.12
and
3.15
.
∎

In particular we obtain the promised alternative proof that the definition of
\mathsf{k}_{\alpha+1}
does not depend on the choice of the cofinal sequence when
\alpha
is limit.

The results on maximum and minimum functions can be leveraged to obtain important information about the structure of continuous reducibility on the whole of
\mathsf{Scat}
.
As announced, this generalizes
[
Car13b
, Theorem 5.2]
and we (still) refer to it as the General Structure Theorem.

\mathsf{l}_{0}
\mathsf{k}_{1}
\mathsf{l}_{1}
\mathsf{Scat}_{1}
\mathsf{l}_{2}
\mathsf{k}_{2}
\mathsf{Scat}_{2}
\mathsf{l}_{3}
\mathsf{k}_{3}
\mathsf{Scat}_{3}
\mathsf{l}_{4}
\mathsf{k}_{4}
\mathsf{Scat}_{4}
\mathsf{l}_{\omega}
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda+1}
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda+1}
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda+2}
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+2}
\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda+2}
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda+3}
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+3}
\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda+3}
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda+\omega}
\cdots

Figure 1:
The general structure of continuous reducibility on
\mathsf{Scat}
.

Theorem 3.17

(General Structure)
.

For all functions
f
and
g
in
\mathsf{Scat}
, and all ordinals
\lambda<\omega_{1}
limit or null, we have:

(i)

If
\CB(f)\leq\CB(g)=\lambda
, then
f\leq g
.

(ii)

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\CB(f)=\lambda+n
and
\lambda+2n+1\leq\CB(g)
imply
f\leq g
.

In particular,
2\CB(f)<\CB(g)
implies
f\leq g
.

Proof.

Proceed by induction on
\lambda<\omega_{1}
. Suppose that the theorem is proven for all
\alpha<\lambda
with
\lambda
limit or null.

We first prove the second item. As
f\leq\mathsf{l}_{\lambda+n}
by
Proposition

3.12
and
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+2n+1}\leq g
by
Proposition

3.15
, it is enough to prove that

\mathsf{l}_{\lambda+n}\leq\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+2n+1}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and to do so we proceed by induction on
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
For
n=0
, if
\lambda=0
then
\mathsf{l}_{0}=\emptyset\leq\mathsf{k}_{1}
, so we can suppose that
\lambda\neq 0
.
Take
(\alpha_{n})_{n}
cofinal in
\lambda
and
(\beta_{n})_{n}
an enumeration of
\lambda
then, by induction hypothesis, for some injection
p:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
we have

\mathsf{l}_{\alpha_{n}}\leq\mathsf{k}_{\beta_{p(n)}+1}
so by
Propositions

2.25
and
3.4
we get

\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\equiv\bigoplus_{n}\mathsf{l}_{\alpha_{n}}\leq\bigoplus_{n}\mathsf{k}_{\beta_{n}+1}\leq\pgl_{n}\mathsf{k}_{\beta_{n}+1}\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}.

If now
\alpha=\lambda+(n+1)
then using the induction hypothesis,
Propositions

3.7
and
3.4
we see that

\mathsf{l}_{\alpha}\equiv\omega\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda+n}\leq\pgl\pgl\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+2n+1}\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+2n+3}=\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+2(n+1)+1}
.

To see the first item, take a function
g:A\to B
of
\CB
-rank
\lambda
. By
Proposition

3.12

f\leq\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
for any
f\in\mathsf{Scat}_{\leq\alpha}
, so it is enough to show that if
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq g
.
If
\lambda=0
then
g
is the empty function, so suppose that
\lambda
is limit.

We are going to find a sequence
(s_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{<\mathbb{N}}
of finite sequences pairwise incomparable for the prefix relation such that the sequence
(\CB(g\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{N_{s_{n}}}))_{n}

is either constant equal to
\lambda
or strictly below
\lambda
and cofinal in
\lambda
. Thanks to the induction hypothesis, an application of
Proposition

2.27

to the (pairwise disjoint) clopen sets
(N_{s_{n}})_{n}
allows then to conclude.

Consider the tree
T=\{s\in\mathbb{N}^{<\mathbb{N}}\mid\CB(g\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{N_{s}})=\lambda\}
, notice that
T\neq\emptyset
because it contains at least the empty sequence.
If
[T]
is infinite then an application of
Fact

2.34
allows to find the desired sequence, so we can suppose that
[T]
is finite.
Let
F
be the set of
\sqsubset
-minimal elements of
\mathbb{N}^{<\mathbb{N}}\setminus T
. Then
\{\CB(g\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{N_{s}})\mid s\in F\}
is a subset of
\lambda
and we claim that it is cofinal in
\lambda
, which allows us to find the desired sequence. Towards a contradiction assume that for some
\beta<\lambda
we have
\CB(g\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{N_{s}})<\beta
for all
s\in F
. Then, by
Fact

2.14

(i)
,
\CB_{\beta}(g)\cap g^{-1}(N_{s})=\emptyset
for all
s\in F
and so
\CB_{\beta}(g)\subseteq g^{-1}([T])
. But as
[T]
is finite, we have
\CB_{\beta+1}(g)=
and so
\CB(g)\leq\beta+1
, a contradiction.
∎

Remark 3.18
.

Note that we actually have
\mathsf{l}_{n}\leq\mathsf{k}_{2n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and so the General Structure
Theorem

3.17
for functions of finite
\CB
-ranks is slightly stronger: for every
f,g
with finite
\CB
-rank,
2\CB(f)\leq\CB(g)
implies
f\leq g
.

As a consequence we can finally prove that
Theorem

1.5
implies
Theorems

1.2
and
1.4
.
When a
bqo
is also a partial order we call it a
better partial order
.
We will use that a sum of
bqo
indexed by a better partial order is itself still a
bqo
(
[
Sim85
, (9.14)]
).
The following result has the exact same proof as
[
Car13b
, Theorem 5.4]
.

Proposition 3.19
.

If
\mathsf{Scat}_{\beta}
is
bqo
for all
\beta<\alpha
, then
\mathsf{Scat}_{<\alpha}
is
bqo
.
In particular,
Theorem

1.5
implies
Theorems

1.2
and
1.4
.

Proof.

Consider the partial order
(\mathbb{N},\leq^{\bullet})
, where

m\leq^{\bullet}n\Longleftrightarrow m=n\text{ or }2m<n.

It is immediate to see that
(\mathbb{N},\leq^{\bullet})
is a partial order and a
wqo
and it is
bqo
by
[
CP14
, Example 1.6]
.
Similarly, we define a partial order
\leq^{\bullet}
on
\omega_{1}
by
\alpha_{0}\leq^{\bullet}\alpha_{1}
if and only if
\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{1}
or
2\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}
. As any ordinal
\alpha
can be uniquely written as
\alpha=\lambda+n
with
\lambda
limit or null and
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
2\alpha=\lambda+2n
, we have:

\lambda_{0}+n_{0}\leq^{\bullet}\lambda_{1}+n_{1}\Longleftrightarrow\lambda_{0}<\lambda_{1}\mbox{ or }\bigl(\lambda_{0}=\lambda_{1}\mbox{ and }n_{0}\leq^{\bullet}n_{1}\bigr).

As this partial order is the sum of the
bqo

(\mathbb{N},\leq^{\bullet})
along the well-ordered set of countable limit ordinals, it is
bqo
.
Finally, consider the sum
S_{<\alpha}=\sum_{\beta<\alpha}\mathsf{Scat}_{\beta}
of the levels
\mathsf{Scat}_{\beta}
, quasi-ordered by continuous reducibility, along the better partial order
\leq^{\bullet}
. By our hypothesis,
\mathsf{Scat}_{\beta}
is
bqo
for all
\beta<\alpha
, so
S_{<\alpha}
is
bqo
too.

Now observe that by the General Structure
Theorem

3.17
, the mapping
h:\mathsf{Scat}_{<\alpha}\to S_{<\alpha}
given by
h(f)=(\CB(f),f)
is a co-homomorphism. Namely, we have
f\leq g
whenever
h(f)\leq h(g)
in
S
, which by definition is equivalent to

\CB(f)<^{\bullet}\CB(g)\text{ or }(\CB(f)=\CB(g)\text{ and }f\leq g).

Therefore, it follows that continuous reducibility is
bqo
on
\mathsf{Scat}_{<\alpha}
.

In particular, assuming
Theorem

1.5
holds, then
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha}
is
bqo
for all
\alpha<\omega_{1}
by
Proposition

2.33
. So
\mathsf{Scat}
is
bqo
(
Theorem

1.4
) and hence
Theorem

1.2
follows by
Theorem

2.13
.

∎

The General Structure
Theorem

3.17
has many interesting consequences, we point out two of them that will reveal useful in the sequel.

Corollary 3.20
.

Let
\lambda
be a limit ordinal.

(i)

If
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is in
\mathsf{Scat}_{<\lambda}
, then
\pgl_{n}f_{n}\leq\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
,
and if moreover
(\CB(f_{n}))_{n}
is regular and
\sup_{n}(\CB(f_{n}))=\lambda
, then
\pgl_{n}(f_{n})\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
.

(ii)

If
\CB(f)\geq\lambda+2
, then
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq f
,

Proof.

Fix any increasing cofinal sequence
(\alpha_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\lambda
. There is an increasing sequence
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
such that
2\CB(f_{n})\leq\alpha_{k_{n}}
for all
n
.
So by
Theorem

3.17
we have
f_{n}\leq\mathsf{k}_{\alpha_{k_{n}}+1}
and in turn
\pgl_{n}f_{n}\leq\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
by
Proposition

3.7
.
If moreover
(\CB(f_{n}))_{n}
is regular and
\sup_{n}(\CB(f_{n}))=\lambda
, then by
Proposition

3.3

\CB(\pgl_{n}f_{n})=\lambda+1
,
so by
Proposition

3.15

\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
, being a minimum, it reduces to
\pgl_{n}f_{n}
.

For the second item, by
Theorem

3.17
we have
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
and so
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq\pgl\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}=\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+2}
. Since
\CB(f)\geq\lambda+2
, we have
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+2}\leq f
by
Proposition

3.15
and so
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq f
, as desired.

For the last point, suppose towards a contradiction that they are equivalent and let
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}=\pgl_{n}\mathsf{k}_{\alpha_{n}+1}
for
(\alpha_{n})_{n}
cofinal in
\lambda
. By
Proposition

4.5
, it follows that
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq\bigoplus_{n<M}\mathsf{k}_{\alpha_{n}+1}
for some
M\in\mathbb{N}
, but then
\CB(\mathsf{l}_{\lambda})=\lambda\leq\sup_{n<M}(\alpha_{n}+1)<\lambda
.
∎

Proposition

2.35
gives us the structure of
\mathsf{Scat}_{1}
(in other words, locally constant functions), and
the General Structure
Theorem

3.17
tells us that functions with same limit rank are all continuously equivalent.
The next cases are those of
\mathsf{Scat}_{2}
, and
\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda+1}
for limit
\lambda
.
Interestingly, these two cases have more in common than just being the next ones to deal with,
see
Corollaries

4.11
,
5.11
,
7.5
and
4
.

4
Centered functions

At this point in the paper, one could wonder if it is possible to use combinations of all the results shown already and analyze all functions in
\mathsf{Scat}

using their decomposition into simple functions guaranteed by
Lemma

2.21
.

As we saw in
Proposition

3.3
, the pointed gluing of a sequence of functions is simple when the corresponding sequence of
\CB
-ranks is regular. Moreover simple functions can be decomposed in a sequence of rays at the distinguished point with regular
\CB
-ranks.
However, the latter sequence may not always be regular for continuous reducibility and the pointed gluing of this sequence of rays may not be equivalent to the simple function.

Scattered functions which are equivalent to a pointed gluing of some
\leq
-regular sequence play an important role in our analysis and enjoy a natural alternative definition.
We call them centered functions and we now study them in detail.

This section culminates with the results that the
bqo
character of continuous reducibility implies that centered functions are ubiquitous.
Finally, we show that finite generation at levels of
\mathsf{Scat}
ensures a finite number of centered functions of any given rank, which is a crucial step in our proof.

4.1
Definition and characterization

Definition 4.1
.

Let
A
be a topological space. A
center
for a function
g:A\to B
is a point
x\in A
such that for every neighbourhood
U
of
x
we have
g\leq g\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}
.
We say that a function
g:A\to B
is
centered
if it admits a center.

The functions
\mathsf{k}_{\alpha+1}
and
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha<\omega_{1}
are examples of centered functions (
(0)^{\infty}
is a center). More generally
saying that a sequence
(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of functions is
regular
if
\{j\in\mathbb{N}\mid f_{i}\leq f_{j}\}
is infinitefor all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

Fact 4.2
.

If
(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a regular sequence in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
, then
(0)^{\infty}
is a center for
\pgl_{i}f_{i}
.

Proof.

Set
f:=\pgl_{i}f_{i}
.
By
Proposition

3.14
, it is enough to show that for every clopen neighbourhood
U
of
(0)^{\infty}
and every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
there exists a continuous reduction
(\sigma,\tau)
from
f_{n}
to
f
such that
\im\sigma\subseteq U
and
(0)^{\infty}\notin\overline{\im(f\sigma)}
.

So let
U\ni(0)^{\infty}
be clopen and
m\in\mathbb{N}
. By regularity of
(f_{i})_{i}
we can find
n
large enough such that
N_{(0)^{n}}\subseteq U
and
f_{m}\leq f_{n}
, which clearly induces the desired reduction.
∎

The property of being centered is stable under continuous equivalence as a result of the following fact.

Fact 4.3
.

Let
f,g
be functions between topological spaces.
Suppose that
x
is a center for
f
and that
(\sigma,\tau)
continuously reduces
f
to
g
.

(i)

For every neighborhood
U
of
\sigma(x)
, we have
f\leq g\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}
.

(ii)

If moreover
g\equiv f
, then
\sigma(x)
is a center for
g
.

(iii)

If
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
is an open covering of
\dom g
, then there exists
i\in I
with
f\leq g\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{i}}
.

Proof.

For the first item, if
U
is a neighbourhood of
\sigma(x)
, then by continuity of
\sigma
,
\sigma^{-1}(U)
is a neighbourhood of
x
.
We have
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\sigma^{-1}(U)}\leq g\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}
as witnessed by
(\sigma\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U},\tau)
and
f\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\sigma^{-1}(U)}
since
x
is a center for
f
, so
f\leq g\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}
.

If moreover
g\leq f
then by transitivity
g\leq g\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}
holds. So in this case
\sigma(x)
is a center for
g
. This gives the second point.

If now
(A_{i})_{i}
covers
\dom(g)
, then
\sigma(x)\in A_{i}
for some
i\in I
, but as
A_{i}
is open, using the first point we obtain the third one.
∎

We next establish an important interaction between the existence of centers and the property of being scattered.

Proposition 4.4
.

Suppose that
f:A\to B
is centered with
A
metrizable and
B
Hausdorff.
Then
f
is scattered if and only if all centers have the same image by
f
.

Moreover when
f
is scattered, then it is simple and any center of
f
is mapped to its distinguished point.

Proof.

Assume that
x
is a center for a function
f
, we start by making two remarks. First,
x
is
f
-isolated if and only if
f
is constant. Indeed, if
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}
is constant on a neighborhood
U
of
x
, then the whole
f
is constant since we have
f\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}
.

Second, for all ordinal
\alpha
if
\CB_{\alpha}(f)\neq\emptyset
then
x\in\CB_{\alpha}(f)
and
x
is a center for
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\CB_{\alpha}(f)}
. To see this, note that for every neighborhood
U
of
x
we have
f\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}
via some
(\sigma,\tau)
, hence
\sigma(\CB_{\alpha}(f))\subseteq\CB_{\alpha}(f))\cap U
and
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\CB_{\alpha}(f)}\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\CB_{\alpha}(f)\cap U}
by
Proposition

2.15
. So if
\CB_{\alpha}(f)
is nonempty, then so is
\CB_{\alpha}(f)\cap U
for all neighborhood
U
of
x
. It follows that
x
belongs to the closed set
\CB_{\alpha}(f)
and it is a center for
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\CB_{\alpha}(f)}
.

Now assume that
f
is scattered with
\gamma=\CB(f)
, then by
Theorem

2.7
we have
\CB_{\gamma}(f)=\emptyset
. By our second remark, all centers of
f
belong to
\CB_{\beta}(f)
for all
\beta<\gamma
. In particular, we must have
\gamma=\alpha+1
and all centers of
f
belong to
\CB_{\alpha}(f)
. Now all centers of
f
are centers of
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\CB_{\alpha}(f)}
and they are
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\CB_{\alpha}(f)}
-isolated. So by our first remark,
f
is constant on
\CB_{\alpha}(f)
, hence
f
is simple and all centers have the same image.

Finally, suppose now that
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
are centers for
f
such that
f(x_{0})\neq f(x_{1})
.
Then using our two previous remarks, we see by induction that
x_{0},x_{1}\in\CB_{\alpha}(f)
for all
\alpha
. Therefore
f
has nonempty perfect kernel and so it is not scattered by
Theorem

1.3
.
∎

Therefore, in particular, if
f\in\mathsf{Scat}
is scattered, then there is a unique point
y
in its image such that for every center
x
for
f
we have
f(x)=y
.
We call this point the
cocenter
of
f
.
The previous proposition has the following fundamental consequence which highlights a strong form of rigidity of centered functions with respect to continuous reducibility.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
f,g\in\mathsf{Scat}
be centered with cocenter
y_{f}
and
y_{g}
respectively.
Assume that
f\equiv g
and that
(\sigma,\tau)
is a continuous reduction from
f
to
g
. Then the following holds:

(i)

\tau(y_{g})=y_{f}
,

(ii)

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have
y_{g}\notin\overline{g\sigma(\dom f^{(y_{f},n)})}
,

(iii)

for all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
there is
M\geq m
such that
f^{(y_{f},n)}\leq\bigoplus_{i=m}^{M}g^{(y_{g},i)}
,

(iv)

(f^{(y_{f},n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is reducible by finite pieces to
(g^{(y_{g},n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof.

First item. Let
x
be a center for
f
. Since
f\equiv g
,
\sigma(x)
is a center for
g
by
Fact

4.3
and so
g\sigma(x)=y_{g}
by
Proposition

4.4
.
Hence
\tau(y_{g})=\tau g\sigma(x_{f})=f(x_{f})=y_{f}
as desired.

Second item: suppose not, then there is a sequence
(x_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\dom f^{(y_{f},n)}
with
g\sigma(x_{i})\to y_{g}
, so
f(x_{i})=\tau g\sigma(x_{i})\to\tau(y_{g})=y_{f}
by continuity of
\tau
.
But by definition of rays
f(x_{i})\notin N_{y_{f}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{n+1}}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, so
(f(x_{i}))_{i}
cannot converge to
y_{f}
, a contradiction.

Third item: let
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
, use the continuity of
g
to get
U\ni\sigma(x)
open with
g(U)\subseteq N_{y_{g}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{m}}
.
Since
\sigma(x)
is a center for
g
,
g\equiv g\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}
and we can choose a continuous reduction
(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
witnessing
f\leq g\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}
. By the previous item, there exists
M>m
such that
N_{y_{g}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{M+1}}
is disjoint from
\overline{g\sigma^{\prime}(\dom f^{(y_{f},n)})}
. This shows that
(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
restricts to the desired reduction.

Last item: By a recursive application of the previous item.
∎

Given a finite set
F
of functions, we define
\pgl F=\pgl(\bigoplus F)^{\infty}
, and
we write
f\leq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(F)
to say that
f\leq g
for some
g\in\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(F)
.

Corollary 4.6
.

Suppose that
f:A\to B
in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
,
G\subseteq\mathsf{Scat}
finite and
f\equiv\pgl G
. Then
f
is centered and for every open set
V\subseteq B
which excludes its cocenter, we have
f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}\leq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(G)
.

Proof.

Suppose that
f\equiv g=\pgl G
and let
(\sigma,\tau)
witness
f\leq g
. By
Fact

4.2
,
g((0)^{\infty})=(0)^{\infty}
is the cocenter of
g
, so
f
is centered by
Fact

4.3
and
y=\tau((0)^{\infty})
is the cocenter of
f
. Therefore,
(f^{(y,n)})_{n}
is reducible by finite pieces to
(g^{((0)^{\infty},n)})_{n}=(\bigoplus G)^{\infty}
by
Proposition

4.5
.
So for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we get
f^{(y,n)}\leq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(G)
and if
V\subseteq B
is open with
V\notowner y
then there exists
M\in\mathbb{N}
with
V\subseteq\bigcup_{n<M}B^{(y,n)}
. Hence, it follows that
f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}\leq\bigsqcup_{n<M}f^{(y,n)}\leq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(G)
, as desired.
∎

The following theorem ties centered functions in
\mathsf{Scat}
to the pointed gluing operation, as promised.

Theorem 4.7
.

Let
f:A\rightarrow B
be in
\mathsf{Scat}
.

(i)

If
f
is centered with cocenter
y
, then
f\equiv\pgl_{n\in\mathbb{N}}f^{(y,n)}
,

(ii)

The function
f
is centered if and only if
f\equiv\pgl_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f_{i}
for some monotone (or regular) sequence
(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of functions .

In particular, if
f
is centered with cocenter
y
, then
f
is simple with distinguished point
y
and
\CB(f)=(\sup_{n}\CB(f^{(y,n)}))+1
.

Proof.

Assume that
f\in\mathsf{Scat}
is centered with cocenter
y
.
For
(i)
, by
Corollary

3.8
we know that
f\leq\pgl_{n}f^{(y,n)}
, so it remains to prove
f\geq\pgl_{n}f^{(y,n)}
.
To do so we apply
Proposition

3.14
with
x
a center for
f
and let
U
be a neighborhood of
x
: by centeredness there is a continuous reduction
(\sigma,\tau)
from
f
to
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}
.
By
Proposition

4.5
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have
f(x)=y\notin\overline{f\sigma(f^{-1}(B^{(y,n)}))}
, so
(\sigma,\tau)
restricts to witness the required reductions
f^{(y,n)}\leq f
.

The reverse implication of
(ii)
follows from
Fact

4.2
and
Fact

4.3
.
Next we show that
f\equiv\pgl_{i}f_{i}
for some monotone sequence
(f_{i})_{i}
, establishing the forward implication.

By
Proposition

4.5
, for all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
m^{\prime}>m
such that
f^{(y,n)}\leq\bigoplus_{i=m}^{m^{\prime}}f^{(y,i)}
.
We can recursively apply this fact to get a pairwise disjoint family of finite sets
(I_{n})_{n}
such that, setting
f_{n}=\bigoplus_{i\in I_{n}}f^{(y,i)}
,
the sequence
(f_{n})_{n}
is monotone, and for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have
f^{(y,n)}\leq f_{n}
.
Moreover
(f_{n})_{n}
is clearly reducible by pieces to
(f^{(y,n)})_{n}
, so by a double application of
Proposition

3.7
, we have
\pgl_{n}f_{n}\equiv\pgl_{n}f^{(y,n)}
.

Finally, by
Proposition

4.4
the distinguished point of
f
is also its cocenter
y
and so
\CB(f)=(\sup_{n}\CB(f^{(y,n)}))+1
by
Proposition

3.10
.
∎

4.2
Centered functions and structure of continuous reducibility

Here we collect some important consequences of the structure of continuous reducibility on centered functions.

The first result shows that if continuous reducibility is
bqo
up to a certain level of
\mathsf{Scat}
, then every function at the next level is locally centered.

Recall that
bqo
is stable under (the taking of) infinite sequences (see for instance
[
CP20
, Theorem 3.18]
):
if
(Q,\leq_{Q})
is
bqo
, then the class
Q^{\mathbb{N}}
of infinite sequences of elements of
Q
is also
bqo
when ordered as follows.
Given
s,t
in
Q^{\mathbb{N}}
say
s\leq_{Q^{\mathbb{N}}}t
when there is a strictly increasing function
\iota:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
satisfying
s(x)\leq_{Q}t(\iota(x))
.

Theorem 4.8
.

For all
\alpha<\omega_{1}
, if
\mathsf{Scat}_{<\alpha}
is a
bqo
11
11

11

For those interested,
2
-
bqo
seems to be enough. The curious reader is sent to
[
CP20
, Definition 4.1]
.
,
then every function in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha}
is locally centered.

Proof.

By strong induction on
\alpha
.

\alpha=0
:
The empty function, the only element of
\mathsf{Scat}_{0}
, is trivially locally centered (although not centered).

\alpha
limit:
Let
f\in\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha}
. Since
f
has limit
\CB
-rank, it is locally in
\mathsf{Scat}_{<\alpha}
. Therefore by induction hypothesis, it is locally centered.

\alpha
successor:
Let
f:A\to B
in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha}
. By the Decomposition
Lemma

2.21
,
f
is locally simple,
so without loss of generality we can suppose that
f
is simple and that
f\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
by
Proposition

2.5
. We let
\bar{y}
be the distinguished point of
f

and write
B^{(i)}
and
f^{(i)}
for
B^{(\bar{y},i)}
and
f^{(\bar{y},i)}
respectively. Note that we have
\CB(f^{(i)})<\alpha
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
x\in A
, we show that it admits a neighbourhood
U
such that
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}
is centered.

If there exists
s\sqsubset x
such that
\CB(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{N_{s}})<\CB(f)
then, by induction hypothesis, we are done.
Otherwise, for all
s\sqsubset x
we have
\CB(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{N_{s}})=\CB(f)
.
Writing
\alpha=\beta+1
,
Fact

2.14

(i)
ensures that
N_{s}\cap\CB_{\beta}(f)\neq\emptyset
for all
s\sqsubset x
,
so
x
belongs to the closed set
\CB_{\alpha}(f)
. Hence, we have
f(x)=\bar{y}
and each function
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{N_{s}}
is simple.

Hence, for each
n
, the sequence of rays
(f^{(i)}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{N_{x\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{n}}})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
takes value in
\mathsf{Scat}_{<\alpha}
.
As
\mathsf{Scat}_{<\alpha}
is
wqo
under continuous reducibility, we can choose by induction a non-decreasing sequence
(j_{n})_{n}
in
\mathbb{N}

such that the sequence of functions
\rho_{n}=(f^{(i)}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{N_{x\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{n}}})_{i\geq j_{n}}
is regular for all
n
(
[
Peq17
, Proposition 2.9]
).
Note that
m<n
implies
\rho_{m}\geq_{(\mathsf{Scat}_{<\alpha})^{\mathbb{N}}}\rho_{n}
, since
N_{x\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{m}}\supseteq N_{x\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{n}}
implies
f^{(i)}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{N_{x\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{m}}}\geq f^{(i)}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{N_{x\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{n}}}
for all
i\geq j_{n}
,
so
(\rho_{n})_{n}
is decreasing in
(\mathsf{Scat}_{<\alpha})^{\mathbb{N}}
.
Since
\mathsf{Scat}_{<\alpha}
is
bqo
,
(\mathsf{Scat}_{<\alpha})^{\mathbb{N}}
is
wqo
and so there exists
m
such that for all
n>m
we have
\rho_{m}\equiv_{(\mathsf{Scat}_{<\alpha})^{\mathbb{N}}}\rho_{n}
.
Define
U=N_{x\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{m}}\setminus f^{-1}(\bigcup_{i<{j_{m}}}B^{(i)})
, we show that
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}\equiv\pgl\rho_{m}
.
Since
\rho_{m}
is regular,
\pgl\rho_{m}
is centered by
Theorem

4.7
and so will be
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}
by
Fact

4.3
.

The fact that
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}\leq\pgl\rho_{m}
follows from
Corollary

3.8
.
To show that
\pgl\rho_{m}\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}
using
Proposition

3.14
, it is enough to show that for every
i\geq j_{m}
and every
n>m
,
there exists
(\sigma,\tau)
that continuously reduces
f^{(i)}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{N_{x\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{m}}}
to
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}
such that

\im\sigma\subseteq N_{x\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{n}}
and
\bar{y}\notin\overline{\im f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}\sigma}
.
This is possible since
\rho_{m}\leq\rho_{n}
and so for every
i\geq j_{m}
there exists
i^{\prime}\geq j_{n}\geq j_{m}
with
f^{(i)}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{N_{x\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{m}}}\leq f^{(i^{\prime})}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{N_{x\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{n}}}
, as desired.
∎

Pointed gluing behaves rather specifically in a finitely generated class, as pointed out by the following proposition that we use repeatedly in the sequel.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
F\subseteq\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
be finite and
(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
.

(i)

If
f_{i}\leq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(F)
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\pgl_{i}f_{i}\leq\pgl F
.

(ii)

If for all
f\in F
and all
i\in\mathbb{N}
there is
j\geq i
such that
f\leq f_{j}
, then
\pgl F\leq\pgl_{i}f_{i}
.

Proof.

For both points, we build a reduction by pieces and then use
Proposition

3.7
.

For the first item: by hypothesis for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
there is an integer
k_{n}
such that
f_{n}\leq k_{n}F
. Set then
K_{n}=\sum_{i<n}k_{i}
, and
I_{n}=\left[K_{n},K_{n+1}\right)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
This is a family of pairwise disjoint finite subsets of
\mathbb{N}
witnessing a reduction by pieces from
(f_{i})_{i}
to
(\bigoplus F)^{\infty}
.

For the second item, we build by induction a reduction by pieces.
Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, suppose that we have built a family
(I_{m})_{m<n}
of pairwise disjoint finite subsets of
\mathbb{N}
such that for all
m<n
we have
\bigoplus F\leq\bigoplus_{i\in I_{m}}f_{i}
.
Set
j=\max(\bigcup_{m<n}I_{m})+1
and use the hypothesis to fix an injective function
\iota:F\rightarrow[j,\infty)
such that for all
g\in F
we have
g\leq f_{\iota(g)}
.
Setting
I_{n}=\iota(F)
, which is finite since
F
is, yields the desired reduction by pieces. ∎

The next theorem shows how finitely generated levels guarantee a neat representation of centered functions. Let us write
\mathsf{Scat}_{[\lambda,\lambda+n]}=\bigcup_{i\leq n}\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda+i}
for
\lambda
limit or null and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Theorem 4.10
.

Let
\lambda
be zero or a limit ordinal and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Assume that
\mathsf{Scat}_{[\lambda,\lambda+n]}
is generated by some finite set
F
.
Then for every centered function
g\in\mathsf{Scat}_{[\lambda,\lambda+n+1]}
either
g\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
or there exists a non empty
G\subseteq F
such that
g\equiv\pgl G
.

In particular, there are finitely many centered functions up to equivalence in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda+n+1}
.

Proof.

Let
g\in\mathsf{Scat}_{[\lambda,\lambda+n+1]}
be centered, so of successor
\CB
-rank by
Theorem

4.7
.
In particular,
g\not\equiv\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
, so
\lambda<\CB(g)\leq\lambda+n+1
.

By
Theorem

4.7
, there is a
\leq
-monotone sequence
(g_{i})_{i}
such that
g\equiv\pgl_{i}g_{i}
and
for every
i
we have
\CB(g_{i})<\CB(g)\leq\lambda+n+1
and
(\sup_{i}\CB(g_{i}))+1=\CB(g)>\lambda
. In particular, we have
\sup_{i}\CB(g_{i})\geq\lambda
.
Assume first that
\sup_{i}\CB(g_{i})=\lambda
. If
\lambda=0
, then
g\equiv\mathsf{k}_{1}=\pgl\emptyset
. Otherwise
\lambda
is limit and
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\equiv\pgl_{i}g_{i}\equiv g
by
Corollary

3.20
.

Assume now that
\sup_{i}\CB(g_{i})>\lambda
. By monotonicity there exists
j\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\CB(g_{i})\geq\lambda
for all
i\geq j
, and using monotonicity again
we get
g\equiv\pgl_{i\geq j}g_{i}
by two applications of
Proposition

3.7
.
Now for every
i\geq j
, since
g_{i}\in\mathsf{Scat}_{[\lambda,\lambda+n]}
we can choose a way of writing
g_{i}\equiv\bigoplus_{f\in F}n_{i,f}f
and set
G_{i}=\{f\in F\mid n_{i,f}>0\}
.
We define
G=\bigcup_{i\geq j}G_{i}
and show that
g\equiv\pgl G
. Note that
G
cannot be empty, since this would imply
g_{i}=\emptyset
for all
i\geq j
and so
\sup_{i}\CB(g_{i})=0
, a contradiction.

By definition of
G
,
g_{i}\in\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(G)
for all
i\geq j
. Moreover, for all
f\in G
we have
f\in G_{i}
for some
i\geq j
, hence
f\leq g_{i}
and so
f\leq g_{k}
for all
k\geq i
by monotonicity of
(g_{i})_{i\geq j}
. Using
Proposition

4.9
, we obtain
g\equiv\pgl G
.
∎

Here is the first common point between
\mathsf{Scat}_{2}
and
\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda+1}
for all
\lambda
limit that we announced.

Corollary 4.11
.

Let
\lambda<\omega_{1}
be either equal to 1 or infinite limit.
Then, up to continuous equivalence, there are two centered functions in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda+1}
:
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
and
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
. Moreover,
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}<\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
holds.

Proof.

We can use
Theorem

4.10
since the hypothesis is valid thanks to
Proposition

2.35
for
\lambda=1
, and to
Theorem

3.17
for
\lambda
limit.

In case
\lambda=1
, any centered function in
\mathsf{Scat}_{2}
is equivalent to
\pgl G
where
G
is either
\{\mathsf{k}_{1}\}
,
\{\mathsf{l}_{1}\}
or
\{\mathsf{k}_{1},\mathsf{l}_{1}\}
.
Therefore we get
\mathsf{k}_{2}
and
\pgl\{\mathsf{k}_{1},\mathsf{l}_{1}\}\equiv\pgl\mathsf{l}_{1}
by
Proposition

4.9
, as wanted.

In case
\lambda
is limit the result is straightforward since the only possible
G
is
\{\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\}
.

For the last part, note that
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\leq\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
and suppose towards a contradiction that equivalence holds. If
\lambda=1
, then using
Proposition

4.5
we get
\mathsf{l}_{1}=\id_{\mathbb{N}}\leq n\id_{1}=n\mathsf{k}_{1}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, a contradiction. If
\lambda
is limit, let
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}=\pgl_{n}\mathsf{k}_{\alpha_{n}+1}
for
(\alpha_{n})_{n}
cofinal in
\lambda
. By
Proposition

4.5
again, it follows that
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq\bigoplus_{n<M}\mathsf{k}_{\alpha_{n}+1}
for some
M\in\mathbb{N}
, but then
\CB(\mathsf{l}_{\lambda})=\lambda\leq\sup_{n<M}(\alpha_{n}+1)<\lambda
, a contradiction again.
∎

4.3
Simple functions at successors of limit levels

Proposition 4.12
.

Let
f
be in
\mathsf{Scat}
, assume that
f=\bigsqcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f_{i}
for some sequence
(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of centered functions and set
I=\{n\in\mathbb{N}\mid\CB(f_{n})=\sup_{i}\CB(f_{i})\}
.

(i)

\CB(f)
is successor if and only if
I\neq\emptyset
,

(ii)

The
\CB
-degree of
f
is the cardinality of the set of cocenters of the functions
f_{i}
for
i
in
I
.

In particular,
f
is simple if and only if
I\neq\emptyset
and for all
n\in I
the cocenters of the functions
f_{n}
coincide with the distinguished point of
f
.

Proof.

Observe first that by
Corollary

2.16
we have
\CB(f)=\sup_{i}\CB(f_{i})
.

To see the first item, suppose that
\CB(f)=\alpha+1
for some
\alpha<\omega_{1}
. By
Fact

2.14

(i)
we have
\CB_{\alpha}(f)=\bigsqcup_{n}\CB_{\alpha}(f_{n})
.
Since
\CB_{\alpha}(f)
is non-empty, so must be
\CB_{\alpha}(f_{n})
for some
n
, which means that
\CB(f_{n})=\alpha+1
and
n\in I\neq\emptyset
.
If now
I\neq\emptyset
, then for any
n\in I
we have
\CB(f_{n})=\sup_{i}\CB(f_{i})=\CB(f)
and by
Theorem

4.7

\CB(f_{n})
is successor, hence
\CB(f)
is too.

For the second point, if
\CB(f)
is limit then our convention is that the
\CB
-degree of
f
is
0
, which is also the cardinality of the set of cocenters of functions in
I
, since
I=\emptyset
by the first point. Suppose now that
\CB(f)=\alpha+1
for some
\alpha<\omega_{1}
and so
I\neq\emptyset
.
For all
n\in I
the function
f_{n}
, being centered, is simple of distinguished point its cocenter
y_{n}
by
Theorem

4.7
.
Since by
Fact

2.14

(i)
we have
\CB_{\alpha}(f)=\bigsqcup_{i}\CB_{\alpha}(f_{i})
and
\CB_{\alpha}(f_{i})=\emptyset
if
i\notin I
, we actually have

\CB_{\alpha}(f)=\bigsqcup_{n\in I}\CB_{\alpha}(f_{n})
, and
f(\CB_{\alpha}(f))=f(\bigsqcup_{n\in I}\CB_{\alpha}(f_{n}))=\{y_{n}\mid n\in I\}
, which proves our point.
∎

Theorem 4.13
.

Let
\lambda
be limit or 1. Assume that continuous reducibility is
bqo
on
\mathsf{Scat}_{<\lambda}
.
Any simple function
f\in\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda+1}
is continuously equivalent to one of
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1},\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\bigoplus\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
or
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
.

Proof.

Since we assume that
\leq
is
bqo
on
\mathsf{Scat}_{<\lambda}
, by the General Structure
Theorem

3.17
it is also
bqo
on
\mathsf{Scat}_{\leq\lambda}
. So by

Theorem

4.8
we can write
f=\bigsqcup_{i\in I}f_{i}:A_{i}\to B
with
(f_{i})_{i\in I}
a sequence of centered functions. As
f
is simple,
f\leq\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
by
Proposition

3.12
, so if
f_{i}\equiv\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
for some
i\in I
, then
f\equiv\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
.

Since
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
and
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
are the only two centered functions in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda+1}
by
Corollary

4.11
,
we can suppose that for all
n\in I
if
\CB(f_{n})>\lambda
then
f_{n}\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
.
By
Proposition

4.12
we have
f_{i}\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
for at least one
i\in I
, and
the distinguished point
\bar{y}
of
f
is the cocenter of
f_{i}
for any
f_{i}\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
.
In the rest of this proof, all rays are taken with respect to
\bar{y}
.
By simplicity
\CB_{\lambda}(f)\subseteq f^{-1}(\{\bar{y}\})
, so
\CB(f^{(n)})\leq\lambda
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Also we note that
\CB(f_{i}^{(j)})<\lambda
for all
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
, which follows from
Proposition

4.5
when
f_{i}\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
.

If
\CB(f^{(n)})<\lambda
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then
f\leq\pgl_{n}f^{(n)}\leq\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
by
Corollaries

3.8
and
3.20
and so
f\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
.

So we can suppose that
\CB(f^{(n)})=\lambda
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Fix
W=B^{(n)}
, we have
\bar{y}\notin W
,
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{W}

by
Theorem

3.17
, and
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{B\setminus W}
by centeredness of
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
.
Since
W
is clopen we have
f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{B\setminus W}\bigoplus f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{W}\equiv f
by
Corollary

2.29
, so
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\bigoplus\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq f
.

We show that
f\leq\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\bigoplus\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
also holds in that case.
To do so we are going to find a clopen partition
A=C_{0}\sqcup C_{1}
satisfying

f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{C_{0}}\leq\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
and
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{C_{1}}\leq\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
, which by
Proposition

2.25

gives
f\leq\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\bigoplus\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
.

We split
f
“along the diagonal”. For all
j\in\mathbb{N}
, let
g_{j}=\bigsqcup_{i\leq j}f_{i}^{(j)}
and
h_{j}=\bigsqcup_{i>j}f_{i}^{(j)}
.
Consider the clopen sets
C^{j}_{i}=\dom(f_{i}^{(j)})=A_{i}\cap f^{-1}(B^{(j)})
for all
i,j
. Let
C_{0}=\bigcup\{C^{j}_{i}\mid i>j\}
and its complement
C_{1}=A\setminus C_{0}
. Since
\CB(f^{(j)}_{i})<\lambda
for all
i,j
we have
\CB(h_{j})\leq\lambda
,
so
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{C_{0}}\leq\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
by
Proposition

3.12
.
For all
j
, we also have
g_{j}=\bigsqcup_{i\leq j}f_{i}^{(j)}
and so
\CB(g_{j})<\lambda
. Since the functions
g_{j}
correspond to the rays of
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{C_{1}}
at
\bar{y}
, a combination of
Corollaries

3.8
and
3.20
then yields

f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{C_{1}}\leq\pgl_{j}g_{j}\leq\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
.

Note that
C_{0}
is open as a union of clopen sets. To see that
C_{1}
is open too, note that
C_{1}=\bigcup_{i}(A_{i}\setminus C_{0})
and that
A_{i}\setminus C_{0}=\bigcup_{j\leq i}C^{j}_{i}
is clopen for all
i
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Moreover, we show in
Theorem

7.6
that in fact
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}<\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\bigoplus\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}<\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
. Finally, note that
the Decomposition
Lemma

2.21
yields the following corollary.

Corollary 4.14
.

For
\lambda
limit or 1, if continuous reducibility is
bqo
on
\mathsf{Scat}_{<\lambda}
, then
the set of functions in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda+1}
that have finite degree is finitely generated by the set
\{\mathsf{l}_{\lambda},\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1},\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\}
.

5
Precise structure

We have now reached the point where we need to define a finite set of generators, for that we require a third and last operation.

5.1
The wedge operation

Finite gluing and pointed gluing allow us to generate all functions with compact domains up to continuous equivalence by
Theorem

3.16
.
The infinite gluing allows us to build functions which map non converging sequences to non converging sequences.
With the pointed gluing, we can build functions which send converging sequences to converging sequences.

The
wedge operation
, that we now introduce, allows us to construct functions exhibiting two new “continuous” behaviours on sequences that appear to be essential for functions with non compact domains.
First, several sequences converging to distinct limits can be mapped to converging sequences that share the same limit.
Second, a non converging sequence can be mapped to a converging sequence.
While gluing and pointed gluing naturally arise from operations on spaces, this new operation is fundamentally an operation on functions, with no obvious counterpart for spaces.

Definition 5.1
.

Given a natural number
k\in\mathbb{N}
and functions
(f_{i})_{i\leq k+1}
in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
,
we define a function
f:=\textstyle{\bigvee}(f_{0},\ldots,f_{k}\mid f_{k+1})
called the
wedge
of
(f_{0},\ldots,f_{k}\mid f_{k+1})
as follows.

Let
A_{i}=\pgl\dom f_{i}
, for
i\leq k
,
A_{k+1+i}=\dom f_{k+1}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, and
B_{j}=\bigoplus_{i\leq k+1}\im f_{i}
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
.
The domain of
\textstyle{\bigvee}(f_{0},\ldots,f_{k}\mid f_{k+1})
is
\bigoplus_{i}A_{i}
and its range is
\pgl_{j}B_{j}
.
Then we set for all
i,j
in
\mathbb{N}
:

\displaystyle f((i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{\infty})

\displaystyle=(0)^{\infty}\mbox{ if }i\leq k

\displaystyle f((i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{j}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}x)

\displaystyle=(0)^{j}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}f_{i}(x)\mbox{ if }i\leq k

\displaystyle f((k+1+i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}x)

\displaystyle=(0)^{i}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(k+1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}f_{k+1}(x)

\cdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\cdots
f_{0}
f_{1}
g

Figure 2:
The wedge operation
\textstyle{\bigvee}(f_{0},f_{1}\mid g)
.

Remark 5.2
.

This finitary operation is closely related to the following infinitary operation.Given an infinite matrix of functions
f_{i,j}:A_{i,j}\to B_{i,j}
,
{(i,j)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}}
, we can define
\textstyle{\bigvee}(f_{i,j}):\bigoplus_{j}\pgl_{i}A_{i,j}\to\pgl_{i}\bigoplus_{j}B_{i,j}
by

\displaystyle(j)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{\infty}

\displaystyle\longmapsto(0)^{\infty}

\displaystyle(j)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{i}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}x

\displaystyle\longmapsto(0)^{i}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(j)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}f_{i,j}(x)

Alternatively, we can first form the function
w=\bigoplus_{j}\pgl_{i}f_{i,j}
whose codomain is given by
\bigoplus_{j}\pgl_{i}B_{i,j}
.
Naturally, each
\pgl_{i}B_{i,j}
is a pointed space with basepoint
p_{j}=(0)^{\infty}
and their
wedge sum
12
12

12

See for instance
[
Rot88
, p.153]
.
is defined through
the topological quotient
\pi:\bigoplus_{j}\pgl_{i}B_{i,j}\to\bigvee_{j}\pgl_{i}B_{i,j}
with respect to the equivalence relation
\{((j)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}p_{j},(j^{\prime})\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}p_{j^{\prime}})\mid j,j^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}\}
.
Then, we can define
\textstyle{\bigvee}(f_{i,j})
by
\pi w
, which explains our choice of name for this operation.

Observe that our finitary operation
\textstyle{\bigvee}(f_{0},\ldots,f_{k}\mid f_{k+1})
can be viewed as the following particular case of the infinitary wedge. Each of first
k+1
functions
f_{0},\ldots,f_{k}
are repeated infinitely in each of the first
k+1
columns,
(f_{i,j})_{j}=(f_{i})^{\infty}
for
i=0,\ldots,k
, hence we informally refer to them as “the verticals”.
The function
f_{k+2}
is repeated infinitely on the diagonal of the remainder of the matrix, say
f_{i,i}=f_{k+2}
for all
i>k
, so we informally call it “the diagonal”. All other entries are filled with the empty function.

Note that for any function
f
we have
\pgl f\equiv\textstyle{\bigvee}(f|\emptyset)
.

Fact 5.3
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
(f_{i})_{i\leq k+1}
be a sequence in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
, and set
f:=\textstyle{\bigvee}(f_{0}\ldots f_{k}\mid f_{k+1})
.

(i)

The wedge operation preserves countability and Polishness of domain and range, surjectivity, finite-to-oneness, and continuity of functions.

(ii)

If
k>0
, then
f
is not injective.

(iii)

If
f_{i}\in\mathsf{Scat}
for all
i\leq k+1
then so does
f
, and

\CB(f)=\max\big(\{\CB(f_{i})+1\mid i\leq k\}\cup\{\CB(f_{k+1})\}\big).

As with our other operations on functions, we now to look for upper and lower bound criteria.
We can first adapt the “upper bound” sufficient criterion
Proposition

3.7
to get an upper bound criterion for the wedge operation.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
f:A\to B
in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
be continuous and
(f_{i})_{i\leq k+1}\subseteq\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Suppose that there exist
y\in B
and a clopen partition
(A_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of
A
with the following properties:

(i)

For all
i\leq k
,
((f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{i}})^{(y,j)})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is reducible by pieces to
(f_{i})^{\infty}
,

(ii)

(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{i}})_{i>k}
is reducible by pieces to
(f_{k+1})^{\infty}
,

(iii)

f(A_{i})\rightarrow y
.

Then
f\leq\textstyle{\bigvee}(f_{0},\ldots,f_{k}\mid f_{k+1})
.

Proof.

Use the hypotheses to fix a family
(I_{n})_{n}
of pairwise disjoint finite subsets of
\mathbb{N}
and reductions
(\sigma^{D}_{n},\tau^{D}_{n})
from
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{n}}
to
\bigoplus_{i\in I_{n}}f_{k+1}
for all
n>k
,
and to get continuous reductions
(\sigma^{V}_{i},\tau^{V}_{i})
from
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{i}}
to
\pgl f_{i}
with
\tau^{V}_{i}((0)^{\infty})=y
using
Proposition

3.7
, for all
i\leq k
.

Define
\sigma
as follows:

\displaystyle\sigma(x)=

\displaystyle(i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}\sigma^{V}_{i}(x)

if
x\in A_{i}
for some
i\leq k
,

\displaystyle\sigma(x)=

\displaystyle\sigma^{D}_{i}(x)

if
x\in A_{i}
for some
i>k
.

Define
\tau
as follows:

\displaystyle\tau((0)^{\infty})=

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle\tau((0)^{j}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}x)=

\displaystyle\tau^{V}_{i}((0)^{j}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}x)

if
i\leq k
and
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\tau((0)^{i}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(k+1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}x)=

\displaystyle\tau^{D}_{n}((i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}x)

if
i\in I_{n}
.

By construction
(\sigma,\tau)
is a reduction from
f
to
\textstyle{\bigvee}(f_{0},\ldots,f_{k}\mid f_{k+1})
. The function
\sigma
is continuous as a disjoint union of continuous maps by
Lemma

2.22
.
To see that
\tau
is continuous, note that
\tau
is continuous on
U=\dom\tau\setminus\{(0)^{\infty}\}
again by
Lemma

2.22
.
To use
Lemma

3.5
, we claim that for every sequence
(x_{n})_{n}
in
U
that converges to
(0)^{\infty}
we have
\tau(x_{n})\to y
.
If
(x_{n})
is included in
U_{i}=\bigcup_{j}N_{(0)^{j}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(i)}
for some
i\leq k
, then continuity of
\tau_{i}^{V}
ensures that
\tau(x_{n})\to\tau^{V}_{i}((0)^{\infty})=y
.
If now
(x_{n})_{n}
is included in
U_{k+1}=\bigcup_{i}N_{(0)^{i}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(k+1)}
then
\tau(x_{n})\to y
because we assumed that
f(A_{i})\to y
.
Since the sets
U_{0},\ldots U_{k},U_{k+1}
form a finite partition of
U
, the claim follows.
∎

As a corollary, we obtain that, up to continuous equivalence, any function of the form
\textstyle{\bigvee}(f_{0},\ldots,f_{k}\mid g)
is equivalent to one where the verticals
\{f_{i}\mid i\leq k\}
form an antichain for continuous reducibility
13
13

13

We go further in
Lemma

7.3
and prove the existence of a reduced form for the wedge of continuous functions
. To state this corollary, we use the
domination
order on sets of functions, which will also be used in the sequel. For
F,G
set of functions we say that
F
is
dominated
by
G
if for all
f\in F
there is
g\in G
with
f\leq g
. Note that if
G
is finite, then
G
is equivalent for domination to any maximal antichain of maximal elements of
G
.
When no confusion is possible, we simply denote by
F\leq G
for domination and
f\leq G
instead of
\{f\}\leq G
.

Corollary 5.5
.

Assume that
f_{0},\ldots,f_{k},h_{0},\ldots,h_{l},g\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
are continuous. If
\{f_{i}\mid i\leq k\}
is equivalent to
\{h_{j}\mid j\leq l\}
for domination, then

\textstyle{\bigvee}(f_{0},\ldots,f_{k}\mid g)\equiv\textstyle{\bigvee}(h_{0},\ldots,h_{l}\mid g).

Proof.

To see that the left hand side wedge, denoted by
f
, reduces to right hand side wedge,
for each
i\leq k
let
\phi(i)=\min\{j\leq l\mid f_{i}\leq h_{j}\}
and set
A_{j}=\bigcup\{N_{(i)}\mid\phi(i)=j\}
for
j\leq l
.
Notice that the sets
A_{j}
,
j\leq l
partition
\dom f
and for
y=(0)^{\infty}
we have
(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{j}})^{(y,n)}\equiv\bigoplus_{i\in\phi^{-1}(j)}f_{i}\leq|\phi^{-1}(j)|h_{j}
for all
j\leq l
.
∎

Finding
‘natural’
sufficient conditions for when a wedge is a lower bound for a function
f
has proven to be quite tricky,
in particular to make sure that the map
\tau
is well-defined. So far, we have not been able to find a condition resembling that of
Lemma

3.13
.
We can however generalize
Proposition

3.14
which enjoys much stronger hypotheses.
In these specific cases we can
‘disjointify’
enough pieces of the reduction to make sure that their union is well-defined.

Lemma 5.6

(Disjointification Lemma)
.

Let
f:A\to B
in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
be continuous and
(f_{i})_{i\leq k+1}\subseteq\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Suppose that there exist
y\in\im f
and
(x_{i})_{i\leq k}
in
f^{-1}(\{y\})
such that

(i)

for every
i\leq k
, for every open
U\ni x_{i}
there exists
(\sigma,\tau)
reducing
f_{i}
to
f

with
\im(\sigma)\subseteq U
and
y\notin\overline{\im(f\sigma)}
, and

(ii)

for every open
V\ni y
, there exists
(\sigma,\tau)
reducing
f_{k+1}
to
f
with
\im(f\sigma)\subseteq V
and
y\notin\overline{\im(f\sigma)}
.

Then
\textstyle{\bigvee}(f_{0},\ldots,f_{k}\mid f_{k+1})\leq f
.

Proof.

We simultaneously define by induction on
n
, a sequence
(V_{n})_{n}\subseteq B
of open neighborhoods of
y
and sequences
(U_{i,n})_{n}\subseteq A
of open neighborhoods of
x_{i}
for each
i\leq k
satisfying the following conditions:

1)

V_{n}\rightarrow y
in
B
,

2)

U_{i,n}\rightarrow x_{i}
in
A
, and

3)

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, for all
i\leq k+1
, there is a continuous reduction
(\sigma_{i,n},\tau_{i,n})
from
f_{i}
to
f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V_{n}\setminus V_{n+1}}
,
satisfying
\im(\sigma_{i,n})\subseteq U_{i,n}
in case
i\leq k
.

Set
V_{0}=B
and
U_{i,0}=A
for all
i\leq k
.
Fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
and suppose that all relevant sets are built for
m\leq n
.
For all
i\leq k
, use
(i)
on
U_{i,n}\cap f^{-1}(V_{n})
(that is an open set containing
x_{i}
) to find reductions
(\sigma_{i},\tau_{i})
from
f_{i}
to
f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V_{n}}
.
Use
(ii)
on
V_{n}
to find a reduction
(\sigma_{k+1},\tau_{k+1})
from
f_{k+1}
to
f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V_{n}}
.
Together,
(i)
and
(ii)
guarantee that
y
is not in the closed set
\bigcup_{i\leq k+1}\overline{\im(f\sigma_{i})}
, so
we can find a natural number
N
such that
V_{n+1}:=N_{y\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{N}}
is disjoint from
\bigcup_{i\leq k+1}\overline{\im(f\sigma_{i})}
.
But by continuity of
f
at
x_{i}
, for all
i\leq k
since
f(x_{i})=y\in V_{n+1}
there exists a natural number
N_{i}\geq n
such that
U_{i,n+1}=N_{x_{i}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{N_{i}}}\subseteq U_{i,n}\cap f^{-1}(V_{n+1})
.
This finishes the inductive construction.

Fix now a bijection
\iota:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow(k+1)\times\mathbb{N}
and for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
write
\iota(m)=(\iota_{0}(m),\iota_{1}(m))
. For all
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
pick a continuous reduction
(\sigma_{m},\tau_{m})
from
f_{\iota_{0}(m)}
to
f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V_{m}\setminus V_{m+1}}
as guaranteed by the above construction.

Name
\varphi
the inverse of
\iota
and define
(\sigma,\tau)
as follows:

\displaystyle\sigma((i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{\infty})

\displaystyle=x_{i},

\displaystyle\sigma((i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{n}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}x)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\varphi(i,n)}(x),

\displaystyle\text{for all $i\leq k$ and all $n\in\mathbb{N}$},

\displaystyle\sigma((k+1+n)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}x)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\varphi(k+1,n)}(x),

\displaystyle\text{for all $n\in\mathbb{N}$}.

Also, we let
\tau(y)=(0)^{\infty}
and if
z\in\dom\tau_{m}\subseteq V_{m}\setminus V_{m+1}

we set

\tau(z)=(0)^{\iota_{1}(m)}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(\iota_{0}(m))\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}\tau_{m}(z).

We claim that the pair
(\sigma,\tau)
is a reduction from
g:=\textstyle{\bigvee}(f_{0},\ldots,f_{k}\mid f_{k+1})
to
f
. By construction we have
g=\tau f\sigma
.
To see that
\sigma
is continuous, we use
Lemma

3.5
with
U=\dom g\setminus\{(i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{\infty}\mid i\leq k\}
.
As
\sigma
is continuous on
U
by
Lemma

2.22
, we show the second condition of
Lemma

3.5
.
Take
(x_{n})_{n}
in
U
converging to some
(i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{\infty}
. Therefore there exists
M
such that for all
n\geq M
we can write
x_{n}=(i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{k_{n}}\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}x^{\prime}_{n}
,
with
k_{n}\to\infty
. Therefore
\sigma(x_{n})=\sigma_{\varphi(i,k_{n})}(x^{\prime}_{n})\in U_{i,k_{n}}
for all
n\geq M
and since
k_{n}\to\infty
and
U_{i,k_{n}}\to x_{i}
we get
\sigma(x_{n})\to x_{i}=\sigma((i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{\infty})=\sigma(x)
, as desired.

Continuity of
\tau
similarly follows from
Lemma

3.5
, this time applied to the open set
\dom\tau\setminus\{y\}=\bigsqcup_{m}\dom\tau_{m}
by remarking that
\iota_{1}(m_{k})\to\infty
when
m_{k}\to\infty
.
∎

5.2
Finite generation at successors of limits

As a warm-up for the general case, we can now apply the new operation to complete the analysis of
\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda+1}
when
\lambda
is 1 or limit.
This will allow us to see all the ingredients of the proof of the general case in a simplified setting.

We need following elaboration on
Fact

2.34
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
X_{0},\ldots,X_{n}
be infinite subsets of a metrizable space
B
.
Then there are pairwise disjoint infinite sets
Y_{i}\subseteq X_{i}
for all
i\leq n
such that
\bigcup_{i\leq n}Y_{i}
is discrete.

Proof.

We prove the statement by induction on
n
. If
n=0
, this is simply
Fact

2.34
.
So assume it holds for all collections of size
n
and let
X_{0},\ldots,X_{n+1}
be infinite subsets of
B
. We distinguish two cases.

First case: there exist distinct
i,j\leq n+1
such that
X_{i}\cap X_{j}
is infinite.
Reindexing if necessary, assume
j=n+1
and define
\tilde{X}_{i}=X_{i}\cap X_{n+1}
and
\tilde{X}_{k}=X_{k}
otherwise.
We can apply the induction hypothesis to
\tilde{X}_{0},\ldots\tilde{X}_{n}
to obtain pairwise disjoint infinite sets
\tilde{Y}_{i}\subseteq\tilde{X}_{i}
, for
i\leq n
, whose union is discrete.
To obtain the desired sets for the original collection, it suffices to set
Y_{j}=\tilde{Y}_{j}
for
j\neq i
and further partition
\tilde{Y}_{i}
into two infinite sets
Y_{i}
and
Y_{n+1}
.

Second case: for all
i,j\leq n+1
the set
X_{i}\cap X_{j}
is finite. We can make them disjoint: set
\tilde{X}_{i}=X_{i}\setminus\bigcup_{j\neq i}X_{j}
for all
i\leq n
.
Apply the induction hypothesis to
\tilde{X}_{i}
for
i\leq n
to get infinite pairwise disjoint sets
Y_{i}\subseteq\tilde{X}_{i}
,
i\leq n
with a discrete union.
Now pick a discrete
Y_{n+1}\subseteq\tilde{X}_{n+1}
by
Fact

2.34
and note that the sets
Y_{i}
for
i\leq n+1
are infinite pairwise disjoint discrete sets.
If
Y=\bigcup_{i\leq n+1}Y_{i}
is discrete as well, we are done.
Otherwise, there exists
i\leq n
such that either
Y_{i}\cap\overline{Y_{n+1}}\neq\emptyset
or
\overline{Y_{i}}\cap Y_{n+1}\neq\emptyset
.
Suppose that the former holds, choose
y\in Y_{i}\cap\overline{Y_{n+1}}
and an open neighborhood
V
of
y
such that
V\cap Y_{i}=\{y\}
(since
Y_{i}
is discrete).
Note that
Y_{n+1}\cap V
is infinite and so we can shrink these two sets as follows
Y_{n+1}:=Y_{n+1}\cap V
and
Y_{i}:=Y_{i}\setminus\{y\}
.
Both
Y_{i}
and
Y_{n+1}
are infinite and now
Y_{i}\cap\overline{Y_{n+1}}=\emptyset
.
The latter case is similar and since
n
is finite, we can apply this procedure repeatedly until we obtain the desired family
(Y_{i})_{i\leq n+1}
.
∎

As an application, we show that if for infinitely many points
y\in\im f
we can reduce a single function
g
to corestrictions of
f
on arbitrary small neighborhood of
y
, then we have
\omega g\leq f
. We will use the stronger statement which involves a finite set
G
in place of
G
in
Section

6
.
Given a (finite) set
G
of functions in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
, we write
\omega G
for
\omega(\bigoplus G)
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
f:A\to B
between metrizable spaces and
G\subseteq\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
be finite.
Suppose that for all
g\in G
there are infinitely many points
y\in B
such that for all neighbourhood
V
of
y
we have
g\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}
.
Then
\omega G\leq f
.

Proof.

If
G
is empty, then
\omega G=\emptyset
which reduces to any function, so suppose that
G\neq\emptyset
.
For each
g\in G
, let
X_{g}
be the set of points
y\in B
such that for all neighborhood
V\ni y
we have
g\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}
. Since the sets
X_{g}
are infinite subsets of the metrizable space
B
and
G
is finite, we can apply
Lemma

5.7
to get an injective map
G\times\mathbb{N}\to B
,
(g,n)\mapsto y^{g}_{n}
with discrete image such that
y^{g}_{n}\in X_{g}
for all
g\in G
and all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. As
B
is metrizable, we can find pairwise disjoint open sets
V^{g}_{n}
of
B
such that
y^{g}_{n}\in V^{g}_{n}
for all
g\in G
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Therefore
\omega G\leq f
holds by
Proposition

2.27
.
∎

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
be continuous and
G\subseteq\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
be a finite set of centered functions.
If
\omega g\leq f
for all
g\in G
, then
\omega G\leq f
.
Moreover, if
f=\bigsqcup_{i=0}^{n}f_{i}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then

(i)

if
g
is centered and
\omega g\leq f
, then
\omega g\leq f_{i}
for some
i\leq n
,

(ii)

if
\lambda
is limit and
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq f
, then
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq f_{i}
for some
i\leq n
.

Proof.

Take a center
c_{g}
for
g\in G
and a continuous reduction
(\sigma,\tau)
from
\omega g
to
f
. The set
\{f\sigma((n)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}c_{g})\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is infinite and by
Fact

4.3
we have
g\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{V}
whenever
V
is a neighbourhood of
\sigma((n)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}c_{g})
. So by continuity of
f
, for every
n
and every neighborhood
U
of
f\sigma((n)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}c_{g})
it follows that
g\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
. This implies that
\omega G\leq f
by
Lemma

5.8
.

For the second part, take a center
c_{g}
for
g
and a continuous reduction
(\sigma,\tau)
from
\omega g
to
f
. As
f=\bigsqcup_{i\leq n}f_{i}
, there exists
i\leq n
such that
\dom f_{i}
contains
\sigma((n)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}c_{g})
for infinitely many
n
. We conclude as before. Finally for
\lambda
limit, using
Fact

2.31
and
Theorem

3.17
notice that for every sequence
(\beta_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
cofinal in
\lambda
, we have
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\equiv\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathsf{k}_{\beta_{k}}
. Choose an increasing sequence
(\beta_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
cofinal in
\lambda
and recall that
(0)^{\infty}
is a center for each
\mathsf{k}_{\beta_{k}}
. If
(\sigma,\tau)
witness
\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathsf{k}_{\beta_{k}}\leq\bigsqcup_{i\leq n}f_{i}
, then there exists
i\leq n
such that
X=\{k\in\mathbb{N}\mid\sigma((n)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{\infty})\in\dom f_{i}\}
is infinite. Note that the subsequence
(\beta_{k})_{k\in X}
is cofinal in
\lambda
, so
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\equiv\bigoplus_{k\in X}\mathsf{k}_{\beta_{k}}\leq f_{i}
, as before.
∎

We are now ready to show that
\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda+1}
is finitely generated, assuming that
\mathsf{Scat}_{<\lambda}
is
bqo
under continuous reducibility. We first isolate the only situation where the wedge operation is necessary.

Lemma 5.10
.

Suppose that
f=\bigsqcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}f_{n}:A_{n}\to B
for simple functions
f_{n}\in\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda+1}
with pairwise distinct distinguished points
y_{n}
. Assume that the following hold:

(i)

f_{0}\equiv\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
,

(ii)

f_{n}\leq\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\oplus\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
whenever
n>0
, and

(iii)

(y_{n})_{n>0}
converges to
y_{0}
.

Then for all clopen neighborhood
U
of
y_{0}
we have
f\leq\textstyle{\bigvee}(\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\mid\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1})\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
.

Proof.

To prove that
\textstyle{\bigvee}(\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\mid\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1})\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
, we use the Disjointification
Lemma

5.6
with
y=y_{0}
. Note that
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\equiv f_{0}
is centered with cocenter
y_{0}
and so we can choose a reduction
(\sigma,\tau)
witnessing
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq f_{0}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
. We choose
x=\sigma((0)^{\infty})
. To verify the first condition, let
W
be any clopen neighborhood of
x
in
\dom f
, let
W^{\prime}=W\cap f^{-1}(U)
. We have
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\geq f_{0}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{W^{\prime}}
and since
x\in W^{\prime}
we have
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq f_{0}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{W^{\prime}}
by
Fact

4.3
. So picking a witness for the latter reduction, we get the desired reduction
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq f_{0}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
whose
\sigma
satisfy
\im\sigma\subseteq W
and
y_{0}\notin\overline{\im f\sigma}
by
Proposition

4.5
.
For the second condition, let
V
be a neighborhood of
y_{0}
in
U
and note that there exists
n>0
with
y_{n}\in V
, since
(y_{n})_{n>0}
converges to
y_{0}
. Then choosing a clopen neighborhood
W
of
y_{n}
in
V
, note that
\CB(f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{W})=\lambda+1
so
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{W}
by
Proposition

3.15
, which grants us with the desired reduction.

Next, we show that
f\leq\textstyle{\bigvee}(\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\mid\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1})
using
Proposition

5.4
.
Fix a clopen neighborhood basis
(B_{k})_{k}
at
y_{0}
and let
B^{(k)}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, denote the corresponding clopen rays.
Notice that for
n>0
we have
y_{n}\in B_{k_{n}}
for some
k_{n}
and
f_{n}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{B_{k_{n}}}\leq\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\bigoplus\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
. So by
Proposition

2.25
we can pick a clopen subset
A^{1}_{n}
of
f_{n}^{-1}(B_{k_{n}})
such that
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{1}_{n}}\leq\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
and
A^{0}_{n}=A_{n}\setminus A^{1}_{n}
satisfy
\CB(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{0}_{n}})\leq\lambda
. Note that
f(A^{1}_{n})\subseteq B_{k_{n}}
and
k_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
since
(y_{n})_{n>0}
converges to
y_{0}
, so
f(A^{1}_{n})
converges to
y_{0}
.

Setting
A^{0}=\bigcup_{n>0}A^{0}_{n}
we obtain a clopen set of
\dom f
with
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{0}}\leq\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
by
Corollary

2.16
. We define a clopen partition of
\dom f
by setting
\tilde{A}_{0}=A_{0}\cup A^{0}
and
\tilde{A}_{n}=A^{1}_{n}
for
n>0
. Note that the rays of
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\tilde{A}_{0}}
at
y_{0}
all have
\CB
-rank
\leq\lambda
and so they are all reducible to
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
. Moreover
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\tilde{A_{n}}}\leq\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
for all
n>0
and since
f(\tilde{A}_{n})_{n>0}
converges to
y_{0}
, as desired.
∎

Theorem 5.11
.

Let
\lambda
be limit or 1. Suppose that continuous reducibility is
bqo
on
\mathsf{Scat}_{<\lambda}
.
Then
\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda+1}
is generated by the finite set

\{\mathsf{l}_{\lambda},\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1},\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda},\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1},\textstyle{\bigvee}(\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\mid\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}),\mathsf{l}_{\lambda+1}\}.

Proof.

By
Corollary

4.14
, we can assume that
f:A\to B
in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda+1}
has infinite
\CB
-degree.
By the Decomposition
Lemma

2.21
,
f
is locally simple of
\CB
-rank
\lambda+1
. Note that a disjoint union of simple functions of
\CB
-rank
\lambda+1
with same distinguished point is again simple of
\CB
-rank
\lambda+1
. So we can write
f=\bigsqcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}f_{n}
with each
f_{n}:A_{n}\to B
simple of
\CB
-rank
\lambda+1
with distinguished point
y_{n}
so that the points
y_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, are pairwise distinct.

Since
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\leq f_{n}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and neighbourhood
V\ni y_{n}
by
Proposition

3.15
, we obtain
\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\leq f
by
Lemma

5.9
.

We partition
\mathbb{N}=N_{0}\sqcup N_{1}
with
N_{1}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}\mid f_{n}\equiv\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\}
and set
Y_{i}=\{y_{n}\mid n\in N_{i}\}
,
i=0,1
. We distinguish several cases:

N_{1}
is infinite

By centeredness of
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
we can use
Lemma

5.9
again and get
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda+1}\leq f
.
As we know that
f\leq\mathsf{l}_{\lambda+1}
by
Proposition

3.12
, we obtain
f\equiv\mathsf{l}_{\lambda+1}
in this case.

N_{1}
is empty

Then by
Theorem

4.13
, for all
i

we actually have either
f_{i}\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
or
f_{i}\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\bigoplus\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
, but in both cases
f_{i}\leq\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\bigoplus\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq 2\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
. Therefore, we obtain using
Proposition

2.25
:

\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\leq f\leq\bigoplus_{i}f_{i}\leq\omega(\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\oplus\mathsf{l}_{\lambda})\leq\omega(2\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1})\equiv\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1},

so in that case
f\equiv\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
and we are also done.

N_{1}
is finite and non empty

Suppose first that for some clopen
U\supseteq Y_{1}
, the set of
n\in N_{0}
such that
y_{n}\notin U
is infinite,
then
\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{B\setminus U}
by
Lemma

5.9
and
|N_{1}|\cdot\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
by centeredness and
Proposition

2.27
, so

f\leq\bigoplus_{n}f_{n}\leq(\bigoplus_{n\in N_{1}}f_{n})\oplus(\bigoplus_{n\in N_{0}}f_{n})\leq|N_{1}|\cdot\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\oplus\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}\oplus f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{B\setminus U}\leq f,

and in this case
f\equiv|N_{1}|\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\bigoplus\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
, so we are done.

Otherwise, any clopen
U\supseteq Y_{1}
contains all but finitely many of the points
y_{n}
,
n\in N_{0}
.
In this case, choose pairwise disjoint clopen sets
U_{i}\ni y_{i}
,
i\in N_{1}
, such that for all
i\in N_{1}
the set
P_{i}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}\mid y_{n}\in U_{i}\}
is either infinite or equal to
\{i\}
.
Since
Y_{1}
is included in the clopen set
\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}
, it follows that
R=\mathbb{N}\setminus\bigcup_{i}P_{i}
is finite. By adding
R
to some infinite
P_{i}
, we get that
(P_{i})_{i\in I}
is a finite partition of
\mathbb{N}
and we define
f^{i}=\bigsqcup_{n\in P_{i}}f_{n}
.

If
P_{i}=\{i\}
, then

f^{i}\leq\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U_{i}},

since
f^{i}=f_{i}\equiv\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
and
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
is centered.

If
P_{i}
is infinite, note that the sequence
(y_{n})_{n\in P_{i}\setminus\{i\}}
converges to
y_{i}
and so
f^{i}=\bigsqcup_{n\in P_{i}}f_{n}
falls in the case presented in
Lemma

5.10
. Therefore we obtain
f^{i}\leq\textstyle{\bigvee}(\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\mid\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1})\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U_{i}}
in this case.

Now it follows from
Proposition

2.25
and
Proposition

2.27
, that

\bigoplus_{i\in I}f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U_{i}}\leq f\leq\bigoplus_{i\in I}f^{i},

so in this last case the function is a finite gluing of
\textstyle{\bigvee}(\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\mid\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1})
and
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
.

∎

5.3
The generators

We write
\mathcal{P}^{+}(F)
