for the set of non-empty subsets of a set
F
.
If
F
is a set of functions, we define
\pgl\mathcal{P}^{+}(F):=\{\pgl F^{\prime}\mid\emptyset\neq F^{\prime}\subseteq F\}
and
\omega\{F\}:=\{\omega f\mid f\in F\}
.
For (finite) sets of functions
F_{i}
with
i\leq k+1
we set
\textstyle{\bigvee}(F_{0},\ldots,F_{k}\mid F_{k+1})=\textstyle{\bigvee}(\bigoplus F_{0},\ldots,\bigoplus F_{k}\mid\bigoplus F_{k+1})
.

We now define for every
\alpha<\omega_{1}
a set
\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}
of centered functions and a set
\mathsf{G}_{\alpha}
of generators.
We set the base case and the limit cases as follows:

Base case

Let
\mathsf{C}_{0}=\emptyset
,
\mathsf{G}_{0}=\emptyset
, and

\mathsf{C}_{1}=\{\mathsf{k}_{1}\}
,

Limit case
\lambda

Let
\mathsf{C}_{\lambda}=\emptyset
,
\mathsf{G}_{\lambda}=\{\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\}
, and

\mathsf{C}_{\lambda+1}=\{\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1},\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\}
.

Then for every
\lambda
limit or null, we define
\mathsf{C}_{\lambda+n+1}
and
\mathsf{G}_{\lambda+n}
by induction on
\mathbb{N}
:

For
n>0

We let

\mathsf{C}_{\lambda+n+1}=\mathsf{C}_{\lambda+n}\cup\pgl\mathcal{P}^{+}(\mathsf{C}_{\lambda+n}\cup\omega\{\mathsf{C}_{\lambda+n}\}),

and we define the set of generators at
{\lambda+n}
by
g\in\mathsf{G}_{\lambda+n}
if

(i)

g\in\mathsf{C}_{\lambda+n}
, or

(ii)

g\in\omega\{\mathsf{C}_{\lambda+n}\}
, or

(iii)

g=\textstyle{\bigvee}(F_{0},\ldots,F_{k}\mid F_{k+1})
for some distinct
F_{0},\ldots F_{k}\in\mathcal{P}^{+}(\mathsf{G}_{\lambda+n-1})
and some
F_{k+1}\subseteq\mathsf{C}_{\lambda+n}
.

We call generators of this last type
wedge generators
.

Note that by our convention
\mathsf{G}_{0}=\emptyset
generates the only function of null
\CB
-rank, namely the empty function.

Fact 5.12
.

(i)

We have
\mathsf{G}_{1}=\{\mathsf{k}_{1},\omega\mathsf{k}_{1}\}
,

(ii)

For
\lambda
limit, the set
\mathsf{G}_{\lambda+1}
is equivalent (for domination) to

\{\mathsf{l}_{\lambda},\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1},\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda},\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1},\textstyle{\bigvee}(\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\mid\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}),\mathsf{l}_{\lambda+1}\}.

Proof.

Note that
\mathsf{C}_{0}=\emptyset
,
\mathsf{G}_{0}=\emptyset
, and
\mathsf{C}_{1}=\{\mathsf{k}_{1}\}
, so
\mathsf{G}_{1}=\{\mathsf{k}_{1},\omega\mathsf{k}_{1}\}
.
For the second item, if
\lambda<\omega_{1}
is limit, we have
\mathsf{C}_{\lambda+1}=\{\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1},\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\}
and note that if
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\in F
then
\textstyle{\bigvee}(\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\mid F)\equiv\mathsf{l}_{\lambda+1}
.
∎

Here are a few useful facts on the combinatorics of generators.
The notation
\mathsf{Scat}_{[\lambda,\alpha]}
stands for
\mathsf{Scat}_{\leq\alpha}\cap\mathsf{Scat}_{\geq\lambda}

Proposition 5.13
.

Let
\alpha=\lambda+n
be a countable ordinal with limit or null and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(i)

We have
\mathsf{C}_{\lambda+n}\subseteq\mathsf{C}_{\lambda+n+1}
and
\mathsf{G}_{\lambda+n}\subseteq\mathsf{G}_{\lambda+n+1}
.

(ii)

The sets
\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}
and
\mathsf{G}_{\alpha}
are included in
\mathsf{Scat}_{[\lambda,\alpha]}
.

(iii)

The sets
\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}
and
\mathsf{G}_{\alpha}
are finite.

(iv)

For all wedge generator
f\in\mathsf{G}_{\alpha}
there are
F,H\subseteq\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}
such that
\{\omega H\}\cup F\leq f
and
f\leq(\bigoplus F)\oplus\omega H
.

(v)

For all
g\in\mathsf{G}_{\alpha}
,
\omega g
is equivalent to
\omega H
for some
H\subseteq\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}
.

(vi)

If
f_{n}\in\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(\mathsf{G}_{\alpha})
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}}f_{n}
is equivalent to a function in
\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(\mathsf{G}_{\alpha})
.

Proof.

(i)

By induction, using the definition of the sets
\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}
and
\mathsf{G}_{\alpha}
.

(ii)

Use
Proposition

3.3
and
Fact

5.3
.

(iii)

It follows from the finiteness of the power set of a finite set and the fact that, in the definition of
\mathsf{G}_{\alpha}
, the requirement
F_{i}\neq F_{j}

for wedge generators forces the sequence of sets
F_{i}
to be finite, thus yielding finiteness of
\mathsf{G}_{\alpha}
.

(iv)

Take
g=\textstyle{\bigvee}(F_{0},\ldots,F_{k}\mid F_{k+1})
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
F_{i}\in\mathcal{P}^{+}(\mathsf{G}_{\alpha-1})
for
i\leq k
and
F_{k+1}\subseteq\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}
.
Set
F=\{\pgl F_{i}\mid i\leq k\}
and
H=F_{k+1}
. We have
F\leq g
,
\omega H\leq g
and
g\leq\bigoplus F\oplus\omega H
by
Corollary

2.26
.

(v)

In case
g
is a wedge generator, take
F,H\subseteq\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}
given by the previous point. By
Propositions

2.25
and
2.28
we get
\omega g\equiv\omega(F\cup H)
.

(vi)

Up to continuous equivalence we can suppose that all
f_{n}
are actually in
\mathsf{G}_{\alpha}
.
Note that by finiteness of
\mathsf{G}_{\alpha}
there is
N\in\mathbb{N}
and a partition
I_{0},\ldots,I_{N}
of
\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\leq N
and
i,j\in I_{n}

we have
f_{i}=f_{j}=g_{n}
for some
g_{n}\in\mathsf{G}_{\alpha}
.
Now if
I_{n}
is infinite, we have
\bigoplus_{i\in I_{n}}f_{i}\equiv\omega g_{n}
, which is equivalent to a finite gluing of functions in
\omega\{\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}\}
by the previous point.
∎

Our goal is to prove:

Theorem 5.14

(Precise Structure)
.

For all
\alpha<\omega_{1}
, every function in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha}
is continuously equivalent to a finite gluing of functions in
\mathsf{G}_{\alpha}
.

For
\alpha<\omega_{1}
, we call
finite generation at level
\alpha
the statement:

\mathrm{FG}(\alpha)

Every function in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha}
is equivalent to a finite gluing of functions in
\mathsf{G}_{\alpha}
.

The notations
\mathrm{FG}(<\alpha)
and
\mathrm{FG}(\leq\alpha)
stand for the conjunction of
\mathrm{FG}(\beta)
for all
\beta<\alpha
and for all
\beta\leq\alpha
, respectively.
We close this section by stating the results that we have already established about the statements
\mathrm{FG}(\alpha)
.

Proposition 5.15
.

We have
\mathrm{FG}(0)
,
\mathrm{FG}(1)
and
\mathrm{FG}(\lambda)
for every
\lambda
limit.
Moreover for every countable ordinal
\alpha=\lambda+n
, with
\lambda
limit or null and
n<\omega
, we have

(i)

\mathrm{FG}(<\lambda)
implies
\mathrm{FG}(\lambda+1)
.

(ii)

\mathrm{FG}(\leq\alpha)
implies that every function in
\mathsf{Scat}_{[\lambda,\alpha]}
is a finite gluing of functions in
\mathsf{G}_{\alpha}
.

(iii)

\mathrm{FG}(<\alpha)
implies that continuous reducibility is a
bqo
on
\mathsf{Scat}_{<\alpha}
.

(iv)

If
\mathrm{FG}(<\alpha)
holds then every function in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha}
is locally centered.

(v)

If
\mathrm{FG}(<\alpha)
holds then every centered function in
\mathsf{Scat}_{[\lambda,\alpha]}
is equivalent to some function in
\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}
. In particular, it is equivalent either to
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
or to
\pgl G
for some
G\subseteq\mathsf{C}_{\alpha-1}\cup\omega\{\mathsf{C}_{\alpha-1}\}
.

Proof.

Note that
Theorem

3.17
trivially implies
\mathrm{FG}(\lambda)
for
\lambda
limit or null. Using
Fact

5.12
,
\mathrm{FG}(1)
amounts to
Proposition

2.35
.

(i)

Note that
\mathrm{FG}(<\lambda)
implies that
\mathsf{Scat}_{<\lambda}
is
bqo
by
Proposition

3.19
, and so
\mathrm{FG}(\lambda+1)
follows from
Theorems

5.11
and
5.12
.

(ii)

Recall that
\mathsf{G}_{\lambda+k}\subseteq\mathsf{G}_{\lambda+k+1}
for all
k<\omega
, so
G_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{k\leq n}G_{\lambda+k}
.

(iii)

This follows from
Propositions

2.33
and
3.19
,

(iv)

This follows from the previous item combined with
Theorem

4.8
.

(v)

Observe that if
\alpha
is limit, there are no centered functions in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha}
and so the statement is vacuously true.
So suppose that
\alpha
is a successor ordinal.
Since by
\mathrm{FG}(<\alpha)
the set
\mathsf{G}_{\alpha-1}
finitely generates
\mathsf{Scat}_{[\lambda,\alpha-1]}
, by
Theorem

4.10
any centered function
f\in\mathsf{Scat}_{[\lambda,\alpha]}
is
either
f\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\in\mathsf{G}_{\alpha}
or
f=\pgl F
for some nonempty finite set
F\subseteq\mathsf{G}_{\alpha-1}
.
We show that if
g\in\mathsf{G}_{\alpha-1}
is a wedge generator, then
\pgl g
is equivalent to some centered generator in
\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}
. Use
Proposition

5.13

(iv)
to get finite sets
G,H\subseteq\mathsf{C}_{\alpha-1}
with
\{\omega H\}\cup G\leq g
and
g\leq\omega H\oplus(\bigoplus G)
.
Note that both
G
and
H
are included in
\mathsf{C}_{\alpha-1}
and that
(g)^{\infty}
is equivalent by pieces to
(F_{g})^{\infty}
with
F_{g}=(G\cup\{\omega h\mid h\in H\})^{\infty}
. Hence
\pgl g
is equivalent to some function
h\in\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}
by
Proposition

3.7
.
Therefore, forming a set
F^{\prime}
where each wedge generator
g
in
F\subseteq\mathsf{G}_{\alpha-1}
is replaced by the functions in
F_{g}
, it follows that
\pgl F\equiv\pgl F^{\prime}
by
Proposition

3.7
,
as desired.

∎

6
Finite generation at double successors

In view of
Proposition

5.15
, it remains to show that
\mathrm{FG}(<\alpha+2)
implies
\mathrm{FG}(\alpha+2)
in order to complete the proof by induction of
Theorem

5.14
.
To do so, writing
\alpha=\lambda+n
with
\lambda
limit and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we can can rely on a decomposition in centered functions, which are either in
\mathsf{Scat}_{<\lambda}
or in
\mathsf{C}_{\alpha+2}
, up to equivalence.
The situation is more complex than for successor of limits, however we can actually assume that they are all in
\mathsf{C}_{\alpha+2}
thanks to
Corollary

3.20

(ii)
(see
Lemma

6.3
).
For this general case, we show that partitions in centered functions can be made particularly well-behaved. We then analyze two gradually more complex situations before we define solvable functions. We finally show that solvable functions are equivalent to finite gluings of our generators and how to reduce the general case to that of solvable functions.

Note that this strategy departs from the one we used at successors of limit (
Theorem

5.11
) which relied on two structural properties of continuous reducibility specific to these levels. First, by
Corollary

4.11
there are up to equivalence only two centered functions: the minimum and the maximum among simple functions, which makes them comparable.
Second, the only generator at this level that requires the wedge operation,
namely
\textstyle{\bigvee}(\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\mid\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1})
, is actually of infinite
\CB
-degree.

6.1
Fine partitions in centered functions

We introduce some terminology and notations for partitions in centered functions.

Given topological spaces
A
and
B
, a
c
-partition
of a function
f:A\to B
in
\mathsf{Scat}
is a clopen partition
\mathsf{P}\subseteq\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{1}(A)
of
A

such that for all
P\in\mathsf{P}
the restriction
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}
is centered with cocenter denoted by
y_{P}
.

Let
\mathsf{P}
be a
c
-partition for
f
. We let
Y_{\mathsf{P}}=\{y_{P}\mid P\in\mathsf{P}\}\subseteq B
be the (countable) set of all cocenters of the functions
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}
for
P\in\mathsf{P}
.
For a centered function
g
and
y\in Y_{\mathsf{P}}
, let
\mathsf{P}_{g,y}=\{P\in\mathsf{P}\mid f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\equiv g\text{ and }y_{P}=y\}
and
f_{(g,y)}=\bigsqcup_{P\in\mathsf{P}_{g,y}}f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}
.

6.1.1
Dissolving lumps

Let
f\in\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha}
for
\alpha=\lambda+n
with
\lambda
limit and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We define
\mathsf{W}_{\alpha}=\{\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\}\cup\{\omega h\mid h\in\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}\}
. We say that
f
is
\mathsf{W}
-regular
at
y\in B
if for all
h\in\mathsf{W}_{\alpha}
the set
\{j\in\mathbb{N}\mid h\leq f^{(y,j)}\}
is either empty or infinite.

Given a
c
-partition
\mathsf{P}
of
f
, a
\mathsf{P}
-lump
is a pair
(g,y)
with
y\in Y_{\mathsf{P}}
and
g
centered such that
f_{(g,y)}
is not
\mathsf{W}
-regular at
y
.
The
rank
of the
\mathsf{P}
-lump is the rank
\CB(g)
of
g
, which is equal to
\CB(f_{(g,y)})
.

Remark 6.1
.

A centered function
f
is
\mathsf{W}
-regular at its cocenter
y
, because for all
j,m
we have
f^{(y,j)}\leq\bigoplus_{m=i}^{M}f^{(y,i)}
for some
M>m
by
Proposition

4.5
. Hence if
\omega h\leq f^{(y,j)}
for some
h\in\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}
, then
\omega h\leq f^{(y,m)}
for arbitrarily large
m
by
Lemma

5.9
. Similarly for
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}=\bigoplus_{n}\mathsf{l}_{\beta_{n}}
with
(\beta_{n})_{n}
cofinal in
\lambda
. Therefore
f_{(g,y)}
is not centered when
(g,y)
is a lump.
In particular, it follows that if
(g,y)
is a lump, then
\mathsf{P}_{g,y}
is infinite. Because if
\mathsf{P}_{g,y}
is finite, then
f_{(g,y)}\equiv g
is centered, hence
\mathsf{W}
-regular. To see this assume that
g\equiv\pgl_{n}g_{n}
for a monotone sequence
(g_{n})_{n}
by
Theorem

4.7
. For all
P\in\mathsf{P}_{g,y}
, the sequence of rays of
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}
along
y
is reducible by pieces to
(g_{n})_{n}
by
Proposition

4.5
. Now each ray of
f_{(g,y)}
at
y
is the finite disjoint union of corresponding rays of
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}
, for
P\in\mathsf{P}_{g,y}
. So the rays of
f_{(g,y)}
at
y
are reducible by pieces to
(g_{n})_{n}
. Therefore
g\leq f_{(g,y)}\leq\pgl_{n}(f_{(g,y)})^{(y,n)}\leq\pgl_{n}g_{n}
by
Proposition

3.7
.

Recall that a partition
\mathsf{P}^{\prime}
is
finer
than a partition
\mathsf{P}
if every
P^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}^{\prime}
is included in some
P\in\mathsf{P}
.
A
c
-partition presenting a lump can be refined to
dissolve
that lump.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\alpha<\omega_{1}
and assume
\mathrm{FG}(<\alpha)
. Let
f\in\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha}
and
\mathsf{P}
a
c
-partition for
f
.

If
(g,y)
is a
\mathsf{P}
-lump of rank
\beta\leq\alpha
, then there exists a finer
c
-partition
\mathsf{P}^{\prime}
such that
(g,y)
is not a
\mathsf{P}^{\prime}
-lump,

\mathsf{P}\setminus\mathsf{P}_{(g,y)}\subseteq\mathsf{P}^{\prime}
, and every
\mathsf{P}^{\prime}
-lump is either a
\mathsf{P}
-lump or has rank
<\beta
.

Proof.

Suppose that
(g,y)
is a
\mathsf{P}
-lump of rank
\beta
, namely that
h=f_{(g,y)}
is not
\mathsf{W}
-regular.
Note that
\CB(h)=\CB(g)=\beta
by
Corollary

2.16
.
There exists
w\in\mathsf{W}_{\beta}
such that
J_{w}=\{j\in\mathbb{N}\mid w\leq h^{(j)}\}
is finite but non-empty.
Since
\mathsf{W}_{\beta}
is finite, there exists
J\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
w\in\mathsf{W}_{\beta}
either
J_{w}\subseteq J
or
J_{w}
is infinite. Let
U=N_{y\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{J+1}}
and note that
h\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
is
\mathsf{W}
-regular.

Now for all
P\in\mathsf{P}_{(g,y)}
, let
P^{\prime}=P\cap f^{-1}(U)
and
A_{P}=P\setminus P^{\prime}
. Note that while
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P^{\prime}}\equiv g
by centeredness and we have
\CB(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{P}})<\CB(g)=\beta\leq\alpha
by
Theorem

4.7
.
So by
\mathrm{FG}(<\alpha)
we can use
Proposition

5.15
to get
\mathsf{P}_{P}
a
c
-partition of
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{P}}
with functions of
\CB
-rank
<\beta
.
Our new
c
-partition for
f
is given by

\mathsf{P}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{P}\setminus\mathsf{P}_{g,y})\cup\{P^{\prime}\mid P\in\mathsf{P}_{g,y}\}\cup\bigcup\{\mathsf{P}_{P}\mid P\in\mathsf{P}_{g,y}\}\,.

Note that
\mathsf{P}^{\prime}_{g,y}=\{P^{\prime}\mid P\in\mathsf{P}_{g,y}\}
and
\bigcup\mathsf{P}^{\prime}_{g,y}=f^{-1}(U)\cap\bigcup\mathsf{P}_{g,y}
, so
(g,y)
is not a
\mathsf{P}^{\prime}
-lump. Moreover for every
Q\in\mathsf{P}^{\prime}\setminus\mathsf{P}
if
Q\notin\mathsf{P}^{\prime}_{g,y}
then
\CB(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{Q})<\CB(g)=\beta
holds, which proves the statement.
∎

6.1.2
Gobbling up small functions

Writing
\alpha=\lambda+n
with
\lambda
limit and
n
finite, note that a
c
-partition of
f\in\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha}
may contain a clopen
P
such that
\CB(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P})<\lambda
.
When
\alpha
is a double successor, that is when
n>1
, the parts of
\CB
-rank bigger than
\lambda+1
can gobble up those of rank
<\lambda
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\lambda<\omega_{1}
be limit and
f\in\mathsf{Scat}
.
Assume that
f=f_{0}\sqcup f_{1}
with
f_{0}
centered,
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq f_{0}
and
f_{1}\leq\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
. Then
f
is centered and
f\equiv f_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
x
be a center for
f_{0}
and let
U\ni x
be clopen in
\dom(f_{0})
. Setting
f_{U}=f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{U}
, we have
f_{U}\leq f_{0}
and we show that
f\leq f_{U}
.

Since
x
is a center for
f_{0}
, we have
f_{0}\leq f_{U}
and in particular
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq f_{U}
as witnessed by some reduction
(\sigma,\tau)
.
Let
\tilde{y}=f\sigma((0)^{\infty})
and
y=f(x)
. If
\tilde{y}\neq y
, choose a clopen neighborhood
V\subseteq\im(f)
of
\tilde{y}
not containing
y
.
Otherwise, set
V=\tau^{-1}(N_{(1)})
, notice that
V
is a clopen set with
y\notin V
, and such that
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq f_{U}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}
as witnessed by restricting
(\sigma,\tau)
.
Now let
W\ni y
be clopen and disjoint from
V
. By centeredness of
f_{0}
, we have
f_{0}\leq f_{U}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{W}
. Moreover
f_{1}\leq\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq f_{U}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}
. Hence by
Propositions

2.25
and
2.27
, we get

f\leq f_{0}\oplus f_{1}\leq f_{U}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{W}\oplus f_{U}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}\leq f_{U}.\qed

6.1.3
Fine
c
-partitions.

We now define the partitions with all the required properties.

Definition 6.4
.

Let
f\in\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda+n+1}
for
\lambda
limit and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We say that a
c
-partition
\mathsf{P}
of
f
is
fine
if there are no
\mathsf{P}
-lumps and
\CB(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P})>\lambda
for all
P\in\mathsf{P}
.

We now show that
\mathrm{FG}(<\alpha)
yields the existence of fine
c
-partitions when
\alpha
is a double successor.
To do so, we take any
c
-partition and start by dissolving all lumps of large rank before gobbling up any small pieces that it may contain.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
\alpha=\lambda+n+2
with
\lambda<\omega_{1}
limit,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and assume
\mathrm{FG}(<\alpha)
.
Then every function in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha}
admits a fine
c
-partition.

Proof.

Take
f\in\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha}
, and start by defining a sequence
(\mathsf{P}_{i})_{i\leq n+2}
of
c
-partitions of
f
such that all
\mathsf{P}_{i}
-lumps have rank
\leq\lambda+n+2-i
.
By
\mathrm{FG}(<\alpha)
,
f
admits a
c
-partition
\mathsf{P}_{0}
by
Proposition

5.15
.

Suppose by induction that
\mathsf{P}_{i}
is defined for some
i\leq n+2
and fix an enumeration
(g_{j},y_{j})_{j\in J}
for
J
an initial segment of
\mathbb{N}
of all
\mathsf{P}_{i}
-lumps of rank
\lambda+n+2-i
.
We define by induction a sequence
(\mathsf{P}^{\prime}_{j})_{j\in J}
of
c
-partitions, starting with
\mathsf{P}^{\prime}_{-1}=\mathsf{P}_{i}
. For all
j\in J
, let
\mathsf{P}^{\prime}_{j+1}
be a
c
-partition refining
\mathsf{P}^{\prime}_{j}
and dissolving the
\mathsf{P}_{i}
-lump
(g_{j},y_{j})
by an application of
Lemma

6.2
.
The limit inferior
\mathsf{P}_{i+1}=\bigcup_{k\in J}\bigcap_{k\leq j\in J}\mathsf{P}^{\prime}_{j}
is the desired
c
-partition.
Note that all
\mathsf{P}_{n+2}
-lumps have rank
<\lambda
. Moreover since
\CB(f)\geq\lambda+2
we have
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq f
by
Corollary

3.20

(ii)
. Therefore
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}
for some
P\in\mathsf{P}_{n+2}
by
Fact

4.3

(iii)
.
So letting
\tilde{\mathsf{P}}=\{Q\in\mathsf{P}_{n+2}\mid f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{Q}\leq\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\}
and
R=\bigcup\tilde{\mathsf{P}}
,
then
\CB(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P\cup R})\geq\CB(\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda})>\lambda
and by
Lemma

6.3

f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P\sqcup R}
is centered and
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P\sqcup R}\equiv f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}
.
Therefore the
c
-partition
\mathsf{P}
obtained from
\mathsf{P}_{n+2}
by replacing
P
and elements of
\tilde{\mathsf{P}}
by
P\cup R
is a fine
c
-partition.
∎

6.2
Pseudo-centered functions

Assuming
\mathrm{FG}(<\alpha+2)
any function in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha+2}
admits a fine
c
-partition
\mathsf{P}
by
Proposition

6.5
.
Hence every
P\in\mathsf{P}
is associated with both a centered function in
\mathsf{C}_{\alpha+2}
and a point
y_{P}
in the codomain of
f
.
The difficulty in proving that
f
is equivalent to a finite gluing of generators in
\mathsf{G}_{\alpha+2}

resides in the potentially complex combinatorial and topological structure of the combination of the mappings
\mathsf{P}\to\mathsf{C}_{\alpha+2}
and
\mathsf{P}\to\im f
.
Before tackling the general case, we deal with ubiquitous situations that are easier to handle.
The first one consists of a disjoint union of the same centered function with only one cocenter,
in other words (using the notations of
Subsection

6.1
) a function
f=f_{(g,y)}
for some centered
g
and
y\in\im(f)
.

Definition 6.6
.

We say that
f\in\mathsf{Scat}
together with a fine
c
-partition is
pseudo-centered
at
y
if
Y_{\mathsf{P}}
is a singleton
\{y\}
and for all
P,P^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}
we have
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\equiv f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P^{\prime}}
.

The following result generalizes
Theorem

4.13
to functions whose
\CB
-rank is a double successor.
The strategy of proof is an elaboration on the successor of limit case.

Theorem 6.7

(Vertical theorem)
.

Let
\alpha<\omega_{1}
and assume
\mathrm{FG}(\leq\alpha+1)
.
Let
f:A\to B
in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha+2}
be pseudo-centered at
y
.
There exist
g\in\mathsf{C}_{\alpha+2}
and
H\subseteq\mathsf{W}_{\alpha+1}
such that
for all clopen neighborhood
U
of
y
there is a clopen set
W\subseteq U
and a clopen partition
A=A^{0}\sqcup A^{1}
such that

(i)

y\notin W
and
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{0}}\leq\bigoplus H\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{W}
,

(ii)

for all clopen set
V\ni y
,
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{1}}\leq g\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}
(in fact
g\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{1}}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}
),

(iii)

\bigoplus H\leq g
, so in particular
f\leq g\bigoplus g
.

Proof.

Fix a fine
c
-partition
\mathsf{P}
such that
Y_{\mathsf{P}}=\{y\}
and some centered
\hat{f}
with
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\equiv\hat{f}
for all
P\in\mathsf{P}
.
In this proof, all rays are taken with respect to
y
, so that
f^{(j)}
denotes the rays of
f
at
y
. Note that
\CB(f^{(j)})\leq\alpha+1
.
Since
\alpha+2=\CB(f)=\CB(\hat{f})\neq\lambda+1
for any limit
\lambda\leq\alpha
, we have
\hat{f}>\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
,
so
\hat{f}\equiv\pgl G
for some non-empty set
G
of
\mathsf{C}_{\alpha+1}
by
Proposition

5.15
and so
\pgl G\in\mathsf{C}_{\alpha+2}
.
Note that for all clopen set
V\ni y
,
\pgl G\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}
, because for any
P\in\mathsf{P}
we have
\pgl G\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}
.

If for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
we have
f^{(j)}\leq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(G)
, then
f\leq\pgl_{j}f^{(j)}\leq\pgl G\equiv g
by
Proposition

3.7
.
Observe that this happens automatically if
\mathsf{P}
is finite.
In this case, we can set
w=\emptyset
,
W=\emptyset
,
A^{0}=\emptyset
and
A^{1}=A
.

Otherwise,
\mathsf{P}
must be infinite and we consider the set

H=\{h\in\mathsf{W}_{\alpha+1}\mid h\nleq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(G)\text{ and }\exists j\in\mathbb{N}\;h\leq f^{(j)}\}

We let
w=\bigoplus H
and we claim that for all clopen
U\ni y
we have
w\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{W}
for some clopen
W
with
y\notin W\subseteq U
.

To see this, let
h\in H
. Since
(f,y)
is not a
\mathsf{P}
-lump, we must have
h\leq f^{(j)}
for infinitely many
j\in\mathbb{N}
.
Suppose that
U\ni y
is clopen, and therefore contains all but finitely many rays
B^{(j)}
.
As
H
is a subset of the finite set
\mathsf{W}_{\alpha+1}=\{\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\}\cup\omega\{\mathsf{C}_{\alpha+1}\}
by
Proposition

5.13
, there is a finite set
J\subseteq\mathbb{N}
such that for all
h\in H
there exists
j\in J
with

h\leq f^{(j)}=f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{B^{(j)}}
and
\bigcup_{j\in J}B^{(j)}\subseteq U
.
Setting
W=\bigcup_{j\in J}B^{(j)}
and
w\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{W}
by
Lemma

5.9
.

Next we show that
w\leq\pgl G
. Note that
f^{(j)}=\bigsqcup_{P\in\mathsf{P}}f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}^{(j)}
and
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}^{(j)}\leq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(G)
by
Proposition

4.5
and so
f^{(j)}\leq\omega G
for all
j
.
Hence by definition of
H
,
h\leq\omega G
for
h\in H
and thus
w\leq\omega G
. Since
\omega G\leq\pgl G
by
Fact

3.4
, it follows that
w\leq\pgl G
.

It remains to find a clopen partition
A=A^{0}\sqcup A^{1}
such that
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{0}}\leq w
and
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{1}}\leq g
.
Fix an enumeration
(A_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\mathsf{P}
, let
f_{i}=f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{i}}
, and for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
set
f^{[j]}=\bigsqcup_{i>j}f_{i}^{(j)}
. We prove the following:

f
B
A
f
B
A
A^{0}
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
f_{0}
f_{1}
f_{2}
f^{(1)}
f^{(0)}
f^{(2)}
f^{(3)}
\cdots
\cdots
\leq g\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.56,0.89}\omega H}\leq
\cdots
\cdots
y
U
W
y
B^{(0)}
B^{(1)}
B^{(2)}
f_{0}
V

Figure 3:
Proof of the Vertical Theorem.

Claim 6.8
.

For all
j\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a clopen partition
(A^{0}_{j},A^{1}_{j})
of
\dom(f^{[j]})

such that:
f^{[j]}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{0}_{j}}\leq w
and
f^{[j]}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{1}_{j}}\leq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(G)
.

Proof.

Let
j\in\mathbb{N}
, we show that
f^{[j]}\leq w\oplus h
for some
h\in\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(G)
.
By
Proposition

2.25
, this grants us with the desired clopen partition
(A^{0}_{j},A^{1}_{j})
of
\dom(f^{[j]})
.

Since
\CB(f^{[j]})<\alpha+2
, either
\CB(f^{[j]})<\lambda
or by
\mathrm{FG}(\leq\alpha+1)
it is equivalent to a finite gluing of functions in
\mathsf{G}_{\alpha+1}
.
In the former case
f^{[j]}\leq G
by
Theorem

3.17
and we are done.
In the latter case, we can write
f^{[j]}=\bigoplus_{i\leq k}g_{i}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
g_{i}\in\mathsf{G}_{\alpha+1}
.
So in order to prove the claim, as
\mathsf{G}_{\alpha+1}
is finite, it suffices to show that for every generator
g\in\mathsf{G}_{\alpha+1}
if
g\leq f^{[j]}
, then
g\leq w\oplus h
for some
h\in\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(G)
.
So let
g\in\mathsf{G}_{\alpha+1}
with
g\leq f^{[j]}
, we distinguish three cases according to the definition of
\mathsf{G}_{\alpha+1}
:

(i)

Suppose that
g\in\mathsf{C}_{\alpha+1}
, so
g
is centered. Since
f^{[j]}=\bigsqcup_{i>j}f_{i}^{(j)}\leq\bigoplus_{i>j}f_{i}^{(j)}
by
Proposition

2.25

and
f_{i}^{(j)}\leq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(G)
for all
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
by
Proposition

4.5
, we actually have
g\leq G
by
Fact

4.3
.

(ii)

Suppose that
g\in\mathsf{W}_{\alpha+1}
. By definition of
H
, we have either
g\leq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(G)
or, since
g\leq f^{[j]}\leq f^{(j)}
,
g\in H
and so
g\leq w
.

(iii)

Finally
14
14

14

Here we see the specificity of double successors!
,
it can be that
g
is a wedge generator, so use
Proposition

5.13
to get
F_{0},F_{1}\subseteq\mathsf{C}_{\alpha+1}
such that
F_{0},\omega F_{1}\leq g
and
g\leq(\bigoplus F_{0})\oplus\omega F_{1}

Since
F_{0}\leq f^{[j]}
, we have
F_{0}\leq G
by the first case.
Since
\omega F_{1}\leq f^{[j]}
, either
\omega F_{1}\leq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(G)
or
\omega\{F_{1}\}\subseteq H
by definition of
H
, and so
\omega F_{1}\leq\bigoplus H=w
.
This shows that for some
h\in\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(G)
we have

g\leq\bigoplus\omega F_{1}\oplus F_{0}\leq w\oplus h,

as desired.∎

We let
A^{0}=\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}A^{0}_{j}
and
A^{1}=A\setminus A^{0}
;

A^{0}
is open as union of the sets
A^{0}_{j}
which are open, we prove
A^{1}
is also open.
Whenever
j\geq i
, we have
A_{i}\cap\dom(f^{[j]})=\emptyset
and so for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
the set
A_{i}\cap A^{0}=A_{i}\cap\bigcup_{j<i}A^{0}_{j}
is clopen, hence so is
A_{i}\setminus A^{0}
.
Therefore
A^{1}=A\setminus A^{0}=\bigcup_{i}A_{i}\setminus A^{0}
is open.

On the one hand,

f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{0}}\leq\bigoplus_{j}f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{0}_{j}}\leq\omega w\equiv w.

On the other hand, for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
we have by
Proposition

2.25

f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{1}}^{(j)}=f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{1}_{j}}\sqcup\bigsqcup_{i\leq j}f_{i}^{(j)}\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{1}_{j}}\oplus\bigoplus_{i\leq j}f_{i}^{(j)}\leq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(G),

where the last reduction follows from the claim and the fact that
f_{i}^{(j)}\leq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(G)
for all
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
. Therefore
(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{1}}^{(j)})_{j}
is piecewise reducible to
(\bigoplus G)^{\infty}
, so by
Proposition

3.7

f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{1}}\leq\pgl G
, which concludes the proof.
∎

6.3
Strongly solvable functions

For a fine
c
-partition of a function
f
, we denote by
\mathsf{P}^{\neq y}=\{P\in\mathsf{P}\mid y_{P}\neq y\}
the members of the partition whose cocenters are distinct from
y
.
Now that we dealt with the case of a unique cocenter
y
, we next analyze the situation where
cocenters
\{y_{P}\mid P\in\mathsf{P}^{\neq y}\}
converge to
y
and the
\{f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\mid P\in\mathsf{P}^{\neq y}\}
also enjoys a nice combinatorial property.

Definition 6.9
.

We say that the function
f:A\to B
together with a fine
c
-partition
\mathsf{P}
is
strongly solvable
at
y\in Y_{\mathsf{P}}
if for all clopen neighborhood
V
of
y

(i)

the set
\{y_{P}\mid P\in\mathsf{P}\text{ and }y_{P}\notin V\}
is finite, and

(ii)

for all
P\in\mathsf{P}^{\neq y}
there exists
Q\in\mathsf{P}^{\neq y}
with
y_{Q}\in V
and
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{Q}
.

Theorem 6.10

(Diagonal Theorem)
.

Assume
\mathrm{FG}(\leq\alpha+1)
for
\alpha<\omega_{1}
.
Let
f:A\to B
in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha+2}
be strongly solvable at
y
.
Then there exists
g\in\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(\mathsf{G}_{\alpha+2})
such that
f\leq g\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
for all clopen set
U\ni y
.

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{P}
be a fine
c
-partition for
f
and
\bar{y}\in Y_{\mathsf{P}}
witness that
f
is strongly solvable at
\bar{y}
.
Let
\mathsf{P}_{M}=\mathsf{P}\setminus\mathsf{P}^{\neq\bar{y}}
and note that if
\mathsf{P}^{\neq\bar{y}}
is non-empty, then
Y^{\prime}=\{y_{P}\mid P\in\mathsf{P}\}\setminus\{\bar{y\}}
is an infinite discrete subset of
B
by strong solvability of
f
.
We write
\alpha=\lambda+n
with
\lambda
limit and
n\in\mathbb{N}
and distinguish two cases.

First case
:
\CB(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P})=\lambda+1
for all
P\in\mathsf{P}_{M}
. Since
\CB(f)=\alpha+2
, there exists
P\in\mathsf{P}_{D}
with
\CB(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P})=\alpha+2
by
Corollary

2.16
.
Choose a set of representatives
D\subseteq\mathsf{C}_{\alpha+2}
for
\{f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\mid P\in\mathsf{P}_{D}\}
by
Proposition

5.15
and pick
h\in D
with
\CB(h)=\alpha+2
.
We show that in that case we can set
g:=\omega D
.
By
Propositions

3.12
and
3.20
we have
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\leq\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq h
for all
P\in\mathsf{P}_{M}
,
so using
Propositions

2.25
and
2.26
we get:

f\leq\bigoplus_{P\in\mathsf{P}}f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\leq\bigoplus_{P\in\mathsf{P}_{D}}f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\oplus\bigoplus_{P\in\mathsf{P}_{M}}f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\leq\omega D\oplus\omega\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq\omega D.

Now fix any clopen set
U\ni\bar{y}
. Since
f
is strongly solvable at
\bar{y}
, for all
g\in D
the set
\{y_{P}\mid P\in\mathsf{P}_{D}\text{ and }y_{P}\in U\text{ and }g\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\}
is infinite. So by
Lemma

5.9
we have
\omega D\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
, which concludes the proof in this case.

Second case
:
\CB(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P})>\lambda+1
for some
P\in\mathsf{P}_{M}
.
Let
\mathsf{P}^{\prime}_{M}=\{P\in\mathsf{P}_{M}\mid\CB(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P})>\lambda+1\}
and choose a finite set of representatives

M\subseteq\mathsf{C}_{\alpha+2}
for
\{f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\mid P\in\mathsf{P}^{\prime}_{M}\}
by
Proposition

5.15
.
For each
g\in M
, let
M_{g}\subseteq\mathsf{C}_{\alpha+1}\cup\omega\{\alpha+1\}
such that
g=\pgl M_{g}
and let
A_{g}=\bigcup\{P\in\mathsf{P}^{\prime}_{M}\mid f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\equiv g\}
.

For all
g\in M
the function
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{g}}
is pseudo-centered, so by
Theorem

6.7
there exists
H_{g}\subseteq\mathsf{C}_{\alpha+1}
,
a clopen
W_{g}\subseteq U
and a clopen partition
A_{g}=A^{0}_{g}\sqcup A^{1}_{g}
such that: first,
\bar{y}\notin W_{g}
and
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{0}_{g}}\leq\omega H_{g}\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{0}_{g}}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{W_{g}}
,
and second for all clopen
V\ni\bar{y}
we have
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{1}_{g}}\leq g\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{1}_{g}}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}
(so in particular,
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{1}_{g}}\equiv g
).

If
\mathsf{P}_{D}
is empty, set
D=\emptyset
. Otherwise, as in the previous case, let
D\subseteq\mathsf{C}_{\alpha+2}
be a set of representatives for
\{f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\mid P\in\mathsf{P}^{\neq\bar{y}}\}

by
Proposition

5.15
. Set
w=\bigoplus_{g\in M}\omega H_{g}
. We claim that for all clopen set
U\ni\bar{y}
we have

f\leq w\oplus\textstyle{\bigvee}((M_{g})_{g\in M}\mid D)\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U},

which concludes the proof, once we prove the existence of the two reductions.

Right reduction
:
w\oplus\textstyle{\bigvee}((M_{g})_{g\in M}\mid D)\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
.
Let
W=\bigcup_{g\in M}W_{g}
and note that by
Lemma

5.9
we have
w\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{W}
.
For each
g\in M
, we choose some
P_{g}\in\mathsf{P}_{M}
such that
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P_{g}}\equiv g
. We define
x_{g}
to be any center for
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P_{g}}
.
Since
W
is clopen and
\bar{y}\notin W
, we can pick a clopen
V\ni\bar{y}
disjoint from
W
and included in
U
.

We actually show that
\textstyle{\bigvee}((M_{g})_{g\in M}\mid D)\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}
by verifying
the two conditions in the premice of
Lemma

5.6
for
\bar{y}
and the points
x_{g}
.

For the first condition, we need to show that for all
g\in M
, for every open
U^{\prime}\ni x_{g}
and all
h\in M_{g}

there exists
(\sigma,\tau)
reducing
h
to
f
with
\im(\sigma)\subseteq U^{\prime}
and
\overline{\im(f\sigma)}\notowner\bar{y}
.
Since
x_{g}\in P_{g}\cap U^{\prime}
is a center for
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P_{g}}\equiv g
, there is a reduction
(\sigma,\tau)
from
g
to
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P_{g}\cap U^{\prime}}
by
Fact

4.3

(i)
.
But the cocenter of
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P_{g}\cap U^{\prime}}
is
\bar{y}
so by
Proposition

4.5
we obtain that the desired reduction by restricting
(\sigma,\tau)
.

To verify the second condition, we need to show that
for every open
U^{\prime}\ni\bar{y}
, there exists
(\sigma,\tau)
reducing
\bigoplus D
to
f
with
\im(f\sigma)\subseteq U^{\prime}
and
\overline{\im(f\sigma)}\notowner\bar{y}
.
It is enough to find such a continuous reduction from
g
to
f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U^{\prime}}
for every
g\in D
, so let
g\in D
.
Because
f
is strongly solvable in
U
, there exists
P\in\mathsf{P}_{D}
such that
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\equiv g
and
y_{P}\in U^{\prime}
.
As
y_{P}\neq\bar{y}
, we can pick a clopen neighbourhood
V^{\prime}\subseteq U^{\prime}
of
y_{P}
that does not include
\bar{y}
.
Since
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}
is centered with cocenter
y_{P}
we have
g\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V^{\prime}}
, which gives the desired reduction.

Altogether we obtain by
Proposition

2.25
:

w\oplus\textstyle{\bigvee}((M_{g})_{g\in M}\mid D)\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{W}\oplus f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}.

Left reduction:

f\leq w\oplus\textstyle{\bigvee}((M_{g})_{g\in M}\mid D)
.
Let
A^{0}=\bigsqcup_{g\in M}A^{0}_{g}
, note that
A^{0}
is clopen (since
M
is finite) and
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{0}}\leq w
by
Proposition

2.25
.
We show that for
A^{1}=A\setminus A^{0}
we have
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{1}}\leq\textstyle{\bigvee}((M_{g})_{g\in M}\mid D)
by verifying the hypotheses of
Proposition

5.4
.
For all
g\in M
we have
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{1}_{g}}\equiv g=\pgl M_{g}
and the cocenter of
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{1}_{g}}
is
\bar{y}
.
By
Proposition

4.5
this implies that the sequence of rays
((f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{1}_{g}})^{(\bar{y},j)})_{j}
is reducible by finite pieces to
(M_{g})^{\infty}
.

In case there is
P\in\mathsf{P}_{M}
with
\CB(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P})=\lambda+1
, let
A^{1}_{0}=\bigcup(\mathsf{P}_{M}\setminus\mathsf{P}^{\prime}_{M})
.
Notice that
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{1}_{0}}
is a simple function of rank
\lambda+1
and distinguished point
\bar{y}
by
Proposition

4.12
. So for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\CB((f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{1}_{0}})^{(\bar{y},j)})\leq\lambda
and by
Theorem

3.17
we get
(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{1}_{0}})^{(\bar{y},j)}\leq\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
.
Recall that any
g\in M
has
\CB
-rank at least
\lambda+2
, so for some
h\in M_{g}
we have
\CB(h)\geq\lambda+1

and in turn
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq h
by
Theorem

3.17
again.
This means that we can safely replace
A^{1}_{g}
by
A^{1}_{g}\sqcup A^{1}_{0}
for some
g\in M
, since
((f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{1}_{g}})^{(\bar{y},j)})_{j}
still reduces by pieces to
(M_{g})^{\infty}
.

If
\mathsf{P}^{\neq\bar{y}}
is empty and so
D=\emptyset
, then
(A^{1}_{g})_{g}\in M
partitions
A^{1}
and so
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{1}}\leq\textstyle{\bigvee}((M_{g})_{g\in M}\mid\emptyset)
by
Proposition

5.4
.

Otherwise, we have
A^{1}=(\bigsqcup_{g\in M}A^{1}_{g})\sqcup A^{D}
with
A^{D}=\bigcup\{P\mid P\in\mathsf{P}^{\neq\bar{y}}\}
, so we need to partition
A^{D}
into an infinite sequence of clopen sets
(A^{D}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

that is reducible by pieces to
(D)^{\infty}
and such that
f(A^{D}_{n})\to\bar{y}
.

Fix any enumeration
(y_{n})_{n\geq 1}
of
Y^{\prime}
and set
A_{n}=\bigcup\{P\in\mathsf{P}\mid y_{p}=y_{n}\}
for all
n\geq 1
.
For all
n\geq 1
let
k_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
y_{n}\in B^{(\bar{y},k_{n})}
and define
A^{D}_{n}=A_{n}\cap f^{-1}(B^{(\bar{y},k_{n})})
and
A^{0}_{n}=A_{n}\setminus A^{D}_{n}
. Finally let
A^{D}_{0}=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}A^{0}_{n}
. We have seen that strong solvability implies
y_{n}\to\bar{y}
, so
k_{n}\to\infty
, and in turn
f(A^{D}_{n})\to\bar{y}
.

To conclude using
Proposition

5.4
, it only remains to prove that
(A^{D}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is reducible by finite pieces to
(D)^{\infty}
, or equivalently that
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{D}_{n}}\leq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(D)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
By our definition, we need to distinguish two cases.

Case
n>0
.
We actually show the stronger relation
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{n}}\leq 2(\bigoplus D)
.
We partition
A_{n}=\bigsqcup_{g\in D}A^{g}_{n}
where
A^{g}_{n}=\bigcup\{P\in\mathsf{P}\mid y_{P}=y_{n}\text{ and }f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\equiv g\}
.
For all
g\in D
,
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{g}_{n}}
is pseudo-centered, so by
Theorem

6.7
we have
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{g}_{n}}\leq 2g
.
Therefore, by
Proposition

2.25
,

f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A_{n}}\leq 2(\bigoplus D).

Case
n=0
.
We finally prove that
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{D}_{0}}\leq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(D)
. Note that
A^{D}_{0}=\bigsqcup_{P\in\mathsf{P}_{D}}P\cap A^{D}_{0}
. Let
P\in\mathsf{P}^{\neq\bar{y}}
,
k
be such that
y_{P}\in B^{(\bar{y},k)}
and
g\in D
with
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\equiv g
. Note that by definition
h:=f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P\cap A^{D}_{0}}=f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{B\setminus B^{(\bar{y},k)}}
.If
g\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
, then as
y_{P}
is the distinguished point of
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}
we have
\CB(h)<\lambda
and so in particular
h\leq\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
. Otherwise,
g\equiv\pgl D_{g}
for some subset
D_{g}\subseteq\mathsf{C}_{\alpha+1}\cup\omega\{\mathsf{C}_{\alpha+1}\}
and so
h\leq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(D_{g})
by
Corollary

4.6
. Since
D
is not empty and for any
g\in D
we have
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq g
, we obtain:

f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{D}_{0}}\leq\bigoplus_{P\in\mathsf{P}_{D}}f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P\cap A^{D}_{y}}\leq\bigoplus_{g\in D}\omega D_{g}\leq\bigoplus_{g\in D}\pgl D_{g}\equiv\bigoplus D

where we used that for each
g\in D
we have
\omega D_{g}\leq\pgl D_{g}=g
by
Fact

3.4
.
∎

6.4
Solvable functions

We finally introduce the weaker notion of
solvable functions
by dropping the first requirement in the definition of strongly solvable functions.
We show that we can reduce the general case to the case of solvable functions and then we show how solvable functions can be expressed in terms of generators.

Definition 6.11
.

We say that the function
f:A\to B
is
solvable
with a fine
c
-partition
\mathsf{P}
if for some
y\in Y_{\mathsf{P}}
, for all
P\in\mathsf{P}^{\neq y}
and all clopen
V\ni y
there exists
Q\in\mathsf{P}^{\neq y}
with
y_{Q}\in V
and
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{Q}
.

Note that if
f
is solvable with
\mathsf{P}
, then either
Y_{\mathsf{P}}
is a singleton or it is infinite.
If
\mathsf{P}
is a fine
c
-partition for a function
f:A\to B
and
U
is a clopen subset of
B
,
we define the corestriction
\mathsf{P}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}=\{P\in\mathsf{P}\mid y_{P}\in U\}
and we let
A^{U}_{\mathsf{P}}=\bigcup\mathsf{P}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
.
Note that
\mathsf{P}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
is a fine
c
-partition of
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{U}_{\mathsf{P}}}
, because any
\mathsf{P}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
-lump is a
\mathsf{P}
-lump.

Theorem 6.12
.

For
\alpha<\omega_{1}
assume
\mathrm{FG}(<\alpha+2)
and let
\mathsf{P}
be a fine
c
-partition of
f:A\to B
in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha+2}
.
Then there exists a countable family
\mathcal{U}
of pairwise disjoint clopen subsets of
B
such that:

(i)

Y_{\mathsf{P}}\subseteq\bigcup\mathcal{U}
, and

(ii)

for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
, the function
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{U}_{\mathsf{P}}}:A^{U}_{\mathsf{P}}\to B
is solvable with
\mathsf{P}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
.

Proof.

We start by observing that for every
y\in Y_{\mathsf{P}}
and every small enough neighborhood
U\ni y
the function
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{U}_{\mathsf{P}}}:A^{U}_{\mathsf{P}}\to B
is solvable in
\mathsf{P}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
.
To see this, fix a basis of clopen neighborhoods
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
at
y
and define:

D_{n}=\{g\in\mathsf{C}_{\alpha+2}\mid\exists P\in\mathsf{P}^{\neq y}\text{ and }y_{P}\in U_{n}\text{ and }f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\equiv g\}

Note that
m<n
implies
D_{m}\supseteq D_{n}
, therefore as
\mathsf{C}_{\alpha+2}
is finite this sequence must be eventually constant. So let
M\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
D_{M}=D_{n}
for all
n\geq M
and let
U=U_{M}
.
Clearly
\mathsf{P}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
is a fine
c
-partition of
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{U}_{\mathsf{P}}}
. Suppose that
P\in\mathsf{P}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
with
y_{P}\neq y
and let
V
be a clopen neighborhood of
y
. As we assume
\mathrm{FG}(<\alpha+2)
, there exists
g\in\mathsf{C}_{\alpha+2}
with
g\equiv f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}
by
Proposition

5.15
, so
g\in D_{M}
. Moreover there exists
n>M
with
U_{n}\subseteq V
, hence as
D_{n}=D_{M}
, it follows that there exists
Q\in\mathsf{P}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
with
y_{Q}\in U_{n}\subseteq V
and
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{Q}\equiv f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}
.

Now fix an enumeration
(y_{n})_{n}
of
Y_{\mathsf{P}}
. We define by induction a sequence
(U_{k})_{k}
such that the sets
U_{k}
are pairwise disjoint clopen sets of
B
,
they cover
Y_{\mathsf{P}}
, and for all
k
the restriction
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{U_{k}}_{\mathsf{P}}}:A^{U_{k}}_{\mathsf{P}}\to B
is solvable in
\mathsf{P}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U_{k}}
.
Let
n_{0}=0
and use the observation to choose a neighborhood
U_{0}
of
y_{0}
such that
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{U_{0}}_{\mathsf{P}}}:A^{U_{0}}_{\mathsf{P}}\to B
is solvable in
\mathsf{P}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U_{0}}
. Assume that
(U_{l})_{l<k}
is defined with
U_{l}
clopen pairwise disjoint and
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{U_{l}}_{\mathsf{P}}}:A^{U_{l}}_{\mathsf{P}}\to B
is solvable in
\mathsf{P}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U_{l}}
for all
l<k
. If
Y_{P}\subseteq\bigcup_{l<k}U_{l}
we are done, otherwise let
n_{k}=\min\{n\mid y_{n}\notin\bigcup_{l<k}U_{l}\}

and using the observation again choose a small enough neighbourhood
U_{k}
disjoint from
\bigcup_{l<k}U_{l}
and such that
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{U_{k}}_{\mathsf{P}}}:A^{U_{k}}_{\mathsf{P}}\to B
is solvable in
\mathsf{P}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U_{k}}
.
∎

Remark 6.13
.

If
f
is strongly solvable at
y
then we saw that
f\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
for every neighborhood
U
of
y
.
In contrast, when
f
is solvable with
\mathsf{P}
we will show that
f\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
whenever
U
is a clopen set such that
Y_{\mathsf{P}}\subseteq U
.
Note that it would be possible to strengthen the notion of solvable functions to a notion of
solvable function at
y
which further requires that
for any sequence
(P_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathsf{P}^{\neq y}
with pairwise distinct
y_{P_{n}}
and every clopen set
V\ni y

there exists a sequence
(Q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathsf{P}^{\neq y}
with pairwise distinct
y_{Q_{n}}
in
V
such that
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P_{n}}\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{Q_{n}}
.
This would ensure that
f\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}
for any clopen neighborhood of
y
.
While this may seem desirable and a result analogous to
Theorem

6.12
can be established,
it seems to introduce a complexity which did not appear necessary for our proof.

We now want to show the following statement for all
\alpha<\omega_{1}
:

\text{S}(\alpha)

\mathrm{FG}(\leq\alpha)
implies that for every
f\in\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha+1}
if
f
solvable with
\mathsf{P}
, then there exists
g\in\mathsf{G}_{\alpha+1}
with
f\leq g
and
g\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
for every clopen
U\supseteq Y_{\mathsf{P}}
.

In
Theorem

6.16
, we prove that this is enough to obtain
\mathrm{FG}(\alpha+2)
from
\mathrm{FG}(<\alpha+2)
, thus completing the inductive proof of
Theorem

5.14
.

This latter proof notably relies on a combined application of
Propositions

6.5
and
6.12

to functions in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha+2}
(for
\alpha=\lambda+n
with
\lambda
limit or null) to first get a fine
c
-partition, which is then partitioned to get solvable functions. This process may yield some solvable functions in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda+1}
, and
while we have established
\mathrm{FG}(\lambda+1)
this does not directly imply
\text{S}(\lambda)
15
15

15

Nor does
\text{S}(\lambda)
directly imply
\mathrm{FG}(\lambda+1)
,
which makes
Theorem

5.11
still necessary.
.
So we now prove
\text{S}(\lambda)
for
\lambda
limit or null, before showing
\text{S}(\alpha)
in the successor case.

Proposition 6.14
.

Let
\lambda<\omega_{1}
be limit or null and assume
\mathrm{FG}(\leq\lambda)
. Suppose that
f:A\to B
in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda+1}
is solvable with
\mathsf{P}
.

Then there exists a finite gluing
g
of functions in
\mathsf{G}_{\lambda+1}
such that
f\leq g
and
g\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
for every clopen
U\supseteq Y_{\mathsf{P}}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{P}
be a fine
c
-partition of
f
and
y\in Y_{\mathsf{P}}
witnessing that
f
is solvable and let
U\supseteq Y_{\mathsf{P}}
be clopen in
B
.
We first deal with the case
\lambda=0
.
As
f
is in this case locally constant, we know that
f\equiv|\im f|\mathsf{k}_{1}
from
Proposition

2.35
.
Since
f
is solvable with
\mathsf{P}
, by centeredness
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}
is constant for all
P\in\mathsf{P}
, and so
\im f=Y_{\mathsf{P}}\subseteq U
. So
f=f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}

and the result follows
16
16

16

Note that since
f
is solvable, it actually follows that
\im f
is either infinite or equal to
\{y\}
,
hence
f\equiv\omega\mathsf{k}_{1}
or
f\equiv\mathsf{k}_{1}
.
.

Now assume that
\lambda
is limit, recall that
\mathrm{FG}(\leq\lambda)
ensures that
\mathrm{FG}(\lambda+1)
holds by
Proposition

5.15
, and using
that
\mathsf{P}
is fine with
Corollary

4.11
,
for every
P\in\mathsf{P}
we have
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
or
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\equiv\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
.

First case:
y_{P}=y
for all
P\in\mathsf{P}
, so
f
is simple by
Proposition

4.12
and so
f\leq\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
by
Proposition

3.12
.
If
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\equiv\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
for some
P\in\mathsf{P}
, then
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq(f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P})\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}
for every clopen neighbourhood
V
of
y
by centeredness of
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}
. Otherwise
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
for all
P\in\mathsf{P}
. If
\CB(f^{(y,n)})<\lambda
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then
f\leq\pgl_{n}f^{(y,n)}\leq\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
by
Corollaries

3.8
and
3.20
and so we are done in this case too. Finally, assume that for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\CB(f^{(y,n)})=\lambda
and so
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq f^{(y,n)}
by the General Structure
Theorem

3.17
. As
\mathsf{P}
is a fine partition,
f_{y,\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}}=f
is not a lump and so we can find a large enough
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq f^{(y,N)}
and
B^{(y,N)}\subseteq U
. Therefore for
V=U\setminus B^{(y,N)}
we have
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\oplus\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}\oplus f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{B^{(y,N)}}\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
, as desired
17
17

17

Such a function
f\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\oplus\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
can for example be constructed using the infinitary wedge (cf.
Remark

5.2
) where each column consists of a sequence of functions
(f_{i,j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
whose
\CB
-rank is cofinal in
\lambda
. While
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\oplus\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
does not admit a fine
c
-partition, the function
f
clearly does.
.

Second case: there exists
P\in\mathsf{P}
with
y_{P}\neq y
. This means that
Y_{\mathsf{P}}
is actually infinite since
f
is solvable.
We partition
\mathsf{P}=\mathsf{P}_{0}\sqcup\mathsf{P}_{1}
with
\mathsf{P}_{1}=\{P\in\mathsf{P}\mid f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\equiv\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\}
and set
Y_{i}=\{y_{P}\mid P\in\mathsf{P}_{i}\}
,
i=0,1
.
If
Y_{1}
is empty then
Y=Y_{0}
is infinite and
f\leq\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
by
Corollary

2.26
. Since
Y_{0}\subseteq U
, we get
\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
by
Lemma

5.9
, so we are done. If
Y_{1}
is infinite, then
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda+1}=\omega\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq f
by
Corollary

2.26
and
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda+1}\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
by
Lemma

5.9
, as desired.

Suppose now that
Y_{1}
is finite and nonempty. Then we claim that
Y_{1}=\{y\}
.

Towards a contradiction, assume that
y_{P}\neq y
for some
P\in\mathsf{P}_{1}
. Then
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\equiv\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
, but since
Y_{1}
is finite we can find a small enough clopen neighbourhood
V
of
y
such that
V\cap Y_{1}=\emptyset
. But as
f
is solvable, there exists
Q\in\mathsf{P}^{\neq y}
with
y_{Q}\in V
and
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{Q}
. Since necessarily
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{Q}\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
, we get
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
, in contradiction with
Corollary

4.11
.
So we have
Y_{1}=\{y\}
and
Y_{0}
is infinite. We distinguish two subcases.

(i)

Suppose that for every clopen
V\ni y
the set
Y_{0}\setminus V
is finite. So
Y_{1}
converges to
y
and therefore
Lemma

5.10
allows to conclude.

(ii)

Otherwise,
Y_{0}\cap W
is infinite for some clopen
W\notowner y
and set
V=U\setminus W
. Let
f_{i}=\bigsqcup_{P\in\mathsf{P}_{i}}f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}
for
i=0,1
. By the first case, we have
f_{1}\leq\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq f_{1}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}
and note that
f_{1}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}
. By
Corollaries

2.26
and
5.9
, we get
f_{0}\leq\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{W}
. Therefore

f=f_{1}\sqcup f_{0}\leq\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\oplus\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}\oplus f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{W}\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U},

as desired.∎

Theorem 6.15
.

Assume
\mathrm{FG}(\leq\alpha+1)
for
\alpha<\omega_{1}
. Let
f:A\to B
in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha+2}
be solvable with
\mathsf{P}
.
Then there exists
g\in\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(\mathsf{G}_{\alpha+2})
such that
f\leq g
and
g\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
, so in particular
f\equiv g\equiv f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
, for every clopen
U\supseteq Y_{\mathsf{P}}
.

Proof.

Let
y\in Y_{\mathsf{P}}
witness that
f
is solvable with
\mathsf{P}
and suppose that
U\supseteq Y_{\mathsf{P}}
is clopen in
B
.

By
Proposition

5.15
, there exists a finite set
G\subseteq\mathsf{C}_{\alpha+2}
of representatives for
\{f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\mid P\in\mathsf{P}^{\neq y}\}
.
Let us say that
g\in G
is
reducible infinitely often off

y
if there exists a clopen neighborhood
V
of
y
in
B

such that the set
V_{g}=\{y_{P}\mid P\in\mathsf{P}\text{ and }y_{P}\notin V\text{ and }g\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\}
is infinite. We define

H=\{g\in G\mid\text{$g$ is reducible infinitely often off $y$}\}

Note that
H
is a
\leq
-downward closed subset of
G
and set
D=G\setminus H
.
We further define
\mathsf{P}_{H}=\{P\in\mathsf{P}^{\neq y}\mid\exists h\in H\,f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\equiv h\}
and
\mathsf{P}_{M}=\mathsf{P}\setminus\mathsf{P}_{H}
.

We claim that the function
f_{M}=\bigsqcup_{P\in\mathsf{P}_{M}}f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}
together with
\mathsf{P}_{M}
is strongly solvable at
y
.
To this end, suppose that
W
is a clopen neighborhood of
y
.
To check the first condition, suppose towards a contradiction that the set
\{y_{P}\mid P\in\mathsf{P}_{M}\text{ and }y_{P}\notin W\}
is infinite.
Since
D
is finite, the pigeonhole principle ensures that some
g\in D
reduces infinitely often off
y
, a contradiction with the definition of
D
.
For the second condition, let
P\in\mathsf{P}_{M}
with
y_{P}\neq y
.
As
f
is solvable with
\mathsf{P}
there exists
Q\in\mathsf{P}^{\neq y}
such that
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{Q}
.
Note that if
Q\in\mathsf{P}_{H}
, then so is
P
since
H
is
\leq
-downward closed. Since
P\in\mathsf{P}_{M}
, it follows that
Q\in\mathsf{P}_{M}
too, as desired.

Therefore
f_{M}
is strongly solvable at
x
and by
Theorem

6.10
there exists
g_{M}\in\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(\mathsf{G}_{\alpha+2})
such that

f_{M}\leq g_{M}\leq f_{M}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V}.

for every clopen neighborhood
V
of
y
in
B
.

Finally, note that since
H
is finite we can choose a single clopen neighborhood
V
of
y
in
B
such that the sets
V_{g}
are infinite for all
g\in H
.
It follows from
Lemma

5.9
that
\omega H\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U\setminus V}
for all clopen set
U\supseteq Y_{\mathsf{P}}
.
Hence for
f_{H}=\bigsqcup_{P\in\mathsf{P}_{H}}f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{P}
, the following holds by
Proposition

2.25
:

f_{H}\leq\omega H\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U\setminus V}.

Altogether, for every clopen set
B\supseteq Y_{\mathsf{P}}
we have as desired:

f\leq f_{H}\oplus f_{M}\leq\omega H\oplus g_{M}\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U\setminus V}\oplus f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{V\cap U}\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}.\qed

Theorem 6.16
.

For all
\alpha<\omega_{1}
, if
\mathrm{FG}(<\alpha+2)
holds then so does
\mathrm{FG}(\leq\alpha+2)
.

Proof.

Assume
\mathrm{FG}(<\alpha+2)
and let
f:A\to B
in
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha+2}
, we prove that
f
is equivalent to some function in
\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(\mathsf{G}_{\alpha+2})
.
By
Proposition

6.5
we can take a fine
c
-partition of
f
, and by
Theorem

6.12
there is a countable family
\mathcal{U}

of pairwise disjoint clopen subsets of
B
whose union covers
Y_{\mathsf{P}}
and such that
f_{U}=f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{U}_{\mathsf{P}}}
is solvable for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
.
Now by
Propositions

6.14
and
6.15
, for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
there exists
g_{U}\in\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(\mathsf{G}_{\alpha+2})

such that
f_{U}\leq g_{U}
and
g_{U}\leq f_{U}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
, so in particular
f_{U}\equiv g_{U}\equiv f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}
.
Putting everything together, by
Corollaries

2.26
and
2.28
we have

f=\bigsqcup_{U\in\mathcal{U}}f_{U}\leq\bigoplus_{U\in\mathcal{U}}f_{U}\leq\bigoplus_{U\in\mathcal{U}}g_{U}\leq\bigoplus_{U\in\mathcal{U}}{f_{U}}\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}\leq\bigoplus_{U\in\mathcal{U}}f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{U}\leq f.

So
f\equiv\bigoplus_{U\in\mathcal{U}}g_{U}
, which is as desired if
\mathcal{U}
is finite. If
\mathcal{U}
is infinite, we conclude using
Proposition

5.13

(vi)
.
∎

We can finally conclude the proofs of all our main results:

Proof of

Main Theorems

1
,
2
,
3
,
5.14
,
1.5
,
1.2
,
1.4
and
1.2
.

By
Proposition

5.15
, we have already established
\mathrm{FG}(0)
,
\mathrm{FG}(\lambda)
and
\mathrm{FG}(<\lambda+1)
implies
\mathrm{FG}(\lambda+1)
, for every
\lambda
limit.

Theorem

6.16
therefore completes the proof by induction of
Theorem

5.14
.
This in particular yields
Theorem

1.5
, which in turn implies
Theorems

1.2
and
1.4
by
Proposition

3.19
.
This therefore concludes the proof of
Main Theorems

1
,
2
and
3
, since any
bqo
is
wqo
.
∎

7
Concluding remarks and Open questions

7.1
Comments, questions and optimality

Here are a few interesting corollaries of the whole analysis.

Theorem 7.1
.

Every function in
\mathsf{Scat}
is locally centered.

Proof.

By
Theorems

1.5
,
2.33
and
4.8
.
∎

As a byproduct, we obtained a “normal form” theorem for functions in
\mathsf{Scat}
.

Theorem 7.2
.

Every function in
\mathsf{Scat}
is continuously equivalent to a finite-to-one function whose domain and range are countable Polish spaces.

Proof.

Using
Fact

2.24
, observe first that having countable Polish domain and range and being finite-to-one are all properties preserved by finite gluing,
so by the Precise Structure
Theorem

5.14
, it is enough to prove the result for generators.
By
Facts

2.24
,
3.2
and
5.3
, all three operations – gluing, pointed gluing and wedge – preserve countability and Polishness of both domain and range,
so by induction all generators – built using only these operations – have countable Polish domain and range.

For finite-to-oneness, note that by
Facts

2.24
and
3.2
again the gluing and pointed gluing operations preserve injectivity, so by induction we have:
for all
\alpha<\omega_{1}
the functions
\mathsf{l}_{\alpha},\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\alpha},\mathsf{k}_{\alpha+1}
, and
all functions in
\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}
are injective. The only operation which produces non-injective functions is the wedge, but by
Fact

5.3
it produces finite-to-one generators, thus concluding the proof.
∎

It is now the right moment to open a line of questions regarding the optimality of our general strategy:
was proving the Precise Structure
Theorem

5.14
really necessary to get all its consequences?
For the first instance of this general question, observe that

Theorems

7.1
and
7.2
are statements that follow from the Precise Structure
Theorem

5.14
, but they do not concern
a priori
the generators.
This suggests the following angle:

Question 1
.

Is there a proof of
Theorems

7.1
and
7.2
that does not use the Precise Structure
Theorem

5.14
,
or
Theorem

1.5
, or even
Theorem

1.4
?

The second instance appears already in the introduction:
Theorem

1.5
implies
Theorem

1.4
by
Proposition

2.33
,
but they could still be somehow of the same strength.

Question 2
.

Can we deduce
Theorem

1.5
from
Theorem

1.4
?

Even within our global strategy, one can wonder if all these operations and generators are really needed.
In that direction, the strongest sign is that – up to continuous equivalence – a generating set for
\mathsf{Scat}
cannot omit the generators we identified.

Write
\mathsf{G}=\bigcup_{\alpha<\omega_{1}}\mathsf{G}_{\alpha}
and
\mathsf{C}=\bigcup_{\alpha<\omega_{1}}\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}
.
We already have all we need to show that elements of
\mathsf{C}
and
\omega\{\mathsf{C}\}
cannot be omitted,
but the situation for wedge generators is a bit trickier and needs more preparation.

Lemma 7.3
.

Any wedge generator
f
admits a
reduced form
defined as follows:

f\equiv\textstyle{\bigvee}(f_{0},\ldots,f_{k}\mid F_{k+1})
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
f_{i}\in\mathsf{Scat}
for
i\leq k
and
F_{k+1}\subseteq\mathsf{C}
satisfying:

(i)

(f_{i})_{i\leq k}
is an antichain with respect to continuous reduction.

(ii)

For all
i\leq k
and for all
h\in F_{k+1}
we have
h\not\leq f_{i}
, and
F_{k+1}
is an antichain.

(iii)

Either
f\equiv\omega F_{k+1}
, or for all
i\leq k
we have
\pgl f_{i}\not\leq F_{k+1}
.

Proof.

We start with any
f=\textstyle{\bigvee}(f_{0},\ldots,f_{k}\mid F_{k+1})
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
f_{i}\in\mathsf{Scat}
for
i\leq k
and
F_{k+1}\subseteq\mathsf{C}
,
and we give three finite procedures to get a reduced form for
f
.

First, we can pick an
\leq
-antichain of
\leq
-maximal elements in
\{f_{0},\ldots,f_{k}\}

and still obtain by
Corollary

5.5
a function equivalent to the original one, getting the first item.

For the second procedure:
suppose that some
h\in F_{k+1}
is reduced by
f_{i}
for some
i\leq k
(resp. by some
h^{\prime}\in F_{k+1}
).
By definition of the wedge operation there is
n<|F_{k+1}|
such that
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
we have
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{N_{(k+1+m,n)}}\equiv h
. We want to apply
Proposition

5.4
on a ‘subfunction’ of
f
, so we need to produce
relevant sets
A_{m}
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Define
A_{m}=N_{(m)}
if
i\neq m\leq k
(resp. for all
i\leq k
),
A_{i}=N_{(i)}\cup\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{N}}N_{(k+1+m,n)}
,
and
A_{k+1+m}=N_{(k+1+m)}\setminus N_{(k+1+m,n)}
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
h\leq f_{i}
the partition
(A_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\dom(f)
along with the point
(0)^{\infty}

satisfy the hypotheses of
Proposition

5.4
, which gives
f\leq\textstyle{\bigvee}(f_{0},\ldots,f_{k}\mid F_{k+1}\setminus\{h\})\leq f
.
Iterating this second procedure we get the second item.

For the third item, observe first that
\omega F_{k+1}\leq f
by
Proposition

2.27
, and consider the set
J=\{i\leq k\mid\pgl f_{i}\leq F_{k+1}\}
.
If
J=k+1
then
f\leq\omega F_{k+1}
by
Proposition

2.25
, and otherwise
f\leq\textstyle{\bigvee}((f_{j})_{j\in k+1\setminus J}\mid F_{k+1})\leq f
, concluding the proof.
∎

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
f=\textstyle{\bigvee}(f_{0},\ldots,f_{k}\mid F_{k+1})
be a wedge generator in reduced form and call
F=\{f_{0},\ldots,f_{k}\}\cup F_{k+1}
.
Suppose that
(A,A^{\prime})
is a clopen partition of
\dom(f)
and
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{\prime}}\leq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(F)
.
Then either
f\equiv\omega F_{k+1}
, or
f\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A}
.

Proof.

We show that either
f\equiv\omega F_{k+1}
, or
A
contains
(i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{\infty}
for all
i\leq k
and infinitely many centers for every function in
F_{k+1}
.
Since
A
is a clopen set of
\dom f
the latter allows us to use
Proposition

5.4
inside
A
and conclude.

Suppose now that
x=(i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{\infty}\in A^{\prime}
for some
i\leq k
. Since
x
is a center for
\pgl f_{i}
by
Facts

4.2
and
4.3
we have
\pgl f_{i}\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{\prime}}\leq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(F)
.
Since
\pgl f_{i}
is centered, by
Fact

4.3
we have
\pgl f_{i}\leq F
. But
\CB(\pgl f_{i})=\CB(f_{i})+1
by
Fact

3.2
since
f_{i}\in\mathsf{Scat}
, so
\pgl f_{i}\not\leq f_{i}
.
Therefore
\pgl f_{i}\leq F\setminus\{f_{i}\}
, and since
f
is in reduced form we cannot have
f_{i}\leq f_{j}
, so even less
\pgl f_{i}\leq f_{j}
, for some
j\neq i\leq k
.
We must have then
\pgl f_{i}\leq F_{k+1}
, so
Lemma

7.3
again tells us that
f\equiv\omega F_{k+1}
.

We can thus suppose that
(i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{\infty}\in A
for all
i\leq k
. If
F_{k+1}=\emptyset
we are done, so suppose it is not, and
some function
h\in F_{k+1}
has only finitely many centers in
A
. Then
h
has infinitely many centers in
A^{\prime}
by the pigeonhole
principle, so by centeredness and
Proposition

2.27
we get
\omega h\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A^{\prime}}\leq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(F)
, and by
Lemma

5.9
we obtain
\omega h\leq F
.
But since
h
is centered, we cannot have
\omega h\leq h
by
Proposition

4.5
and in turn
\omega h\leq F\setminus\{h\}
, which contradicts our assumption of normal form.
∎

We are ready to show the optimality of our generating set.

Theorem 7.5
.

Assume that
G\subseteq\mathsf{Scat}
is generating
\mathsf{Scat}
, that is, any function in
\mathsf{Scat}
is continuously equivalent to one in
\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(G)
.
Then
G
contains
\mathsf{G}
up to continuous equivalence, that is for all
f\in\mathsf{G}
there is
g\in G
such that
f\equiv g
.

Proof.

Take
f\in\mathsf{G}
and
g\in\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(G)
such that
f\equiv g
. Write
g=\bigoplus_{i\in I}g_{i}
for some finite
I\subseteq\mathbb{N}
and
g_{i}\in G
for all
i\in I
.
Since
g\leq f
we have
g_{i}\leq f
for all
i\leq n
, it is enough to find
i\in I
such that
f\leq g_{i}
.
We prove this by induction on the definition of generators.

If
f=\mathsf{k}_{1}
,
f=\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
or more generally if
f\in\mathsf{C}
, then
f\leq\bigoplus_{i}{g_{i}}
implies
f\leq g_{i}
for some
i\in I
by
Fact

4.3
.
If
f=\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
for
\lambda
limit, then necessarily
\CB(g_{i})=\lambda
for some
i\in I
by
Corollary

2.16
and so
g_{i}\equiv f
by the General Structure
Theorem

3.17
.
If
f\in\omega\{\mathsf{C}\}
, then similarly
f\leq g_{i}
for some
i\in I
by
Lemma

5.9
.

Suppose now that
f=\textstyle{\bigvee}(f_{0},\ldots,f_{k}\mid F_{k+1})
is a wedge generator in reduced form and call
F=\{f_{0},\ldots,f_{k}\}\cup F_{k+1}
.
Note that if
B
is any clopen set of
\im f
that does not contain
(0)^{\infty}
then by definition of the wedge operation we have
f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{B}\leq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(F)
.

Since
g\leq f
by
Proposition

2.27
there is a relative clopen partition
(B_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\im f
such that
g_{i}\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{B_{i}}
.
If
(0)^{\infty}\notin B=\bigcup_{i\in I}B_{i}
then
f\leq g\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{B}\leq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(F)
, so by
Lemma

7.4
applied to
(\emptyset,\dom(f))

we obtain
f\equiv\omega F_{k+1}
and we are done.

Suppose now that
(0)^{\infty}\in B
. There is a unique
n\in I
such that
(0)^{\infty}\in B_{n}
, and for some clopen partition
(C,C^{\prime})
of
\im f
we have
B_{n}\subseteq C
and
\bigcup_{i\neq n}B_{i}\subseteq C^{\prime}
. Note that
\bigoplus_{i\neq n}g_{i}\leq f\mathord{\downharpoonright}^{C^{\prime}}\leq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathsf{Fin}}(F)
.
Now apply
Lemma

7.4
to the clopen partition
(A,A^{\prime})=(f^{-1}(C),f^{-1}(C^{\prime}))
of
\dom f
.
If
f\equiv\omega F_{k+1}
once again we are done, otherwise
f\leq f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A}\leq g_{n}
so we are also done.
∎

Observe that if
Theorem

7.5
shows that one cannot omit our generators,
the sets
\mathsf{G}_{\alpha}
that we have identified are far from being minimal, for at least two reasons.
First because some pairs of distinct generators can be continuously equivalent, as evidenced (for instance) by the Reduced Form
Lemma

7.3
.
Second because some generators could be dispensable, besides for each
\alpha=\lambda+n
with
\lambda
limit or null and
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
our set
\mathsf{G}_{\alpha}
generates not only
\mathsf{Scat}_{\alpha}
but actually the whole (bigger) class
\mathsf{Scat}_{[\lambda,\alpha]}
.
This suggest the following question.

Question 3
.

Given
\alpha=\lambda+n
with
\lambda<\omega_{1}
limit and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, what is the smallest number of functions needed to finitely generate
\mathsf{Scat}_{[\lambda,\alpha]}
?

In case
\alpha=\lambda
is limit, the answer to
Question

3
is trivially 1, since in that case
\mathsf{G}_{\lambda}=\{\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\}
.
We now study
Question

3
at
\lambda+1
for
\lambda
limit or 1, proving first the optimality of
Theorem

5.11
.

Theorem 7.6
.

Let
\lambda<\omega_{1}
be limit or 1.
Up to continuous equivalence, the smallest subset that generates
\mathsf{Scat}_{[\lambda,\lambda+1]}
is given by

\{\mathsf{l}_{\lambda},\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1},\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda},\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1},\textstyle{\bigvee}(\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\mid\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}),\mathsf{l}_{\lambda+1}\}.

\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
\textstyle{\bigvee}(\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\mid\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1})
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda+1}

Figure 4:
Hasse diagram of our minimal set of generators of
\mathsf{Scat}_{[\lambda,\lambda+1]}
for
\lambda
limit or
1
.

Proof.

For
\lambda
limit or
1
, let
G
be the set from the statement.
By
Theorems

3.17
and
5.11

G
generates
\mathsf{Scat}_{[\lambda,\lambda+1]}
.
As all functions of
G
are in
\mathsf{G}
by
Theorem

7.5
it is enough to
show that no two of these functions are continuously equivalent.
By
Corollary

4.11
we know that
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}<\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
,
and by
Fact

4.3
we have
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\nleq\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
.
By considering their
\CB
-type, it follows from
Corollary

2.17
that
\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\nleq\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}

and
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\ngeq\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
so these two generators are incomparable. Now note that the following reduction holds

\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}<\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}<\Bigl\{\begin{array}[]{c}\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\\
\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\end{array}\Bigr\}\leq\textstyle{\bigvee}(\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\mid\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1})\leq\mathsf{l}_{\lambda+1}

and we show that
\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\ngeq\textstyle{\bigvee}(\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\mid\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1})
and

\textstyle{\bigvee}(\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\mid\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1})\ngeq\mathsf{l}_{\lambda+1}
.

Suppose that
(\sigma,\tau)
reduces
\textstyle{\bigvee}(\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\mid\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1})
to
\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
. In particular we would get

\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\equiv\textstyle{\bigvee}(\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\mid\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1})\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{N_{(0)}}\leq\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1},

and by
Fact

4.3
since
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
is centered we would have
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
, a contradiction.

Suppose that
(\sigma,\tau)
reduces
\mathsf{l}_{\lambda+1}
to
\textstyle{\bigvee}(\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\mid\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1})
, then by
Proposition

2.15
the function
\sigma
maps injectively the points of
\CB
-rank
\lambda
for the former function into those of the latter function. Therefore there exists
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
j\neq 0
such that
\sigma((i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{\infty})=(j)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{\infty}
, so by centeredness of
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
we would get
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
, a contradiction again.
∎

We obtain as a corollary the answer to
Question

3
in case
\alpha=\lambda+1
.

Corollary 7.7
.

The smallest sets generating
\mathsf{Scat}_{[\lambda,\lambda+1]}
are all order-isomorphic, for
\lambda<\omega_{1}
limit and for
\lambda=1
.

In particular they all have the same size of 6 generators.

More generally the partial orders induced by the quasi-ordered classes
(\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda+1},\leq)
are all isomorphic, for
\lambda
limit or 1.

Question 4
.

Given
\lambda<\omega_{1}
limit or
1
, are the partial orders induced by the classes
(\mathsf{Scat}_{\lambda+n+1},\leq)
isomorphic for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
?

We can now give long-awaited examples.

Proposition 7.8
.

In
\mathsf{Scat}
some functions are not equivalent to any injective function, there are injective functions not equivalent to any embedding, and there are
\leq
-antichains of any finite size.

More specifically, for
\lambda<\omega_{1}
limit or 1:

(i)

The injection
\textstyle{\bigvee}(\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\mid\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1})
is not continuously equivalent to an embedding.

(ii)

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
non null
(k\pgl\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\bigoplus(n-k)\pgl\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1})_{k\leq n}
is a
\leq
-antichain.

(iii)

The function
\textstyle{\bigvee}(\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda},\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\mid\emptyset)
is a simple and not continuously equivalent to the pointed gluing of its rays, nor to an injective function.

Proof sketch.

First item: suppose that
(\sigma,\tau)
is a reduction from
f:=\textstyle{\bigvee}(\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\mid\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1})
to an embedding
g\equiv f
.
Observe that
\CB_{\lambda+1}(f)=\{(i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{\infty}\mid i\in\mathbb{N}\}
so by
Proposition

2.15
we have
\{\sigma((i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{\infty})\mid i\in\mathbb{N}\}\subseteq\CB_{\lambda+1}(g)
.
If
\{\sigma((i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{\infty})\mid i\in\mathbb{N}\}
is not discrete, any limit point of it should be of CB-rank
\lambda+2
for the embedding
g
,
which is impossible as
\CB(g)=\lambda+1
.
So
\{\sigma((i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{\infty})\mid i\in\mathbb{N}\}
is discrete, and since
g
is an embedding the set
\{g\sigma((i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{\infty})\mid i\in\mathbb{N}\}
is also discrete in
\im g
.
Pick pairwise disjoint open sets
U_{i}\ni g\sigma((i)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{\infty})
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
and by centeredness and
Proposition

2.27
we obtain

\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\bigoplus\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\leq g\leq f\leq\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\bigoplus\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
.

Therefore if
f
is equivalent to an embedding, then it must be equivalent to
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\bigoplus\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
.
Now since
f\in\mathsf{G}
, by (the proof of)
Theorem

7.5
this implies that either
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\equiv f
or
\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\equiv f
,
and this is impossible by
Theorem

7.6
.

Second item: first show that
f:=\pgl\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
and
g:=\pgl\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
are incomparable.
Note that there are both of
\CB
-rank
\lambda+2
and
\CB_{\lambda+1}(f)=\CB_{\lambda+1}(g)=\{(0)^{\infty}\}
.
Suppose that
(\sigma,\tau)
reduces
g
to
f
then by
Proposition

2.15
we have
\sigma((0)^{\infty})=(0)^{\infty}
,
and as
f
is injective we furthermore have
\sigma^{-1}(\{(0)^{\infty}\})=\{(0)^{\infty}\}
and
\tau^{-1}(\{(0)^{\infty}\})=\{(0)^{\infty}\}
by
Proposition

2.4
.

Therefore by continuity
g^{((0)^{\infty},0)}\leq\bigsqcup_{i\leq k}f^{((0)^{\infty},i)}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. So we get
\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\leq k\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
, in contradiction with
Corollary

2.17
.
Similarly,
f\leq g
implies
\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}\leq\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
, contradicting
Theorem

7.6
.

Now suppose that
mf\bigoplus ng\leq pf\bigoplus qg
holds for
n,m,p,q\in\mathbb{N}
. By
Facts

4.3
and
2.17
, as
f
is centered and incomparable with
g
, each of the
m
copies of
f
must be reduced by distinct copies of
f
of the right hand side, so
m\leq p
. Similarly, we must have
n\leq q
.

Third point: observe that
f:=\textstyle{\bigvee}(\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda},\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\mid\emptyset)

to is simple of
\CB
-type
(\lambda+2,1)
.
If
f
was centered, any center would be of maximal
\CB
-rank (see proof of
Proposition

4.4
) and so one of
(0)^{\infty}
or
(1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{\infty}
would be a center for
f
. But none of these two points are center for
f
, for this would imply either
f\leq\pgl\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda}
or
f\leq\pgl\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}
, and since
f
reduces both of these functions, it would contradict the second point.

Now suppose that
(\sigma,\tau)
is a continuous reduction from
f

to an injective function
g\equiv f
.
There are now two possibilities: either
\sigma((0)^{\infty})\neq\sigma((1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{\infty})
or
\sigma((0)^{\infty})=\sigma((1)\mathord{{}^{\smallfrown}}(0)^{\infty})
.
In the former case we have
tp(g)=(\lambda+2,2)
because
g
is injective, so
g\nleq f
, a contradiction. In the latter case,
using
Facts

4.3
and
3.14
we would have

\pgl\{\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda},\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\}\leq g\equiv f\leq\pgl f^{((0)^{\infty},n)}\equiv\pgl\{\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda},\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\}
,
thus we would have
f\equiv\pgl\{\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda},\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\}
which is impossible by
Fact

4.3

since
\pgl\{\pgl\mathsf{l}_{\lambda},\omega\mathsf{k}_{\lambda+1}\}
is centered whereas
f
is not .
∎

Observe that in the previous proposition we build antichains of unbounded finite size using only
2
incomparable generators.
In the same line as
Question

3
, one can wonder if
\mathsf{Scat}_{n}
contains more and more generators as
n
increases. Recently in
[
Sca24
, Theorem 3.3.5]
Scamperti has built – using the gluing and pointed gluing operations on spaces –
sequences of cardinality
n
of closed countable Polish spaces of CB-rank
n
for all
n\geq 2
that form an antichain for topological embeddability.
The identity functions on these spaces are actually all among our generators, which implies that the number of generators in
\mathsf{Scat}_{n}
goes to infinity as
n\to\infty
.

7.2
Sharpness

In our
Main Theorems

1
,
2
and
3
there are various hypotheses on the complexity of: the domain, the co-domain, and the function itself.
Trying to see when one of these hypotheses in one these results can be relaxed leads to a large number of open questions.

Proposition

2.11
guarantees a decomposition of the domain of any scattered function,
in this respect it does make sense to conjecture that scattered functions (even discontinuous ones) may be constructed from simpler ones,
which might in turn allow for a (very general)
bqo
result, reminiscent of Fraïssé’s Conjecture for scattered linear orders.

Conjecture 1

(Functional Fraïssé’s Conjecture - FFC)
.

Continuous reducibility is
bqo
on the class of scattered functions.

Our
Theorem

1.4
proves the fragment of the FFC for continuous functions with zero-dimensional separable metrizable domain and metrizable range.
If the full FFC seems quite ambitious, getting it for functions on separable metrizable domains might be more accessible.
Our proof however makes essential use of continuity (for example in
Propositions

3.12
and
4.7
) and zero-dimensionality (for example in
Proposition

2.19
).

We now develop two directions for future research, sources for open questions motivated by some further results.
First, what happens for classes of definable functions on Polish spaces, and second what happens to the Perfect Function Property on larger classes of functions.

7.2.1
On functions with Polish domain

Here is the very first conjecture one can think of after
Main Theorem

1
.

Conjecture 2
.

Continuous reducibility is a
bqo
on Borel functions between Polish zero-dimensional spaces.

This was
[
Car13b
, Question 5.6]
, we upgrade it here to the status of conjecture, and we do the same for the next question.

[
Car13b
, Question 5.7]
gives indeed a possibility to attack the above
bqo
conjecture by splitting the problem in the study of injective functions on one side,
and those with countable image on the other, provided we first prove the following statement, interesting on its own.

Conjecture 3
.

Given any Borel function
f
between Polish (zero-dimensional) spaces, there is a Borel partition
(A,B)
of
\dom f
such that

f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A}
is continuously equivalent to an injection, and
f\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}
has countable image.

If we drop zero-dimensionality, the picture becomes quickly much more complex.
Schlicht has indeed proven
[
Sch18
, Theorem 1.7]
that the Wadge quasi-order on Borel subsets of a Polish space
X
is a
wqo
if and only if
X
is not zero-dimensional,
which by
Proposition

2.1
translates to continuous reducibility being non
wqo
in Borel functions from any non zero-dimensional Polish space.
This does not however ruin all hopes for continuous functions.

We can start by giving an upper bound for the descriptive complexity of the quasi-order of continuous reducibility on continuous functions with a compact Polish domain.
Given Polish spaces
X
and
Y
